Psychological Tests: Authors, Versions, Items and Subtests

Every psychological test the Indian clinical psychology entrances (RCI MA, NIMHANS, IHBAS, RML, JSS, CIP, Amity) keep asking about, one table per test: full name, who built it and when, the current edition and the ones before it, publisher, age range, number of items, every subtest, scale or index, how it is scored and normed, what it measures, its Indian adaptation, and the trap examiners set on it. Each family opens with a from-scratch explanation of how that kind of test works and closes with a comparison table. Where a count or a year genuinely differs between sources, the table says so instead of guessing: an honest "uncertain" is worth more in the hall than a confident wrong number.

In this book
  1. Intelligence tests (international)
  2. Performance tests and Indian intelligence tests
  3. Developmental, adaptive behaviour and social maturity scales
  4. Objective personality inventories
  5. Aptitude, interest, creativity and attitude measures
  6. Projective techniques
  7. Neuropsychological tests and batteries
  8. Clinical rating scales and symptom inventories
  9. Structured diagnostic interviews and the mental status examination
  10. Index of every test in this book

1. Intelligence tests (international)

An intelligence test is a standardised set of tasks, given in exactly the same way to everyone, whose scores are compared with a large reference group (the norm sample) of people of the same age. The test does not measure "intelligence" directly; it samples behaviour (defining words, copying block patterns, repeating digits) that theory and data say depends on general reasoning ability. The score tells you where a person stands relative to age-mates, which is why the same raw score means something very different at age 7 and at age 70.

The modern story starts in Paris. The French government wanted a way to identify children who would not profit from ordinary schooling, and Alfred Binet and Theodore Simon (1905) produced the first practical intelligence scale: 30 tasks arranged in rough order of difficulty. In the 1908 revision Binet and Simon grouped tasks by the age at which typical children pass them, which gave the idea of a mental age (MA): a child who passes the tasks typical of 8-year-olds has an MA of 8, whatever their actual age. A third revision followed in 1911.

Mental age alone is awkward, because being 2 years behind means far more at age 4 than at age 14. The German psychologist William Stern (1912) proposed dividing mental age by chronological age (CA), giving a "mental quotient". Lewis Terman (1916), in the Stanford revision of the Binet scale at Stanford University, multiplied this by 100 and called it the intelligence quotient: ratio IQ = (MA / CA) x 100. A 10-year-old with an MA of 12 has a ratio IQ of 120. The ratio IQ breaks down in adolescence and adulthood, because mental age stops growing in the late teens while chronological age keeps rising, so an ordinary 40-year-old would get an absurdly low "IQ".

David Wechsler (1939), working at Bellevue Hospital in New York, solved this with the deviation IQ. Instead of a ratio, the person's score is converted to a standard score that shows how far they sit from the mean of their own age group, on a scale with mean 100 and standard deviation (SD) 15. An IQ of 115 now means "1 SD above the age mean" (about the 84th percentile) at any age. Every major modern test uses deviation scores. Within Wechsler batteries, individual subtests are reported as scaled scores with mean 10 and SD 3. Theoretical frameworks evolved alongside: Charles Spearman's (1904) general factor g, Raymond Cattell's (1941, 1963) fluid (Gf) versus crystallised (Gc) intelligence, John Horn's extensions, and John Carroll's (1993) three-stratum model, which merged into the Cattell-Horn-Carroll (CHC) model that now organises the indices of the Wechsler, Stanford-Binet and KABC-II.

Definition

Ratio IQ = (mental age / chronological age) x 100 (Stern 1912 idea, Lewis Terman 1916 formula and name). Deviation IQ = a standard score locating the person relative to same-age peers, mean 100, SD 15 on Wechsler tests (Wechsler 1939). Scaled score = subtest standard score, mean 10, SD 3. T score = mean 50, SD 10. z score = mean 0, SD 1.

Exam trap

"Who coined IQ?" Stern proposed the quotient (MA/CA) in 1912; Terman added the x 100 and popularised the term "IQ" in the 1916 Stanford-Binet. Wechsler introduced the deviation IQ for adults. The 1960 Stanford-Binet Form L-M also switched to a deviation IQ, but with SD 16, not 15. Alfred Binet himself never used the IQ.

How to read age ranges in this book: Wechsler-style notation "16:0 to 90:11" means 16 years 0 months up to 90 years 11 months.

WAIS-IV: Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, Fourth Edition

The WAIS-IV is the standard individually administered adult intelligence test worldwide and the one most often named in Indian entrance papers. The examiner gives a series of subtests (verbal questions, block puzzles, digit repetition, symbol copying), converts each raw score to an age-scaled score, and combines these into four index scores and a Full Scale IQ. Interpretation moves from the global score (FSIQ) to the indices to individual subtests, looking for statistically significant strengths and weaknesses. The fourth edition is the one that dropped the old Verbal IQ and Performance IQ in favour of four indices.

FieldDetail
Full nameWechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, Fourth Edition
Author(s), yearDavid Wechsler (original Wechsler-Bellevue Intelligence Scale, 1939). WAIS-IV (2008) was developed by the publisher's research team after Wechsler's death (1981), still under his name.
VersionsWechsler-Bellevue Form I (1939), Wechsler-Bellevue Form II (1946), WAIS (1955), WAIS-R (1981), WAIS-III (1997), WAIS-IV (2008), WAIS-5 (2024, see next entry)
PublisherPearson (formerly The Psychological Corporation)
Age range16:0 to 90:11
ItemsItem counts are per subtest (they differ widely, and testing stops at discontinue rules); a single total item count is not a meaningful or standard figure. 15 subtests in all.
Subtests / scales / indices10 core subtests: Verbal Comprehension: Similarities, Vocabulary, Information. Perceptual Reasoning: Block Design, Matrix Reasoning, Visual Puzzles. Working Memory: Digit Span (forward, backward and the new sequencing task), Arithmetic. Processing Speed: Symbol Search, Coding.
5 supplemental subtests: Comprehension (VCI), Picture Completion and Figure Weights (PRI), Letter-Number Sequencing (WMI), Cancellation (PSI). Figure Weights, Letter-Number Sequencing and Cancellation are normed only for ages 16 to 69.
4 indices: Verbal Comprehension Index (VCI), Perceptual Reasoning Index (PRI), Working Memory Index (WMI), Processing Speed Index (PSI).
Full Scale IQ (FSIQ) from the 10 core subtests.
Optional composites: General Ability Index (GAI = VCI + PRI subtests, less affected by working memory and speed) and Cognitive Proficiency Index (CPI = WMI + PSI subtests).
New in WAIS-IV: Visual Puzzles, Figure Weights, Cancellation. Dropped from WAIS-III: Object Assembly and Picture Arrangement.
Scoring and normsFSIQ and indices mean 100, SD 15; subtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3. US standardisation sample 2,200. FSIQ 130 and above: very superior; 69 and below: extremely low (WAIS-IV classification labels).
MeasuresGeneral intellectual ability (g) and four broad abilities: verbal reasoning and knowledge, nonverbal and fluid reasoning, working memory, processing speed
Indian adaptationPearson has marketed an India edition with Indian norms (reported as WAIS-IV INDIA); year and sample details uncertain. In routine Indian practice, especially for certification, the older Indian performance scale WAPIS (Ramalingaswamy, 1974) and Indian norms of other tests are still widely used.
Exam trap

WAIS-IV has no VIQ or PIQ (those ended with WAIS-III). It has 4 indices, not 5 (5 primary indices belong to WISC-V and WAIS-5). The core-versus-supplemental split is 10 + 5 = 15. Comprehension and Picture Completion are supplemental in WAIS-IV, while Visual Puzzles is core. GAI is built from VCI and PRI; CPI from WMI and PSI: examiners swap these.

WAIS-5: Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, Fifth Edition

The WAIS-5 is the 2024 revision of the adult Wechsler. Its main structural change is to bring the adult test into line with WISC-V: the old Perceptual Reasoning Index is split into a Visual Spatial Index and a Fluid Reasoning Index, giving five primary indices. It was designed for both paper and digital (Q-interactive tablet) administration and shortened the subtests needed for an FSIQ. Because it is very new, Indian clinics and most Indian textbooks still teach WAIS-IV; treat WAIS-5 details below as current but verify against the manual.

FieldDetail
Full nameWechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, Fifth Edition
Author(s), yearDavid Wechsler (original scale 1939); WAIS-5 developed by Pearson's research team, published 2024
VersionsWAIS-5 (2024); previous: WAIS-IV (2008), WAIS-III (1997), WAIS-R (1981), WAIS (1955), Wechsler-Bellevue (1939)
PublisherPearson
Age range16:0 to 90:11
ItemsPer subtest; total subtest count reported as about 20 across primary, secondary and complementary groupings (exact count and grouping: uncertain, check the manual)
Subtests / scales / indices5 primary indices: Verbal Comprehension (VCI), Visual Spatial (VSI), Fluid Reasoning (FRI), Working Memory (WMI), Processing Speed (PSI).
New subtests (as reported): Running Digits, Set Relations, Spatial Addition (Spatial Addition previously appeared in the Wechsler Memory Scale, WMS-IV), and Digit Span is split into separately scored tasks such as Digit Sequencing (details uncertain).
Retained subtests include Similarities, Vocabulary, Information, Block Design, Visual Puzzles, Matrix Reasoning, Figure Weights, Arithmetic, Coding, Symbol Search. Which older subtests (for example Comprehension, Picture Completion, Cancellation, Letter-Number Sequencing) were dropped or moved: uncertain.
FSIQ is derived from a reduced set of about 7 subtests (exact composition uncertain). Ancillary indices such as GAI and CPI are available.
Scoring and normsFSIQ and indices mean 100, SD 15; subtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3. US norms.
MeasuresGeneral intelligence and five CHC-aligned broad abilities
Indian adaptationNone standard (as of this writing)
Exam trap

The key conceptual change from WAIS-IV to WAIS-5 is PRI split into VSI + FRI, mirroring the WISC-V change of 2014. If a question asks which adult Wechsler has a Perceptual Reasoning Index, the answer is WAIS-IV (and WISC-IV), not WAIS-5. Note the naming: WAIS-5 uses an Arabic numeral, WAIS-IV a Roman one.

WISC-V: Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children, Fifth Edition

The WISC-V is the children's version of the Wechsler, used for school-age assessment of intellectual disability, giftedness and specific learning disorder. It was the first Wechsler to split perceptual reasoning into separate visual-spatial and fluid reasoning indices, producing five primary indices. Subtests are organised in three tiers (primary, secondary, complementary), and the FSIQ comes from just 7 primary subtests, which shortens testing.

FieldDetail
Full nameWechsler Intelligence Scale for Children, Fifth Edition
Author(s), yearDavid Wechsler (WISC, 1949); WISC-V published 2014 (US), developed by Pearson's research team
VersionsWISC-V (2014); WISC-IV (2003); WISC-III (1991); WISC-R (1974); WISC (1949)
PublisherPearson
Age range6:0 to 16:11
ItemsPer subtest. 21 subtests in all: 10 primary, 6 secondary, 5 complementary (the 16 primary plus secondary subtests are the ones that yield the classic scores).
Subtests / scales / indices10 primary subtests: Similarities, Vocabulary (VCI); Block Design, Visual Puzzles (VSI); Matrix Reasoning, Figure Weights (FRI); Digit Span, Picture Span (WMI); Coding, Symbol Search (PSI).
6 secondary: Information, Comprehension, Picture Concepts, Arithmetic, Letter-Number Sequencing, Cancellation.
5 complementary: Naming Speed Literacy, Naming Speed Quantity, Immediate Symbol Translation, Delayed Symbol Translation, Recognition Symbol Translation.
FSIQ from 7 subtests: Similarities, Vocabulary, Block Design, Matrix Reasoning, Figure Weights, Digit Span, Coding.
5 primary indices: VCI, VSI, FRI, WMI, PSI.
Ancillary indices: Quantitative Reasoning, Auditory Working Memory, Nonverbal, General Ability (GAI), Cognitive Proficiency (CPI).
Complementary indices: Naming Speed, Symbol Translation, Storage and Retrieval.
Scoring and normsFSIQ and indices mean 100, SD 15; scaled scores mean 10, SD 3. US norm sample 2,200.
MeasuresGeneral intelligence and five CHC broad abilities; complementary subtests support learning-disorder evaluation
Indian adaptationWISC-IV INDIA (Pearson, Indian norms) is reported; year uncertain. A WISC-V India edition: uncertain. The classic Indian WISC adaptation is MISIC (Malin, 1969), covered in section 2.
Exam trap

WISC-IV (2003) had 4 indices (VCI, PRI, WMI, PSI) and 15 subtests (10 core + 5 supplemental), the same architecture as WAIS-IV. WISC-V has 5 primary indices, 10 primary subtests and FSIQ from 7. Picture Span (visual working memory) is new to WISC-V. Do not confuse "10 primary subtests" with "10 subtests for FSIQ".

WPPSI-IV: Wechsler Preschool and Primary Scale of Intelligence, Fourth Edition

The WPPSI is the Wechsler for preschoolers. Because a 3-year-old cannot do what a 6-year-old can, the WPPSI-IV uses two age bands with different batteries: the younger band gets fewer, simpler subtests and fewer indices. It uses child-friendly working-memory tasks (remembering pictures, placing animal cards in a zoo) and picture-based processing-speed tasks.

FieldDetail
Full nameWechsler Preschool and Primary Scale of Intelligence, Fourth Edition
Author(s), yearDavid Wechsler (WPPSI, 1967); WPPSI-IV published 2012
VersionsWPPSI-IV (2012); WPPSI-III (2002); WPPSI-R (1989); WPPSI (1967, original ages about 4 to 6:6)
PublisherPearson
Age range2:6 to 7:7, in two bands: 2:6 to 3:11 and 4:0 to 7:7
ItemsPer subtest; 15 subtests in total
Subtests / scales / indicesBand 2:6 to 3:11: primary indices VCI, VSI, WMI (3) plus FSIQ. Subtests include Receptive Vocabulary, Information, Picture Naming, Block Design, Object Assembly, Picture Memory, Zoo Locations.
Band 4:0 to 7:7: primary indices VCI, VSI, FRI, WMI, PSI (5) plus FSIQ. Adds Matrix Reasoning, Picture Concepts (FRI), Bug Search, Cancellation, Animal Coding (PSI), Similarities, Vocabulary, Comprehension.
New in WPPSI-IV: Picture Memory and Zoo Locations (working memory), Bug Search, Animal Coding, Cancellation (processing speed).
Scoring and normsFSIQ and indices mean 100, SD 15; scaled scores mean 10, SD 3
MeasuresEarly cognitive ability, school readiness, early identification of developmental delay and giftedness
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Age boundaries: WPPSI-IV ends at 7:7 and WISC-V begins at 6:0, so ages 6:0 to 7:7 overlap and the examiner chooses. WAIS-IV begins at 16:0 and WISC-V ends at 16:11, another overlap. The youngest WPPSI-IV band has only 3 primary indices.

SB5: Stanford-Binet Intelligence Scales, Fifth Edition

The Stanford-Binet is the direct descendant of the 1905 Binet-Simon scale and the test that gave the world the IQ. The fifth edition measures five CHC-type factors, each through one verbal and one nonverbal subtest, which allows a Verbal IQ and a Nonverbal IQ as well as an FSIQ. Testing starts with two routing subtests that estimate the person's level so that later subtests begin at the right difficulty. It is valued for its very wide age range and good floor and ceiling, useful in assessing both intellectual disability and high giftedness.

FieldDetail
Full nameStanford-Binet Intelligence Scales, Fifth Edition
Author(s), yearAlfred Binet and Theodore Simon (1905, Binet-Simon scale; revisions 1908, 1911). Lewis M. Terman (1916, Stanford revision). Lewis Terman and Maud Merrill (1937, Forms L and M; 1960 combined Form L-M). Robert L. Thorndike, Elizabeth Hagen and Jerome Sattler (1986, SB4). Gale H. Roid (2003, SB5).
VersionsSB5 (2003); SB4 (1986); Form L-M renormed (1972); Form L-M (1960, deviation IQ introduced, SD 16); Forms L and M (1937); Stanford revision (1916); Binet-Simon (1905, 1908, 1911). Early SB5 (2003) for young children is a related product.
PublisherRiverside Publishing (later Houghton Mifflin Harcourt, now Riverside Insights); later distribution details uncertain. SB5 is now also distributed by PRO-ED (uncertain).
Age range2 to 85+ years
ItemsItems are organised in levels within subtests; total item count uncertain. The 1905 Binet-Simon had 30 tasks.
Subtests / scales / indices5 factors: Fluid Reasoning, Knowledge, Quantitative Reasoning, Visual-Spatial Processing, Working Memory.
Each factor is tested in a nonverbal and a verbal domain, giving 10 subtests.
Routing subtests: Nonverbal Fluid Reasoning (Object Series/Matrices) and Verbal Knowledge (Vocabulary); together they give the Abbreviated Battery IQ (ABIQ).
Composites: Full Scale IQ, Nonverbal IQ, Verbal IQ, 5 factor index scores.
SB4 (1986) for comparison: 15 subtests in 4 areas (Verbal Reasoning, Abstract/Visual Reasoning, Quantitative Reasoning, Short-Term Memory).
Scoring and normsSB5 FSIQ, VIQ, NVIQ and factor indices mean 100, SD 15; subtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3. SB4 Standard Age Score mean 100, SD 16 (subtests mean 50, SD 8). 1916 and 1937: ratio IQ. SB5 norm sample about 4,800. Also gives change-sensitive scores.
MeasuresGeneral intelligence (g) plus five CHC broad abilities, verbal and nonverbal
Indian adaptationBinet-Kamat Test (V. V. Kamat, 1934; revised 1967), based on the 1916 Stanford-Binet; see section 2
Exam trap

SD 16 versus SD 15: the Stanford-Binet used SD 16 from Form L-M (1960) through SB4 (1986); SB5 switched to SD 15 to match Wechsler. SB5 author is Gale Roid (not Thorndike, who led SB4). SB5 has 5 factors and 10 subtests; SB4 had 4 areas and 15 subtests. The 1937 forms are named L and M after Lewis Terman and Maud Merrill.

Raven's Progressive Matrices (SPM, CPM, APM, SPM Plus, Raven's 2)

Raven's matrices are nonverbal reasoning tests: each item shows a pattern (a matrix of figures) with one piece missing, and the person chooses the missing piece from 6 or 8 options. Because no reading, speaking or culture-specific knowledge is needed, the test is used across languages and with people who have hearing or language problems. John Raven designed it to measure Charles Spearman's g, specifically eductive ability (making meaning out of confusion, seeing relations), as distinct from reproductive ability (recalling learned information), which he measured with the companion Mill Hill Vocabulary Scale. Items get "progressively" harder within each set, hence the name.

FieldDetail
Full nameRaven's Progressive Matrices: Standard (SPM), Coloured (CPM), Advanced (APM)
Author(s), yearJohn C. Raven (developed with Lionel Penrose; Penrose and Raven 1936 paper); SPM published 1938. Later revisions and manuals by John Raven (son), J. C. Raven and J. H. Court.
VersionsSPM (1938); CPM (1947); APM (1947, revised 1962); SPM Plus (1998, harder items to restore ceiling); Raven's 2 (2018, Pearson, digital and paper, item-bank based)
PublisherOriginally H. K. Lewis, London; later Oxford Psychologists Press; now Pearson
Age rangeSPM: about 6 years to adulthood (norms extend to old age). CPM: 5 to 11 years, elderly people and people with intellectual or physical disability. APM: adolescents and adults of above-average ability. Raven's 2: reported about 4 to 90 years (uncertain).
ItemsSPM: 60 items in 5 sets (A, B, C, D, E) of 12 each. CPM: 36 items in 3 sets (A, Ab, B) of 12 each, printed in colour to hold children's attention. APM: Set I 12 items (practice/screening) + Set II 36 items. Raven's 2: short computer-generated forms from an item bank (item count per form uncertain).
Subtests / scales / indicesNo subtests; a single total score. Sets are ordered by difficulty.
Scoring and normsRaw score (number correct) converted to percentiles and grades (Grade I "intellectually superior" at or above the 95th percentile, through Grade V "intellectually defective" at or below the 5th percentile); IQ-equivalent conversion possible. Untimed (APM can be timed). Score consistency check: the obtained set-wise pattern is compared with the expected composition for that total.
MeasuresSpearman's g, eductive (fluid) reasoning, nonverbal abstract reasoning
Indian adaptationIndian norms have been collected in several studies and appear in some Indian manuals; the authorship commonly cited (for example a Deshpande norm set) is uncertain. Widely used in Indian disability certification as a nonverbal measure.
Exam trap

Count the sets: SPM has 5 sets, A to E, 60 items; CPM has 3 sets, A, Ab, B, 36 items; APM has Set I (12) + Set II (36). Set Ab exists only in the CPM. CPM is "coloured" (for children and elderly), not "complex". Raven measures eductive ability; the Mill Hill Vocabulary Scale measures reproductive ability.

CFIT: Cattell Culture Fair Intelligence Test

Raymond Cattell built this test to measure fluid intelligence (Gf), the ability to reason with new material, while minimising the influence of language, schooling and culture. All items are figural (series, odd-one-out, matrices, topology). Cattell first called it "culture free", then admitted no test can be free of culture and renamed it "culture fair". It is timed and group-administrable.

FieldDetail
Full nameCulture Fair Intelligence Test (earlier Culture Free Intelligence Test)
Author(s), yearRaymond B. Cattell (Culture Free Test, 1940); Culture Fair Intelligence Test, Raymond B. Cattell and A. K. S. Cattell (1949; later editions 1950s and 1960s, exact years uncertain)
VersionsScale 1, Scale 2, Scale 3; Scales 2 and 3 each have parallel Forms A and B
PublisherInstitute for Personality and Ability Testing (IPAT), Champaign, Illinois
Age rangeScale 1: 4 to 8 years and adults with intellectual disability. Scale 2: 8 to 14 years and average adults. Scale 3: high school, college and superior adults.
ItemsScale 2 commonly reported as 46 items per form (Series 12, Classification 14, Matrices 12, Conditions 8); Scale 3 as 50 items per form (13, 14, 13, 10). Treat exact counts as uncertain. Scale 1 has 8 subtests, some of which require verbal instructions and are not truly culture fair.
Subtests / scales / indicesScales 2 and 3: 4 subtests: Series, Classification, Matrices, Conditions (Topology)
Scoring and normsDeviation IQ; the CFIT has traditionally been normed with SD 16 (sources differ; some tables give SD 24 for older scales). Timed subtests.
MeasuresFluid intelligence (Gf)
Indian adaptationIndian norms are reported in various studies; no single standard adaptation (uncertain)
Exam trap

Cattell's test measures fluid, not crystallised, intelligence. It was first called "Culture Free" (1940) and renamed "Culture Fair". The four Scale 2/3 subtests are Series, Classification, Matrices, Conditions: "Conditions" (topology) is the one examiners use as a distractor.

KABC-II: Kaufman Assessment Battery for Children, Second Edition

The KABC was built from neuropsychological theory rather than from older IQ tests. Its first edition used Alexander Luria's distinction between sequential processing (handling information in serial order, like digit recall) and simultaneous processing (integrating information all at once, like seeing a whole pattern), and it separated processing ability from acquired knowledge. The second edition offers a dual interpretive model: the examiner chooses Luria (excluding knowledge, fairer to children from deprived or bilingual backgrounds) or CHC (including knowledge) before testing.

FieldDetail
Full nameKaufman Assessment Battery for Children, Second Edition (Normative Update)
Author(s), yearAlan S. Kaufman and Nadeen L. Kaufman (KABC, 1983; KABC-II, 2004)
VersionsKABC-II NU (2018, normative update); KABC-II (2004); KABC (1983)
PublisherAmerican Guidance Service (AGS) originally; now Pearson
Age rangeKABC-II: 3 to 18 years. KABC (1983): 2:6 to 12:6.
Items18 subtests in KABC-II (commonly reported; not all given to every age); item totals uncertain
Subtests / scales / indicesScales (Luria name / CHC name): Sequential / Short-term Memory (Gsm); Simultaneous / Visual Processing (Gv); Learning / Long-term Storage and Retrieval (Glr); Planning / Fluid Reasoning (Gf); Knowledge / Crystallised Ability (Gc, CHC model only).
Global scores: Mental Processing Index (MPI, Luria model), Fluid-Crystallised Index (FCI, CHC model), Nonverbal Index (NVI).
KABC (1983): Sequential Processing, Simultaneous Processing, Mental Processing Composite, Achievement scale.
Scoring and normsGlobal and scale scores mean 100, SD 15; subtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3
MeasuresCognitive processing and intelligence in children; designed to reduce cultural and language bias
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

MPI (Luria model) excludes Knowledge; FCI (CHC model) includes it. The original KABC's theory was Alexander Luria's sequential versus simultaneous processing, which also underlies the Das-Naglieri PASS model. Do not confuse the Kaufmans' KABC with their adolescent and adult test, the KAIT (1993), or the brief KBIT.

Leiter-3: Leiter International Performance Scale, Third Edition

The Leiter is a fully nonverbal intelligence test: instructions are given by gesture and pantomime and the child responds by placing cards or foam pieces. It exists for people who cannot be tested verbally: deaf and hard-of-hearing children, children with autism or language disorder, and people who do not speak the examiner's language. Russell Leiter developed it in the late 1920s partly while working in Hawaii with a culturally diverse population.

VersionsLeiter-3 (2013); Leiter-R (1997); Arthur Adaptation (1950); Leiter scale (1929, 1936, 1948 revisions; years sources differ)

Attention and Memory battery: Attention Sustained, Forward Memory, Reverse Memory, Nonverbal Stroop.

Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

The Leiter is a completely nonverbal scale (no spoken instructions), unlike Raven's, which still uses brief verbal instructions. Leiter-R and Leiter-3 were both co-authored by Gale Roid, the SB5 author, so "Roid" can appear as the answer for two different tests.

CAS2: Das-Naglieri Cognitive Assessment System, Second Edition

The CAS operationalises the PASS theory of intelligence (Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive), which J. P. Das, an Indian-born psychologist from Odisha who worked at the University of Alberta, derived from Alexander Luria's three functional units of the brain: arousal and attention (unit 1, brainstem and reticular system), coding as simultaneous and successive processing (unit 2, posterior cortex), and planning (unit 3, frontal lobes). Instead of asking "how much intelligence", it asks "which cognitive processes are weak", which is useful in learning disability and ADHD assessment.

FieldDetail
Full nameDas-Naglieri Cognitive Assessment System (CAS); Cognitive Assessment System, Second Edition (CAS2)
Author(s), yearJack A. Naglieri and J. P. Das (1997); CAS2: Jack A. Naglieri, J. P. Das and Sam Goldstein (2014)
VersionsCAS2 (2014; also CAS2 Brief and Spanish editions); CAS (1997)
PublisherCAS: Riverside; CAS2: PRO-ED
Age range5:0 to 18:11
ItemsCAS: 12 subtests (Standard Battery), 8 subtests (Basic Battery); item totals uncertain
Subtests / scales / indices4 PASS scales (Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive), 3 subtests each in the Standard Battery; Full Scale. Example subtests: Matching Numbers and Planned Codes (Planning), Expressive Attention and Number Detection (Attention), Nonverbal Matrices (Simultaneous), Word Series (Successive).
Scoring and normsPASS scale and Full Scale scores mean 100, SD 15; subtests mean 10, SD 3
MeasuresPASS cognitive processes
Indian adaptationPASS-based research in India (Das's group, Odisha) exists; no standard Indian edition (uncertain)
Exam trap

PASS = Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive (not "Sequential", which is the KABC Luria label for the same idea). Theoretical source: Luria, not CHC.

Goodenough-Harris Drawing Test (Draw-a-Man)

The child is simply asked to draw a person ("make a picture of a man; make the very best picture you can"). Florence Goodenough noticed that children's drawings acquire details (neck, fingers, correct proportion, clothing) in a regular developmental order, so the number of details correctly included can be scored as an index of intellectual maturity. It is a quick, nonthreatening, nonverbal screening measure, not a projective test (projective human figure drawing, such as Karen Machover's 1949 Draw-a-Person, is a separate tradition).

FieldDetail
Full nameGoodenough-Harris Drawing Test (originally Goodenough Draw-a-Man Test)
Author(s), yearFlorence L. Goodenough (1926, Measurement of Intelligence by Drawings); revised by Dale B. Harris (1963). A later quantitative system: Jack Naglieri (1988, Draw a Person: A Quantitative Scoring System).
VersionsGoodenough-Harris (1963: Man, Woman and Self drawings); Goodenough Draw-a-Man (1926)
Publisher1963 edition: Harcourt, Brace and World (uncertain)
Age rangeAbout 3 to 15 years (Harris norms); most valid below about 10 to 12 years
ItemsGoodenough 1926: 51 scoring points. Harris 1963: Man scale 73 points, Woman scale 71 points; Self drawing not formally scored.
Subtests / scales / indicesMan scale, Woman scale (Harris); also a quality-scale method (comparing with 12 sample drawings)

Indian adaptationPramila Phatak (also spelt Pathak), M. S. University of Baroda: Indian norms for the Draw-a-Man test (year uncertain, 1960s). Commonly used in Indian ID assessment batteries.
Exam trap

51 points belongs to Goodenough (1926); 73 points (man) belongs to Harris (1963). Goodenough's Draw-a-Man is an intelligence test; Machover's Draw-a-Person and Buck's House-Tree-Person are projective tests. Do not mix the authors.

CMMS: Columbia Mental Maturity Scale

The CMMS is a picture-classification test: each card shows 3 to 5 drawings and the child points to the one that does not belong. Since it needs only a pointing response and very little verbal ability, it was originally developed to test children with cerebral palsy and other motor or speech impairments. It measures general reasoning ability through perceptual and conceptual classification.

FieldDetail
Full nameColumbia Mental Maturity Scale
Author(s), yearBessie B. Burgemeister, Lucille Hollander Blum and Irving Lorge (1954)
VersionsThird edition (1972); earlier editions 1954, 1959
PublisherPsychological Corporation / Harcourt (uncertain)
Age range3:6 to 9:11
Items1972 edition: 92 items arranged in 8 overlapping levels, so each child takes a subset (about 51 to 65 items)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle scale
Scoring and normsAge Deviation Score (mean 100, SD 16); percentiles, stanines, Maturity Index
MeasuresGeneral reasoning ability (classification)
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

The CMMS was built for children with cerebral palsy (motor disability), whereas the Leiter targets language and hearing problems. Its task is "which one does not belong" (classification), not matrix completion.

PPVT-5: Peabody Picture Vocabulary Test, Fifth Edition

The PPVT measures receptive vocabulary: the examiner says a word and the person points to the one of four pictures that matches it. No speaking or reading is needed, so it is quick (10 to 15 minutes) and usable with people who cannot speak. It was once marketed as a verbal intelligence screen; today it is treated as a measure of receptive vocabulary that correlates with verbal ability. Its companion, the Expressive Vocabulary Test (EVT), measures naming.

FieldDetail
Full namePeabody Picture Vocabulary Test, Fifth Edition
Author(s), yearLloyd M. Dunn (1959); PPVT-R and PPVT-III co-authored by Lloyd M. Dunn and Leota M. Dunn; PPVT-4 by Lloyd and Douglas Dunn; PPVT-5 by Douglas M. Dunn (2018)
VersionsPPVT-5 (2018); PPVT-4 (2007); PPVT-III (1997); PPVT-R (1981); PPVT (1959)

Scoring and normsStandard score mean 100, SD 15; percentiles, age equivalents
MeasuresReceptive (hearing) vocabulary, verbal ability screen
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

The PPVT is receptive vocabulary (point to picture), not expressive. The original 1959 test was by Lloyd Dunn; "Lloyd and Leota Dunn" are the authors from the 1981 revision onward. Do not confuse it with the Peabody Individual Achievement Test (PIAT).

2. Performance tests and Indian intelligence tests

A performance test is an intelligence test in which the person responds by doing something with materials (arranging blocks, fitting shapes into a board, sliding pieces, drawing) rather than by answering in words. Performance tests developed for three reasons. First, the US Army testing programme of World War I (the Army Alpha for literate recruits and the Army Beta for illiterate and non-English-speaking recruits, Robert Yerkes and colleagues, 1917) showed that verbal tests unfairly penalise people without schooling or language. Second, clinicians needed tests for deaf, speech-impaired and non-English-speaking children. Third, verbal and performance tasks seemed to tap partly different abilities: W. P. Alexander (1935) argued for a "practical" factor (F) beyond g, and Wechsler built both Verbal and Performance scales into his tests.

India needed performance tests for the same reasons, even more acutely: at independence most of the population was not literate, the country has dozens of languages, and verbal Western tests are culturally loaded. Indian psychologists therefore did two things: they adapted Western tests (Binet-Kamat from the Stanford-Binet; MISIC from the WISC; WAPIS from the WAIS; Indian norms for Raven's and the Draw-a-Man), and they assembled batteries of existing performance tests with Indian norms (the Bhatia Battery). Many of these instruments date from the 1930s to 1970s and use old norms, a limitation examiners like to ask about, yet they remain in daily use in Indian clinics and are named in the government guidelines for certifying intellectual disability.

High yield

Indian adaptation pairs to memorise: Binet-Kamat (V. V. Kamat) from Stanford-Binet; MISIC (A. J. Malin, Nagpur) from WISC; WAPIS (P. Ramalingaswamy) from WAIS performance scale; Malin's VSMS (A. J. Malin, Nagpur) from Doll's Vineland; DASII (Pramila Phatak, Baroda) from Bayley; Bhatia Battery (C. M. Bhatia) is an Indian battery of mostly Western performance tests.

Bhatia Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence

C. M. Bhatia assembled five tests, four performance tests and one memory test, and standardised them on Indian school children so that intelligence could be assessed without depending on literacy or a particular language. The tests are given individually with materials, and the scores are converted into an overall index. It remains one of the most-asked Indian tests in entrance MCQs, usually as "name the five subtests" or "who developed it".

FieldDetail
Full nameBhatia Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence (from Bhatia's monograph Performance Tests of Intelligence under Indian Conditions)
Author(s), yearC. M. Bhatia (1955)
Versions1955 original; no major revision widely documented (uncertain)
PublisherOxford University Press (1955 monograph); test materials from Indian suppliers (uncertain)
Age rangeOriginally standardised on children aged 11 to 16 years (Uttar Pradesh sample, uncertain); used clinically more widely
ItemsCommonly cited: Koh's Block Design 10 designs, Alexander Pass-Along 8 designs, Pattern Drawing 8 cards, Picture Construction 5 pictures, Immediate Memory for digits forward and backward. Treat individual counts as uncertain (sources differ).
Subtests / scales / indices5 subtests: (1) Koh's Block Design Test; (2) Alexander Pass-Along Test; (3) Pattern Drawing Test; (4) Immediate Memory (auditory digit span, forward and backward); (5) Picture Construction Test. A shorter battery (omitting tests for illiterate groups) is also described (uncertain which).
Scoring and normsRaw scores (with time credits on several tests) converted from tables to a performance quotient / IQ equivalent; separate norms reported for literate and illiterate groups (uncertain)
MeasuresNonverbal, practical (performance) intelligence; visuospatial reasoning, planning, short-term memory
Indian adaptationIt is itself an Indian battery
Exam trap

The Bhatia Battery's memory subtest is Immediate Memory (digits), the only one that is not a performance task. Seguin Form Board is NOT in the Bhatia Battery, nor is Cube Construction or Picture Completion; these are common distractors. Bhatia's Koh's Block Design uses only part of the original 17 designs.

Kohs Block Design Test

The person is given coloured cubes and a printed design card and must arrange the blocks so that their top faces reproduce the design. Success requires analysing the whole design into parts (analysis) and putting the blocks together again (synthesis), so it measures visuospatial reasoning and nonverbal abstract ability. Wechsler borrowed it as the Block Design subtest, which is still one of the best single measures of g in the Wechsler batteries and is sensitive to right parietal brain damage.

FieldDetail
Full nameKohs Block Design Test
Author(s), yearSamuel C. Kohs (1920 article; 1923 book Intelligence Measurement: A Psychological and Statistical Study Based upon the Block-Design Tests)
VersionsOriginal 1920/1923; adapted into the Wechsler Block Design subtest (1939 onward), Bhatia Battery (1955), Alexander's battery
PublisherStoelting (original materials; uncertain)
Age rangeOriginally about 5 years to adult (uncertain)
Items17 designs (original), using up to 16 blocks. Cube faces: red, white, blue, yellow, red/white diagonal, blue/yellow diagonal. Bhatia uses 10 designs (sources commonly cite).
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle test
Scoring and normsAccuracy and time (time credits); mental age in the original
MeasuresVisuospatial analysis and synthesis, nonverbal reasoning, visuomotor construction
Indian adaptationIncluded in the Bhatia Battery (1955)
Exam trap

16 is the number of blocks; 17 is the number of designs in the original Kohs test. Examiners offer both. The author is Samuel Kohs (often printed "Koh's" in Indian texts).

Alexander Pass-Along Test

The person is given a shallow tray containing coloured (red and blue) wooden blocks and must reach a target pattern shown on a card by sliding blocks along the tray, without lifting any piece out. It requires planning several moves ahead, so it measures practical problem solving and foresight. Alexander believed tests like this tap a practical ability (his "F" factor) that matters for technical and vocational success.

FieldDetail
Full nameAlexander Pass-Along Test
Author(s), yearW. P. Alexander (1932); part of the Alexander Performance Scale (with Kohs Block Design and Cube Construction) described in Intelligence, Concrete and Abstract (1935)
VersionsOriginal 1932; Bhatia Battery version (1955)
PublisherUncertain
Age rangeChildren and adolescents, about 8 years upward (uncertain)
ItemsOriginal: 9 designs (uncertain); Bhatia version: 8 designs (commonly cited)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle test
Scoring and normsSuccess within time limit plus time credits
MeasuresPlanning, practical intelligence, visuospatial problem solving
Indian adaptationIncluded in the Bhatia Battery (1955)
Exam trap

The rule is "slide, do not lift". Alexander's Performance Scale = Pass-Along + Kohs Block Design + Cube Construction; Bhatia replaced Cube Construction with other tests.

Seguin Form Board Test

The child is shown a wooden board with 10 recesses of different shapes; the shapes are removed and stacked, and the child must put them back as quickly as possible. It is repeated over three trials. It is one of the oldest psychological instruments, originally a training device in Edouard Seguin's physiological method of educating children with intellectual disability, later turned into a timed test. In Indian clinics it is used as a quick nonverbal measure for young children and people with intellectual disability, with results expressed as a mental age.

FieldDetail
Full nameSeguin Form Board Test (SFB)
Author(s), yearEdouard Seguin (form board in his 1866 book Idiocy and its Treatment by the Physiological Method; used earlier in the 1840s). Standardised as a test by Henry H. Goddard (about 1912; year sources differ), the "Goddard form board".
VersionsGoddard's version is the standard; Indian norms in use (author uncertain)
PublisherVarious Indian suppliers (uncertain)
Age rangeAbout 3 to 14 years (Indian norms commonly up to about 11 or 14; sources differ)
Items10 shapes (for example circle, square, triangle, star, cross, oval, semicircle, hexagon, rectangle, diamond or similar; exact set varies by version)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle test, 3 trials
Scoring and normsTime per trial; shortest time (and sometimes total time) converted to mental age; ratio IQ
MeasuresVisual discrimination of form, visuomotor coordination, speed, nonverbal ability
Indian adaptationIndian norms widely used and listed in the Indian ID certification guidelines; norm author uncertain
Exam trap

Seguin = 10 shapes, 3 trials, shortest time. Seguin was a French physician-educator of the intellectually disabled (pupil of Jean Itard), not a psychometrician. Seguin is not part of the Bhatia Battery.

Gesell Drawing Test

The child copies a series of simple geometric figures (circle, cross, square, triangle, diamond and similar) from cards. Arnold Gesell observed that the ability to copy each shape appears at a typical age (for example a circle around 3 years, a cross around 4, a square around 4 to 5, a triangle around 5, a diamond around 7), so the most complex figure copied correctly gives a developmental level. It is used in Indian clinics as a quick visuomotor and developmental screen.

FieldDetail
Full nameGesell Drawing Test (figure copying from the Gesell developmental norms)
Author(s), yearArnold Gesell (figure-copying norms in the 1920s to 1940s Yale developmental work; exact year of the test form uncertain)
VersionsIndian clinical versions in use; details uncertain
PublisherUncertain
Age rangeAbout 3 to 12 years (sources differ)
ItemsNumber of figures varies by version (often cited around 10 or 11): uncertain
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle test
Scoring and normsEach figure has an age norm; yields mental or developmental age
MeasuresVisuomotor integration, developmental level
Indian adaptationUsed with Indian norms in ID assessment; adapter uncertain
Exam trap

Do not confuse the Gesell Drawing Test (copying figures) with the Goodenough Draw-a-Man (drawing a person) or the Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender 1938, 9 figures, used for visuomotor and organic screening).

MISIC: Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children

MISIC is the Indian adaptation of the original WISC (1949), developed by Arthur J. Malin at the Child Guidance Centre, Nagpur. Items were changed to suit Indian children's experience, and Indian norms were developed. Like the original WISC, it gives a Verbal Quotient, a Performance Quotient and a Full Scale IQ. It is still a standard test for school-age children in Indian clinics and for ID and learning disability certification.

FieldDetail

Author(s), yearArthur J. Malin (1969), Nagpur
Versions1969 (single edition in common use)

Age range6 to 15 years (some sources 6 to 15:11)

MeasuresGeneral intelligence, verbal and performance
Indian adaptationIt is the Indian adaptation of WISC (1949)
Exam trap

Malin adapted two instruments, both in Nagpur: MISIC (from the WISC, 1969) and the Indian VSMS (from Doll's Vineland, 1965/1971). MISIC is based on the WISC, not the WAIS or the Stanford-Binet.

Binet-Kamat Test of Intelligence

The Binet-Kamat is the Indian adaptation of the 1916 Stanford-Binet, prepared by V. V. Kamat for Kannada- and Marathi-speaking children in the Bombay-Karnataka region. Like its parent, it is an age scale: items are grouped by age level, the examiner finds the basal age (level where all items are passed) and adds months of credit for items passed above it to get a mental age, then computes a ratio IQ. It is one of the tests named for ID assessment in India.

FieldDetail

Binet-Kamat is an age scale with ratio IQ, unlike the deviation-IQ Wechsler adaptations. Kamat adapted the Stanford-Binet; Malin adapted the WISC.

WAPIS: Wechsler Adult Performance Intelligence Scale (Indian adaptation)

WAPIS is P. Ramalingaswamy's Indian adaptation of the performance half of the WAIS. Because the performance subtests depend less on language and schooling, they were judged more suitable across India's linguistic groups. It yields a Performance IQ for adolescents and adults and is commonly used in Indian adult assessment, including disability certification.

WAPIS gives a Performance IQ only; there is no verbal scale. Author: Ramalingaswamy. Its 5 subtests are the 5 WAIS performance subtests, including Picture Arrangement and Object Assembly, which were dropped from WAIS-IV.

Draw-a-Man Test: Indian norms (Phatak)

Pramila Phatak (often printed Pathak), of the M. S. University of Baroda, collected Indian norms for Goodenough's Draw-a-Man test, so that Indian children's drawings are judged against Indian age-mates. Administration and point scoring follow Goodenough's method (see section 1). Phatak's Baroda group is also responsible for the Baroda norms of the Bayley scales (DASII, section 3).

Phatak (Baroda) = Indian Draw-a-Man norms and DASII (Bayley adaptation). Do not attribute either to Malin (Nagpur) or Kamat.

Other Indian intelligence measures

Several other names circulate in Indian test catalogues and old question papers. Their details are less well documented, so treat this list as orientation, not as exam-grade fact.

  • Raven's Progressive Matrices, Indian norms: SPM and CPM norms for Indian samples exist in several studies and in Indian supplements; the author most often cited for one norm set (Deshpande) is uncertain, and different clinics use different norm tables.
  • WISC-IV INDIA and WAIS-IV INDIA: Pearson India editions with Indian norms have been marketed; years and sample details uncertain.
  • Bhatt's Nonverbal Test of Intelligence: named in some Indian catalogues; author details, year and structure uncertain. Do not rely on any specific fact about it without the manual.
  • Kamat: see Binet-Kamat above; there is no separate widely used "Kamat's" test beyond the Binet-Kamat.
  • No reliable source was found for an Indian WISC adaptation by "Bhatnagar"; it is left out deliberately.
High yield

The government guidelines for assessing intellectual disability in India (Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment) list Indian or Indian-normed tests such as VSMS (Malin), DST (Bharath Raj), Binet-Kamat, MISIC, WAPIS, Seguin Form Board, Gesell Drawing, Raven's, Bhatia Battery and DASII for different ages. The exact list in the current notification should be checked, but this cluster is what examiners mean by "Indian tests for ID".

3. Developmental, adaptive behaviour and social maturity scales

An IQ test asks "how well can this person reason when tested?". A developmental scale asks "has this infant or young child reached the milestones expected for their age?", and an adaptive behaviour scale asks "how well does this person actually manage everyday life: dressing, eating, communicating, getting along with others, handling money?". These questions matter because intellectual disability is defined by two criteria, deficits in intellectual functioning and deficits in adaptive functioning, both beginning in the developmental period. A child can score 65 on an IQ test yet function independently at home and school; under DSM-5 that child may not meet criteria.

Most developmental and adaptive scales are completed by observation and informant interview (usually a parent or caregiver) rather than by testing the person directly, which makes them usable with infants and with severely disabled people who cannot be tested. Scores are typically expressed as an age equivalent (developmental age, social age) and a quotient: Developmental Quotient (DQ) = developmental age / chronological age x 100, and Social Quotient (SQ) = social age / chronological age x 100. Modern scales (Vineland-3, Bayley-4) use deviation standard scores with mean 100, SD 15 instead.

A further distinction is screening versus diagnostic assessment. Screening tests (Denver II, TDSC) are short, used on many children (for example in paediatric clinics), and only flag children who need further evaluation; they do not give a diagnosis or a reliable quotient. Diagnostic scales (Bayley, Gesell schedules, DASII) take longer and give a quantified developmental level.

Definition

Adaptive behaviour: the conceptual, social and practical skills people learn and perform in their everyday lives (AAIDD definition). Social age (SA): the age level of a person's social competence on the VSMS. SQ = SA / CA x 100. DQ = developmental age / CA x 100.

VSMS: Vineland Social Maturity Scale (Doll)

Edgar Doll, at the Vineland Training School in New Jersey, wanted to measure what intelligence tests missed: how far a person can look after themselves and take part in social life. The VSMS is a list of everyday competencies arranged in order of the age at which they are typically achieved (from "crows, laughs" in infancy to managing others' affairs in adulthood). An informant who knows the person well is interviewed, each item is marked as habitually performed or not, and the total gives a social age and a social quotient. It was the first standardised adaptive behaviour scale.

FieldDetail
Full nameVineland Social Maturity Scale
Author(s), yearEdgar A. Doll (1935); revised manual 1947, 1953 (The Measurement of Social Competence), 1965
Versions1935 original, 1965 revision; superseded in the US by the Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales (1984)
PublisherEducational Test Bureau / American Guidance Service (uncertain)

Items117 items
Subtests / scales / indices8 categories: Self-Help General (SHG), Self-Help Eating (SHE), Self-Help Dressing (SHD), Self-Direction (SD), Occupation (O), Communication (C), Locomotion (L), Socialization (S)
Scoring and normsItems passed give a total score converted to Social Age; SQ = SA / CA x 100
MeasuresSocial competence, self-help, adaptive behaviour
Indian adaptationA. J. Malin, Nagpur (see next entry)
Exam trap

Doll's original VSMS has 117 items and 8 categories; Malin's Indian version has 89 items. The VSMS is given to an informant, not to the child. Self-Help is split into three (General, Eating, Dressing), which is why there are 8 categories and not 6.

VSMS (Indian adaptation, Malin)

A. J. Malin adapted Doll's VSMS for Indian children at the Child Guidance Centre, Nagpur, removing or changing items that did not fit Indian life and restricting the range to childhood. It is the standard Indian measure of social age and social quotient and is listed in the Indian guidelines for certifying intellectual disability, where SQ is used alongside IQ.

FieldDetail
Full nameVineland Social Maturity Scale, Indian adaptation (Malin)
Author(s), yearA. J. Malin (1965; published manual 1971, sources differ), adapting Edgar Doll (1935)

PublisherIndian Psychological Corporation / Nagpur (uncertain)

Subtests / scales / indicesSame 8 categories as Doll (Self-Help General, Self-Help Eating, Self-Help Dressing, Self-Direction, Occupation, Communication, Locomotion, Socialization)

Indian adaptationThis entry is the Indian adaptation
Exam trap

Malin VSMS: 89 items, 0 to 15 years. Bharath Raj DST: 88 items, 0 to 15 years. These two numbers are deliberately placed side by side in MCQs. VSMS gives SQ; DST gives DQ.

Vineland-3: Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales, Third Edition

The Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales replaced Doll's VSMS with a modern, multidimensional instrument. Adaptive behaviour is divided into domains (communication, daily living skills, socialization, motor), each with subdomains, and scored with deviation standard scores. It can be given as a semi-structured interview with a caregiver, or as parent or teacher rating forms. It is the most widely used adaptive behaviour measure in the world and central to intellectual disability and autism diagnosis.

FieldDetail
Full nameVineland Adaptive Behavior Scales, Third Edition
Author(s), yearSara S. Sparrow, David A. Balla and Domenic V. Cicchetti (1984, VABS); Sparrow, Cicchetti and Balla (2005, Vineland-II); Sparrow, Cicchetti and Celine A. Saulnier (2016, Vineland-3)
VersionsVineland-3 (2016); Vineland-II (2005); VABS (1984: Interview Edition Survey Form, Expanded Form, Classroom Edition); based on Doll's VSMS (1935)
PublisherAmerican Guidance Service (1984, 2005); Pearson (2016)
Age rangeVineland-3 and Vineland-II: birth to 90 years. VABS (1984): birth to 18:11 and low-functioning adults.
ItemsVABS 1984: Survey Form 297 items, Expanded Form 577 items, Classroom Edition 244 items. Vineland-3: Comprehensive and Domain-Level forms with different lengths; exact counts uncertain.
Subtests / scales / indicesDomains and subdomains (Vineland-3): Communication (Receptive, Expressive, Written); Daily Living Skills (Personal, Domestic, Community); Socialization (Interpersonal Relationships, Play and Leisure, Coping Skills); Motor (Fine, Gross; optional, birth to 9 years in Vineland-3); Maladaptive Behavior (optional: Internalizing, Externalizing, critical items).
Adaptive Behavior Composite (ABC) from Communication, Daily Living Skills and Socialization.
Forms: Interview Form, Parent/Caregiver Form, Teacher Form (each in Comprehensive and Domain-Level versions).
Scoring and normsABC and domain standard scores mean 100, SD 15; subdomain v-scale scores mean 15, SD 3; age equivalents; Maladaptive scores reported as v-scale scores
MeasuresAdaptive behaviour (personal and social sufficiency)
Indian adaptationNone standard; Malin's VSMS is used instead in Indian certification
Exam trap

The Vineland ABS (1984) authors are Sparrow, Balla and Cicchetti, not Doll. Subdomain scores use a unique v-scale (mean 15, SD 3), not mean 10. The Maladaptive Behavior domain is optional and does not enter the ABC; Motor domain is also optional and age-limited.

AAMD / AAIDD Adaptive Behavior Scales

The American Association on Mental Deficiency (AAMD, later AAMR, now AAIDD) sponsored its own adaptive behaviour scale, used especially in residential and school settings. Part One rates independent functioning and skills; Part Two rates maladaptive (problem) behaviour. A newer AAIDD instrument, the Diagnostic Adaptive Behavior Scale (DABS), was built specifically to decide whether a person meets the adaptive criterion for intellectual disability.

FieldDetail
Full nameAAMD Adaptive Behavior Scale; AAMR Adaptive Behavior Scale: Residential and Community (ABS-RC:2) and School (ABS-S:2); AAIDD Diagnostic Adaptive Behavior Scale (DABS)
Author(s), yearKazuo Nihira, Ray Foster, Max Shellhaas and Henry Leland (1969; revised 1974). ABS-RC:2: Nihira, Leland and Nadine Lambert (1993). ABS-S:2: Lambert, Nihira and Leland (1993). DABS: Marc J. Tassé and colleagues (about 2015 to 2017; year uncertain).
VersionsAs above
PublisherAAMD/AAMR (early); PRO-ED (1993 editions); AAIDD (DABS)
Age rangeABS-S:2: 3 to 21 years. ABS-RC:2: 18 to 80 (uncertain). DABS: 4 to 21 years.
ItemsUncertain (varies by edition)
Subtests / scales / indicesPart One: adaptive domains (for example independent functioning, physical development, economic activity, language development, numbers and time, domestic activity, prevocational/vocational activity, self-direction, responsibility, socialization; exact list by edition uncertain). Part Two: maladaptive behaviour domains. DABS: conceptual, social, practical.
Scoring and normsStandard scores and percentiles
MeasuresAdaptive and maladaptive behaviour
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

The AAMD ABS has a two-part structure (adaptive and maladaptive). The DABS uses the three AAIDD/DSM-5 domains: conceptual, social, practical.

DST: Developmental Screening Test (Bharath Raj)

The DST is an Indian screening test of a child's developmental level, prepared by J. Bharath Raj in Mysore. It lists developmental milestones (motor, language, social, self-help) placed at the age at which Indian children typically achieve them. The examiner, through observation and parent report, finds how many items the child passes and converts this into a developmental age and a DQ. It is quick and widely used for young children in Indian ID assessment.

FieldDetail
Full nameDevelopmental Screening Test
Author(s), yearJ. Bharath Raj (1977; revised 1983, sources differ), Mysore
Versions1977 and 1983 (sources differ on which is the original)
PublisherSwayamsiddha Prakashana, Mysore (uncertain)
Age rangeBirth to 15 years
Items88 items
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle scale of milestones (motor, language, social, self-help areas; not scored separately)
Scoring and normsDevelopmental age; DQ = DA / CA x 100
MeasuresOverall developmental level (screening)
Indian adaptationAn original Indian test
Exam trap

DST (Bharath Raj): 88 items, 0 to 15, gives DQ. Malin VSMS: 89 items, 0 to 15, gives SQ. Do not confuse the Indian DST with the American Denver Developmental Screening Test (DDST).

Gesell Developmental Schedules

Arnold Gesell, at the Yale Clinic of Child Development, filmed and observed thousands of infants and young children and described the typical sequence and timing of developmental behaviours. His schedules turn these norms into an assessment: the child is observed with standard toys and situations, and behaviour in each of four fields is matched to an age level. Gesell's maturational theory (development unfolds according to an inner biological timetable) shaped the whole field, and the Bayley, Denver and Indian DST all borrow from his items.

FieldDetail
Full nameGesell Developmental Schedules
Author(s), yearArnold Gesell (1925, The Mental Growth of the Pre-School Child; 1940, The First Five Years of Life, with colleagues). Revised by Hilda Knobloch and Benjamin Pasamanick (1974, Gesell and Amatruda's Developmental Diagnosis, 3rd ed); later Knobloch, Stevens and Malone (1980).
Versions1925, 1940, 1974 revision, 1980 revision; Gesell Institute's later Gesell Developmental Observation (revised edition, about 2011; uncertain)
PublisherVarious; Gesell Institute (later products)
Age rangeAbout 4 weeks to 5 or 6 years (sources differ)
ItemsUncertain (behaviour items at key ages)
Subtests / scales / indices4 fields (classic): Motor (gross and fine), Adaptive, Language, Personal-Social. (Some later versions separate gross and fine motor, giving 5.)
Scoring and normsMaturity age per field; DQ = maturity age / CA x 100
MeasuresDevelopmental status, neuromotor integrity, early detection of delay
Indian adaptationItems used in Indian DST and Gesell Drawing; no single standard Indian adaptation (uncertain)
Exam trap

Gesell's four fields are Motor, Adaptive, Language, Personal-Social. The term "adaptive" in Gesell means problem-solving with objects (an early form of cognition), not adaptive behaviour in the Vineland sense. Gesell is the name for maturational theory.

Bayley-4: Bayley Scales of Infant and Toddler Development, Fourth Edition

The Bayley is the most widely used individually administered infant test. The examiner presents standard toys and tasks (finding a hidden object, stacking blocks, responding to words) to the infant, and the caregiver completes questionnaires for social-emotional and adaptive behaviour. Its main use is identifying developmental delay and following up high-risk infants (for example preterm babies). Infant test scores predict later IQ only weakly, especially before about 18 months, which examiners like to ask.

FieldDetail
Full nameBayley Scales of Infant and Toddler Development, Fourth Edition
Author(s), yearNancy Bayley (BSID 1969; based on her California First-Year Mental Scale, 1933); BSID-II (1993); Bayley-III (2006); Bayley-4 (2019, Nancy Bayley and Glen P. Aylward)
VersionsBayley-4 (2019); Bayley-III (2006); BSID-II (1993); BSID (1969)
PublisherPsychological Corporation; now Pearson
Age rangeBayley-4 and Bayley-III: 16 days to 42 months. BSID-II: 1 to 42 months. BSID (1969): 2 to 30 months.
ItemsPer scale; total uncertain
Subtests / scales / indicesBayley-III and Bayley-4, 5 domains: Cognitive; Language (Receptive and Expressive Communication subtests); Motor (Fine and Gross Motor subtests); Social-Emotional (caregiver questionnaire; Greenspan Social-Emotional Growth Chart in Bayley-III); Adaptive Behavior (caregiver questionnaire; ABAS-II in Bayley-III, Vineland-3 based in Bayley-4).
Cognitive, Language and Motor are administered to the child; Social-Emotional and Adaptive are caregiver reports.
BSID and BSID-II: Mental Scale (MDI), Motor Scale (PDI), Infant Behavior Record / Behavior Rating Scale.
Scoring and normsBayley-III/4 composite scores mean 100, SD 15; subtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3. BSID/BSID-II MDI and PDI mean 100, SD 16 (uncertain for BSID-II, commonly cited SD 15).
MeasuresInfant and toddler development
Indian adaptationDASII (Pramila Phatak, Baroda): Indian norms of the Bayley (see next entry)
Exam trap

Bayley-III split the old Mental and Motor scales into Cognitive, Language, Motor plus the two caregiver-report scales, giving 5 domains. MDI/PDI belong to BSID and BSID-II only. Age range: 16 days to 42 months (3.5 years), not "0 to 5 years".

DASII: Developmental Assessment Scales for Indian Infants

DASII is the Indian adaptation of the Bayley Scales, developed in Baroda by Pramila Phatak and colleagues by restandardising Bayley items on Indian infants (the "Baroda norms"). It gives separate motor and mental developmental ages and quotients and is widely used in Indian paediatric and child development centres. The Trivandrum Developmental Screening Chart was derived from it.

FieldDetail
Full nameDevelopmental Assessment Scales for Indian Infants
Author(s), yearPramila Phatak and colleagues, M. S. University of Baroda (Baroda norms of the Bayley, 1970s; DASII revision 1997 is commonly cited; years uncertain)
VersionsBaroda norms of BSID (1970s); DASII revised edition (about 1997)
PublisherUncertain
Age rangeBirth to 30 months
ItemsCommonly cited 230 items (67 motor + 163 mental); uncertain
Subtests / scales / indicesMotor scale and Mental scale
Scoring and normsMotor Developmental Quotient (MoDQ) and Mental Developmental Quotient (MeDQ); developmental ages
MeasuresInfant motor and mental development
Indian adaptationIt is the Indian adaptation of the BSID (1969)
Exam trap

DASII is the Indian Bayley (not the Indian Denver or Gesell). Its age range (0 to 30 months) matches the original 1969 BSID (2 to 30 months), not Bayley-III.

Denver II: Denver Developmental Screening Test, revised

The Denver is a screening test for children from birth to 6 years used in paediatric clinics. Each item is drawn on a chart as a bar showing the ages at which 25, 50, 75 and 90 percent of children pass it. The examiner draws a vertical age line through the chart and tests the items it crosses; a child who fails an item that 90 percent of younger children pass has a "delay". The result is a screening category (normal, suspect, untestable), not a quotient.

FieldDetail
Full nameDenver II (Denver Developmental Screening Test, second edition)
Author(s), yearWilliam K. Frankenburg and Josiah B. Dodds (1967, DDST); revised DDST-R (1981); Denver II (Frankenburg, Dodds, Archer, Shapiro and Bresnick, 1990; manual 1992)
VersionsDenver II (1990/1992); DDST-R (1981); DDST (1967); Denver Prescreening Questionnaire (parent version)
PublisherDenver Developmental Materials, Denver
Age rangeBirth to 6 years
ItemsDenver II: 125 items (plus 5 test-behaviour ratings). Original DDST (1967): 105 items.
Subtests / scales / indices4 sectors: Personal-Social, Fine Motor-Adaptive, Language, Gross Motor
Scoring and normsItems scored Pass, Fail, No opportunity, Refusal; interpreted as Advanced, OK/Normal, Caution, Delay; test result Normal, Suspect or Untestable
MeasuresDevelopmental screening (risk of delay)
Indian adaptationIndian validation studies exist; no single standard Indian edition (uncertain)
Exam trap

Denver is a screening test; it does not give an IQ or DQ. Sectors: Personal-Social, Fine Motor-Adaptive, Language, Gross Motor (fine motor is paired with adaptive). Item counts: 105 (DDST 1967) versus 125 (Denver II).

TDSC: Trivandrum Developmental Screening Chart

The TDSC is a very quick Indian screening chart for infants, designed at the Child Development Centre, Thiruvananthapuram (Kerala), so that health workers and doctors could flag developmental delay in a few minutes. Items were selected from the Baroda norms (DASII) of the Bayley, and each is drawn as a horizontal bar spanning the 3rd to 97th percentile age of passing; a child who fails an item whose bar lies entirely to the left of their age line is considered delayed.

FieldDetail

Author(s), yearM. K. C. Nair and colleagues, Child Development Centre, Thiruvananthapuram (1991; year sources differ)
VersionsTDSC (0 to 2 years); an extended TDSC for 0 to 6 years was later reported (item count uncertain)

Items17 items (original 0 to 2 year chart; commonly cited, treat as uncertain)

Scoring and normsPass or fail against the age line; any failed item to the left of the age line indicates delay

Exam trap

TDSC comes from Kerala (Trivandrum), is built on Baroda (DASII) norms of the Bayley, and is a screening chart, not a diagnostic scale.

Classifying intellectual disability: IQ ranges, adaptive criteria and Indian certification

Older systems classified intellectual disability (formerly "mental retardation") mainly by IQ ranges, roughly 2, 3, 4 and 5 SD below the mean of 100. DSM-IV-TR (2000) used overlapping bands (for example "50 to 55 up to about 70") to allow for measurement error. DSM-5 (2013) made a major change: the diagnosis still requires IQ about 2 SD below the mean (approximately 65 to 75, that is 70 plus or minus 5 for measurement error), but severity (mild, moderate, severe, profound) is now defined by adaptive functioning in three domains (conceptual, social, practical), not by IQ. ICD-11 (2019, in force 2022) uses "disorders of intellectual development" with mild, moderate, severe, profound and provisional categories, based on standardised tests where available (mild about 2 to 3 SD below the mean; moderate 3 to 4 SD; severe and profound more than 4 SD, separated from each other only by adaptive behaviour).

In India, intellectual disability is one of the 21 disabilities under the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016; "benchmark disability" is 40 percent or more, which gives access to reservations and benefits. The government guidelines for assessment (Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment; a 2018 gazette notification under the RPwD Act replaced earlier guidelines) use standardised Indian tests to obtain an IQ and a social quotient (SQ, from VSMS) for children and adults, and a DQ for young children (with "global developmental delay" for children under 5). Certification is by a medical board that includes a clinical psychologist. The percentage table below is the one widely quoted in Indian texts from the earlier guidelines; verify it against the current notification before relying on it.

LevelDSM-IV-TR IQICD-10 IQ (mental age in adults)DSM-5 (2013)Indian disability percentage (widely cited, verify)
Borderline intellectual functioning71 to 84 (V-code, not ID)Not a category (ICD-9 used 70 to 84 "borderline")Not ID; V-code "borderline intellectual functioning"IQ 70 to 79: 25 percent
Mild50 to 55 up to about 7050 to 69 (MA 9 to under 12 years)Severity by adaptive functioning; IQ about 65 to 75 is the diagnostic zoneIQ 50 to 69: 50 percent
Moderate35 to 40 up to 50 to 5535 to 49 (MA 6 to under 9)By adaptive functioningIQ 35 to 49: 75 percent
Severe20 to 25 up to 35 to 4020 to 34 (MA 3 to under 6)By adaptive functioningIQ 20 to 34: 90 percent
ProfoundBelow 20 or 25Below 20 (MA under 3)By adaptive functioningIQ below 20: 100 percent
Exam trap

In DSM-5, severity is defined by adaptive functioning, not IQ; IQ ranges were removed from the severity criteria. "Borderline intellectual functioning" is not intellectual disability in any system. ICD-10 mild ID is IQ 50 to 69, while DSM-IV-TR wrote "50 to 55 up to about 70". In ICD-11, severe and profound are distinguished only by adaptive behaviour. In India, SQ from VSMS is used alongside IQ in certification, which is why Malin's VSMS is so frequently examined.
