4. Objective personality inventories

An objective personality inventory is a self-report questionnaire. The person reads a fixed set of statements ("I often feel tense", "I like going to parties") and answers in a fixed format (true/false, yes/no, a 3, 4 or 5 point agreement scale, or a forced choice between two statements). It is called "objective" not because the person is being objective about themselves, but because the scoring is objective: two scorers with the same answer sheet always get the same score, unlike a projective test where interpretation depends on the clinician. The weakness is the mirror image of the strength: the person can see what is being asked, so they can fake good, fake bad, answer carelessly, or simply agree with everything (acquiescence). This is why the serious clinical inventories carry validity scales that check how the test was taken before any clinical scale is read.

There are three classic ways to build an inventory, and examiners love to ask which test used which.

  • Rational (logical or content) keying: the author writes items that obviously reflect the trait, based on theory or clinical judgement. Example: Woodworth Personal Data Sheet, Bell Adjustment Inventory, the MMPI-2 content scales, the Taylor Manifest Anxiety Scale (items chosen by clinicians' judgement).
  • Empirical criterion keying: the author does not care what an item seems to mean; an item is kept only if it statistically separates a criterion group (say, diagnosed depressed patients) from normals. The item "I prefer a shower to a bath" could land on a scale purely because the groups answered it differently. Examples: MMPI (1943), CPI, Strong Vocational Interest Blank.
  • Factor-analytic construction: the author gives a large item pool to many people and lets factor analysis reveal which items cluster into dimensions. Examples: Raymond B. Cattell's 16PF, Hans Eysenck's EPI/EPQ, J. P. Guilford's inventories, and the Big Five tradition (NEO-PI-R).

Many modern tests mix methods; the MMPI-2 Restructured Clinical scales, for instance, were built by factor analysis inside an empirically keyed instrument, and Theodore Millon's MCMI combined theory, internal consistency and external validation (a "sequential" approach).

The first personality inventory was the Woodworth Personal Data Sheet (Robert S. Woodworth, 1917, published 1919), built during World War I to screen US army recruits for "shell shock" proneness (emotional instability). It had about 116 yes/no questions (sources give 116; treat the exact count as uncertain) drawn from psychiatric symptom lists, so it was rationally keyed. Every later inventory is a descendant of this idea of a "paper psychiatric interview".

High yield

First personality inventory: Woodworth Personal Data Sheet (1917). Classic empirically keyed test: MMPI (Hathaway and McKinley, 1943). Classic factor-analytic tests: 16PF (Raymond Cattell), EPQ (Hans Eysenck), NEO-PI-R (Paul Costa and Robert McCrae). "Sane man's MMPI" or the test for normal people built the empirical way: CPI (Gough).

MMPI: Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory

The MMPI was built at the University of Minnesota hospitals to give psychiatrists a quick, standardised aid to diagnosis. Starke R. Hathaway (a psychologist) and J. Charnley McKinley (a neuropsychiatrist) wrote about 1,000 statements, administered them to diagnosed patient groups and to normal visitors to the hospital (the "Minnesota normals"), and kept items that separated each patient group from normals: this is pure empirical criterion keying. The person answers True or False (or Cannot Say). Scores are converted to T scores and interpreted mainly as code types, the two or three highest elevated clinical scales (for example a "2-7" code: depressed and anxious), because the scales turned out to be poor at pure diagnosis but rich in correlates.

FieldDetail
Full nameMinnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory
Author(s), yearStarke R. Hathaway and J. Charnley McKinley; first published 1943 (work began 1939 to 1940; first papers 1940). K scale added by Paul Meehl and Hathaway (1946); scale 0 (Si) by Leon Drake (1946)
VersionsOriginal MMPI (1943), replaced by MMPI-2 (1989), MMPI-2-RF (2008), MMPI-3 (2020); adolescent MMPI-A (1992) and MMPI-A-RF (2016)
PublisherUniversity of Minnesota Press (originally distributed by The Psychological Corporation; current versions distributed by Pearson)
Age rangeOriginally 16 and above
Items550 different statements; the printed booklet had 566 items because 16 items were repeated (originally to help machine scoring and check consistency)
Subtests / scales / indicesValidity (4): ? (Cannot Say: number of omitted items), L (Lie: naive claims of virtue), F (Infrequency: rare, deviant answers, raised in faking bad, confusion or severe pathology), K (Correction: subtle defensiveness; a fraction of K is added to scales 1, 4, 7, 8, 9).
Clinical (10): 1 Hs Hypochondriasis; 2 D Depression; 3 Hy Hysteria (conversion); 4 Pd Psychopathic Deviate; 5 Mf Masculinity-Femininity; 6 Pa Paranoia; 7 Pt Psychasthenia (anxiety, obsessiveness); 8 Sc Schizophrenia; 9 Ma Hypomania; 0 Si Social Introversion.
Hundreds of supplementary research scales (e.g. MacAndrew Alcoholism Scale, Welsh A and R, Barron Ego Strength Es)
Scoring and normsLinear T scores (mean 50, SD 10); clinically significant elevation T 70 or more on the original MMPI. Norms: about 700 Minnesota normals (mostly rural, white, visitors to the hospital), a famous weakness
MeasuresPsychopathology and personality; profile and code-type interpretation
Indian adaptationHindi and other regional translations of MMPI scales were used in Indian research (including a Hindi MMPI work associated with Indian clinical centres); no single standard nationally normed version is attested. Mark details as uncertain
Exam trap

Scale 5 (Mf) and scale 0 (Si) were NOT built by comparing a patient group with normals: Mf contrasted heterosexual men with homosexual men and with women, and Si (Drake 1946) contrasted college women high and low in social participation. Also: the K scale was added later (1946), so the original validity scales of 1943 were ?, L and F. And "566 items" is a booklet with 16 repeats; the number of different items is 550. Psychasthenia (Pt) is the old Janet term for obsessive-compulsive and anxious traits, not "weakness of the body".

Memory hook

Clinical scales in order: "Hs D Hy, Pd Mf Pa, Pt Sc Ma Si" (Hypochondriacs Deeply Hate Psychopaths Making Particularly Peculiar Schizoid Manic Sounds). Scales 1, 2, 3 = the "neurotic triad"; 6, 7, 8, 9 = the "psychotic tetrad". A 1-3 code with a low 2 = the "conversion V".

MMPI-2: Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory, Second Edition

The MMPI-2 kept the original clinical scales (so decades of code-type research still apply) but rewrote dated, sexist or religiously intrusive items, added new items, and collected a modern, nationally representative norm sample. It introduced uniform T scores so that a given T means the same percentile across eight of the clinical scales, and lowered the clinical cut-off to T 65. It also added a large new layer of validity, content and restructured scales.

FieldDetail
Full nameMinnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory, Second Edition
Author(s), yearJames N. Butcher, W. Grant Dahlstrom, John R. Graham, Auke Tellegen and Beverly Kaemmer (Restandardization Committee), 1989. Manual revised 2001. RC scales: Auke Tellegen and colleagues, 2003
VersionsMMPI-2 (1989; revised manual 2001); successor forms MMPI-2-RF (2008) and MMPI-3 (2020). MMPI-2 remains in use
PublisherUniversity of Minnesota Press; distributed by Pearson Assessments
Age range18 and above
Items567 true/false items (the first 370 items allow scoring of the basic validity and clinical scales)
Subtests / scales / indicesValidity: ? (Cannot Say), VRIN (Variable Response Inconsistency: random responding), TRIN (True Response Inconsistency: yea-saying or nay-saying), F (Infrequency), FB or Fb (Back Infrequency, items in the second half of the booklet), FP or Fp (Infrequency-Psychopathology, Arbisi and Ben-Porath 1995, items rare even among psychiatric patients), L (Lie), K (Correction), S (Superlative Self-Presentation, Butcher and Han 1995). FBS (Symptom Validity Scale, Lees-Haley) was added to standard scoring later (2007, sources differ slightly on the year).
Clinical: the same 10 scales (1 Hs to 0 Si), plus Harris-Lingoes and Si subscales.
Content scales (15; Butcher, Graham, Williams and Ben-Porath 1990): ANX Anxiety, FRS Fears, OBS Obsessiveness, DEP Depression, HEA Health Concerns, BIZ Bizarre Mentation, ANG Anger, CYN Cynicism, ASP Antisocial Practices, TPA Type A, LSE Low Self-Esteem, SOD Social Discomfort, FAM Family Problems, WRK Work Interference, TRT Negative Treatment Indicators.
Restructured Clinical (RC) scales (9; Tellegen et al. 2003): RCd Demoralization, RC1 Somatic Complaints, RC2 Low Positive Emotions, RC3 Cynicism, RC4 Antisocial Behavior, RC6 Ideas of Persecution, RC7 Dysfunctional Negative Emotions, RC8 Aberrant Experiences, RC9 Hypomanic Activation.
PSY-5 (Allan Harkness and John McNulty): Aggressiveness, Psychoticism, Disconstraint, Negative Emotionality/Neuroticism, Introversion/Low Positive Emotionality. Supplementary scales (e.g. MAC-R, APS, AAS, Es, Do, Re, Mt, PK)
Scoring and normsT scores (mean 50, SD 10); uniform T for scales 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9 and content scales; linear T for 5 and 0. Clinical elevation T 65 or more. Norms: 2,600 US adults (1,138 men, 1,462 women)
MeasuresAdult psychopathology, personality, response style; widely used in forensic work
Indian adaptationTranslations used in Indian research; no standard Indian-normed MMPI-2 attested (uncertain)
Exam trap

Cut-off shift: T 70 on the MMPI but T 65 on the MMPI-2. Item count shift: 550 (566 printed) to 567. RC scales came in 2003, 14 years after the MMPI-2, and they were built by first extracting a "demoralization" factor (RCd) that inflated every original clinical scale. There is no RC5 and no RC0, because Mf and Si were not restructured. VRIN catches random answering; TRIN catches answering "True" (or "False") regardless of content.

MMPI-2-RF: MMPI-2 Restructured Form

The MMPI-2-RF is a shorter, psychometrically cleaner test built from MMPI-2 items, organised around the RC scales instead of the old clinical scales. It is hierarchical: three broad higher-order scales, then the RC scales, then narrow specific-problem scales, so a clinician can go from "is there a thought problem?" down to "is it persecutory ideation?". It cannot be used to generate classic code types.

FieldDetail
Full nameMinnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-2-Restructured Form
Author(s), yearYossef S. Ben-Porath and Auke Tellegen, 2008 (RBS scale added 2011)
Versions2008; scoring updates 2011; succeeded by MMPI-3 (2020)
PublisherUniversity of Minnesota Press; Pearson
Age range18 and above
Items338 true/false items (a subset of the 567 MMPI-2 items)
Subtests / scales / indices51 scales (50 at 2008 launch; RBS added 2011): 9 validity (VRIN-r, TRIN-r, F-r, Fp-r, Fs Infrequent Somatic Responses, FBS-r, RBS Response Bias, L-r, K-r); 3 Higher-Order (EID Emotional/Internalizing Dysfunction, THD Thought Dysfunction, BXD Behavioral/Externalizing Dysfunction); 9 RC scales; 23 Specific Problems scales (somatic/cognitive, internalizing, externalizing, interpersonal); 2 Interest scales (AES Aesthetic-Literary, MEC Mechanical-Physical); 5 PSY-5 revised scales
Scoring and normsNon-gendered T scores (mean 50, SD 10), elevation usually T 65; norms 2,276 adults drawn from the MMPI-2 normative sample
MeasuresPsychopathology in a hierarchical, Big-Five-compatible structure
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

338 items and 51 scales (not 567, not 10 clinical scales). The RF norms are gender-neutral, unlike the MMPI-2. If an option says "the MMPI-2-RF yields the 2-7-8 code type", it is wrong: code types belong to the original clinical scales.

MMPI-3: Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-3

The MMPI-3 is the 2020 successor to the MMPI-2-RF. It keeps the hierarchical RF design, refreshes and adds items (for example on eating concerns, compulsivity, impulsivity and self-importance), and, crucially, has a new normative sample collected around 2020, so the norms are no longer three decades old.

FieldDetail
Full nameMinnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-3
Author(s), yearYossef S. Ben-Porath and Auke Tellegen, 2020
VersionsMMPI-3 (2020), current; English, US Spanish and Canadian French forms
PublisherUniversity of Minnesota Press; Pearson
Age range18 and above
Items335 true/false items
Subtests / scales / indices52 scales: 10 validity (CRIN Combined Response Inconsistency, VRIN, TRIN, F, Fp, Fs, FBS, RBS, L, K); 3 Higher-Order (EID, THD, BXD); 8 RC scales (RC3 Cynicism dropped); 26 Specific Problems scales; 5 PSY-5 scales
Scoring and normsT scores (mean 50, SD 10), non-gendered; new US normative sample of 1,620 adults matched to census projections

Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Count ladder: MMPI 550 (566), MMPI-2 567, MMPI-2-RF 338, MMPI-3 335, MMPI-A 478, MMPI-A-RF 241. The RF has 51 scales and the MMPI-3 has 52. The MMPI-3 has 8 RC scales, not 9.

MMPI-A and MMPI-A-RF: adolescent forms

Adolescents answered the adult MMPI in exaggerated ways (normal teenagers looked pathological on adult norms), so separate adolescent forms with adolescent norms and youth-relevant items (school, family, peer and conduct problems) were produced. The MMPI-A follows the MMPI-2 design; the MMPI-A-RF follows the restructured design.

FieldDetail
Full nameMinnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-Adolescent; MMPI-A-Restructured Form

Exam trap

An 18-year-old can take either the MMPI-A or the MMPI-2 (the manuals allow judgement for 18-year-olds still in school). The number 1,620 appears twice in this family: the MMPI-A normative sample and the MMPI-3 normative sample. Do not mix them up with 2,600 (MMPI-2) or 2,276 (MMPI-2-RF).

MCMI: Millon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory

Theodore Millon built the MCMI to assess personality disorders and clinical syndromes in people already in mental health settings, in close step with the DSM's multiaxial system (Axis II personality, Axis I syndromes). It is shorter than the MMPI and is anchored in Millon's own biosocial-evolutionary theory of personality. Its unique feature is the base rate (BR) score: instead of a T score, raw scores are transformed so that the cut-offs reflect how common each disorder actually is in psychiatric populations.

FieldDetail
Full nameMillon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory
Author(s), yearTheodore Millon, 1977 (MCMI-I). MCMI-III with Roger Davis and Carrie Millon (manual 1994, 2nd edition 1997); MCMI-IV with Seth Grossman and Carrie Millon, 2015
VersionsMCMI-IV (2015), current; MCMI-III (1994); MCMI-II (1987); MCMI (1977). Related: MACI (adolescents), MPACI (pre-adolescents), MBMD (medical), MIPS
PublisherNational Computer Systems originally; now Pearson
Age range18 and above (adult clinical populations)
ItemsMCMI-IV: 195 true/false items. MCMI, MCMI-II and MCMI-III: 175 items each
Subtests / scales / indicesMCMI-IV (25 clinical scales + validity):
Clinical personality patterns (12): 1 Schizoid, 2A Avoidant, 2B Melancholic, 3 Dependent, 4A Histrionic, 4B Turbulent, 5 Narcissistic, 6A Antisocial, 6B Sadistic, 7 Compulsive, 8A Negativistic, 8B Masochistic.
Severe personality pathology (3): S Schizotypal, C Borderline, P Paranoid.
Clinical syndromes (7): A Generalized Anxiety, H Somatic Symptom, N Bipolar Spectrum, D Persistent Depression, B Alcohol Use, T Drug Use, R Post-Traumatic Stress.
Severe clinical syndromes (3): SS Schizophrenic Spectrum, CC Major Depression, PP Delusional.
Modifying indices: X Disclosure, Y Desirability, Z Debasement; random response indicators: V Invalidity, W Inconsistency.
45 Grossman Facet Scales (3 per personality scale)
Scoring and normsBase rate scores (0 to 115). BR 75 to 84 = presence of the trait or syndrome; BR 85 and above = prominence (pervasive pattern). BR 60 is roughly the median for psychiatric patients. Normed on clinical (patient) samples, not the general population
MeasuresPersonality disorders and clinical syndromes aligned with DSM
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

The MCMI uses BR scores, not T scores, and its key cut-offs are 75 and 85, not 65 or 70. It is normed on patients, so giving it to a non-clinical person (for example a job applicant) over-pathologises them. 175 items for MCMI I to III; 195 only for MCMI-IV. "Melancholic" (2B) replaced "Depressive", and "Turbulent" (4B) is new in MCMI-IV.

16PF: Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire

Raymond B. Cattell set out to map the whole of normal personality. He started from the "lexical hypothesis" (important traits get words in language), reduced Gordon Allport and Henry Odbert's (1936) list of about 4,500 trait words to a manageable set, and factor-analysed ratings and questionnaire data until he found 16 primary source traits. Cattell insisted on three kinds of data: L-data (life record: ratings and real-life behaviour), Q-data (questionnaire self-report) and T-data (objective test behaviour in a controlled situation). The 16PF is his Q-data instrument. Answers are on a three-choice format (a, b uncertain, c).

FieldDetail
Full nameSixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire
Author(s), yearRaymond B. Cattell, 1949 (with Institute for Personality and Ability Testing, IPAT). 5th edition 1993 (Raymond B. Cattell, A. Karen S. Cattell and Heather E. P. Cattell; technical manual Conn and Rieke 1994)
Versions5th edition (1993), current; earlier editions 1949, 1956/57, 1961/62, 1967/68 (Forms A to E). Downward extensions: HSPQ (High School Personality Questionnaire, about 12 to 18), CPQ (Children's Personality Questionnaire, 8 to 12), ESPQ (Early School, 6 to 8)
PublisherIPAT; now Pearson / PSI (sources differ by country)
Age range16 and above
Items5th edition: 185 items. Earlier Forms A and B: 187 items each; Forms C and D shorter (105 items each, uncertain); Form E for low literacy (128 items, uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indices16 primary factors: A Warmth; B Reasoning; C Emotional Stability; E Dominance; F Liveliness; G Rule-Consciousness; H Social Boldness; I Sensitivity; L Vigilance; M Abstractedness; N Privateness; O Apprehension; Q1 Openness to Change; Q2 Self-Reliance; Q3 Perfectionism; Q4 Tension.
5 global factors (5th ed.): Extraversion, Anxiety, Tough-Mindedness, Independence, Self-Control.
Response style indices: Impression Management, Acquiescence, Infrequency
Scoring and normsSten scores ("standard ten"): 1 to 10, mean 5.5, SD 2; stens 1 to 3 low, 8 to 10 high
MeasuresNormal adult personality; used in counselling, selection, research
Indian adaptationHindi adaptation commonly attributed to S. D. Kapoor (Psycho-Centre, New Delhi); exact form and year uncertain
Exam trap

There is no factor D, J, K or P in the adult 16PF (they appeared in Raymond Cattell's research or child forms but did not replicate in adults), which is why the letters jump from C to E and from I to L. Factor B is an ability (reasoning) scale inside a personality test. Stens have mean 5.5 and SD 2; stanines have mean 5 and SD 2 (9 points). Cattell, not Hans Eysenck, wrote about L, Q and T data.

Memory hook

Q-factors (Q1 to Q4) are the ones found only in Questionnaire data, not in Life ratings: that is why they carry a "Q".

MPI: Maudsley Personality Inventory

Hans J. Eysenck, working at the Maudsley Hospital and Institute of Psychiatry, London, built a short inventory for his two orthogonal dimensions, Extraversion-Introversion and Neuroticism-Stability, out of his factor-analytic work on soldiers and patients. It grew out of the Maudsley Medical Questionnaire (1952, neuroticism only) and was soon replaced by the EPI because it lacked a lie scale and its two scales correlated slightly.

FieldDetail
Full nameMaudsley Personality Inventory
Author(s), yearHans J. Eysenck, 1959
VersionsPreceded by Maudsley Medical Questionnaire (1952); replaced by EPI (1964)
PublisherUniversity of London Press (UK); US edition Educational and Industrial Testing Service (uncertain)
Age rangeAdults (older adolescents)
Items48 items (24 E, 24 N), yes/?/no
Subtests / scales / indicesE (Extraversion), N (Neuroticism)
Scoring and normsRaw scores against British norms
MeasuresTwo-factor personality
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian research in Hindi translation; standard version uncertain
Exam trap

The MPI had no Lie scale; the L scale arrived with the EPI (1964). And the "Maudsley" in the name is the London hospital, not an author.

EPI: Eysenck Personality Inventory

The EPI improved on the MPI: E and N were made independent, the wording was simplified for less educated people, a Lie scale was added to catch faking good, and two parallel forms allowed retesting (for example before and after treatment). Extraverts in Eysenck's theory have low cortical arousal (ascending reticular activating system) and seek stimulation; neurotic people have a labile limbic (visceral brain) system.

FieldDetail
Full nameEysenck Personality Inventory
Author(s), yearHans J. Eysenck and Sybil B. G. Eysenck, 1964
VersionsForms A and B (parallel); replaced by the EPQ (1975)
PublisherUniversity of London Press / Hodder and Stoughton (UK); EdITS (US)
Age rangeAdolescents (about 16) and adults
Items57 yes/no items per form: 24 E, 24 N, 9 L
Subtests / scales / indicesE Extraversion, N Neuroticism, L Lie
Scoring and normsRaw scores, percentile norms; E and N plotted on a two-dimensional circle (melancholic, choleric, sanguine, phlegmatic quadrants)
MeasuresE and N temperament
Indian adaptationHindi translations used in Indian research; standard adapter uncertain
Exam trap

57 items = 24 + 24 + 9. The EPI has no P (Psychoticism) scale: P came in the EPQ (1975). Hans Eysenck mapped his quadrants onto Galen's four humours: stable extravert = sanguine, unstable extravert = choleric, unstable introvert = melancholic, stable introvert = phlegmatic.

EPQ and EPQ-R: Eysenck Personality Questionnaire (and Revised)

The EPQ added Eysenck's third super-factor, Psychoticism (P): tough-minded, cold, aggressive, impulsive, nonconformist, which Eysenck thought ran on a continuum to psychotic and psychopathic disorder. The original P scale had poor reliability and skewed scores, so the EPQ-R (1985) rewrote it. The PEN model (P, E, N) is Eysenck's "giant three".

FieldDetail
Full nameEysenck Personality Questionnaire; Eysenck Personality Questionnaire-Revised
Author(s), yearEPQ: Hans J. Eysenck and Sybil B. G. Eysenck, 1975. EPQ-R: Sybil B. G. Eysenck, Hans J. Eysenck and Paul Barrett, 1985. EPQR-A (abbreviated): Leslie Francis, Laurence Brown and Ronald Philipchalk, 1992
VersionsEPQ (1975; adult and Junior forms); EPQ-R (1985) full form and short form (EPQR-S); EPQR-A abbreviated (1992). Later EPP (Eysenck Personality Profiler) has facets
PublisherHodder and Stoughton (UK); EdITS (US)
Age rangeAdult EPQ: 16 and above; Junior EPQ: about 7 to 15
ItemsEPQ adult: 90 items; Junior EPQ: 81 items. EPQ-R full: 100 items (sources sometimes cite 106 when extra research scales are included; treat as uncertain). EPQR-S short form: 48 items (12 per scale). EPQR-A: 24 items (6 per scale). All yes/no
Subtests / scales / indicesP Psychoticism (tough-mindedness), E Extraversion, N Neuroticism, L Lie (social desirability). Full EPQ-R also has Addiction and Criminality research scales
Scoring and normsRaw scores against British age and sex norms
MeasuresPEN model of personality
Indian adaptationHindi and other Indian-language versions exist in research (adapter and year uncertain)
Exam trap

Sequence: MPI 1959 (E, N; 48 items), EPI 1964 (E, N, L; 57 items), EPQ 1975 (P, E, N, L; 90 items), EPQ-R 1985 (100 items; short 48; abbreviated 24). "Psychoticism" in the EPQ is a normal-range trait (tough-mindedness), not a diagnosis of psychosis. The 24-item abbreviated form is by Francis, Brown and Philipchalk, not by the Eysencks.

NEO-PI-R and NEO-PI-3: NEO Personality Inventory (and NEO-FFI)

Paul T. Costa Jr. and Robert R. McCrae, working at the US National Institute on Aging, built the NEO inventories as the standard measure of the Five-Factor Model (Big Five): Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness to Experience, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness. Each domain is broken into six narrower facets, giving 30 facet scores, so the profile is both broad and fine-grained. Responses are on a 5-point scale (strongly disagree to strongly agree). Form S is self-report; Form R is the observer-rating version (spouse, peer).

FieldDetail
Full nameNEO Personality Inventory, Revised; NEO Personality Inventory-3; NEO Five-Factor Inventory
Author(s), yearPaul T. Costa Jr. and Robert R. McCrae. NEO Inventory (1978, N, E, O only); NEO-PI 1985; NEO-PI-R and NEO-FFI 1992; NEO-PI-3 2005 (McCrae, Costa and Thomas Martin)
VersionsNEO-PI-3 and NEO-FFI-3 (2005/2010 manual), current; NEO-PI-R (1992); NEO-PI (1985)
PublisherPsychological Assessment Resources (PAR)
Age rangeNEO-PI-R: 17 and above; NEO-PI-3: 12 and above (readability improved)
ItemsNEO-PI-R: 240 items (plus 3 validity check questions). NEO-PI-3: 240 items (37 items replaced). NEO-FFI and NEO-FFI-3: 60 items (12 per domain). Original NEO-PI 1985: 181 items (facets only for N, E, O; commonly cited, verify)
Subtests / scales / indicesN Neuroticism: Anxiety, Angry Hostility, Depression, Self-Consciousness, Impulsiveness, Vulnerability. E Extraversion: Warmth, Gregariousness, Assertiveness, Activity, Excitement-Seeking, Positive Emotions. O Openness: Fantasy, Aesthetics, Feelings, Actions, Ideas, Values. A Agreeableness: Trust, Straightforwardness, Altruism, Compliance, Modesty, Tender-Mindedness. C Conscientiousness: Competence, Order, Dutifulness, Achievement Striving, Self-Discipline, Deliberation
Scoring and normsT scores (mean 50, SD 10) for domains and facets
MeasuresFive-Factor Model of normal personality
Indian adaptationTranslations (including Hindi, Marathi) used in research and in the cross-cultural FFM studies; no single standard Indian norm (uncertain)
Exam trap

NEO originally stood for Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness (the 1978 inventory measured only these three); A and C were added in 1985 with full facets in 1992. The NEO-FFI (60 items) gives only the five domains, no facets. "Impulsiveness" is a facet of Neuroticism here, not of Conscientiousness.

Memory hook

OCEAN or CANOE for the five domains; 5 x 6 = 30 facets; 30 x 8 = 240 items.

BFI, BFI-2 and BFI-10: Big Five Inventory family

The Big Five Inventory was designed as a short, free, plain-language alternative to the NEO for research. Each item is a short phrase completing "I see myself as someone who...", for example "...is talkative", rated on a 5-point agree-disagree scale. The BFI-2 added facets and balanced keying (equal true- and false-keyed items to control acquiescence). The BFI-10 is a 10-item ultra-short screen for large surveys.

FieldDetail
Full nameBig Five Inventory; Big Five Inventory-2; Big Five Inventory-10
Author(s), yearBFI: Oliver P. John, Eileen M. Donahue and Robert L. Kentle, 1991. BFI-2: Christopher J. Soto and Oliver P. John, 2017. BFI-10: Beatrice Rammstedt and Oliver P. John, 2007
VersionsBFI (1991); BFI-10 (2007); BFI-2 (2017) with BFI-2-S (30 items) and BFI-2-XS (15 items)
PublisherFree for non-commercial research (Berkeley Personality Lab); no commercial publisher
Age rangeAdolescents and adults
ItemsBFI: 44 items. BFI-2: 60 items. BFI-10: 10 items (2 per domain, one true- and one false-keyed)
Subtests / scales / indicesBFI and BFI-10: Extraversion, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, Neuroticism, Openness. BFI-2 domains: Extraversion, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness, Negative Emotionality, Open-Mindedness; 15 facets: Sociability, Assertiveness, Energy Level; Compassion, Respectfulness, Trust; Organization, Productiveness, Responsibility; Anxiety, Depression, Emotional Volatility; Intellectual Curiosity, Aesthetic Sensitivity, Creative Imagination
Scoring and normsMean item scores (1 to 5) per domain; no formal clinical norms
MeasuresBig Five domains (and facets in BFI-2)
Indian adaptationWidely translated (Hindi and other languages) in research; no single standard version
Exam trap

The BFI-10 is by Rammstedt and John (2007), NOT Costa and Robert McCrae, who authored the NEO family. The 10-item TIPI (Ten-Item Personality Inventory) is a different test by Samuel Gosling, Peter Rentfrow and William Swann (2003). BFI = 44 items; BFI-2 = 60, the same count as the NEO-FFI, which is a classic distractor.

HEXACO-PI-R: HEXACO Personality Inventory-Revised

Kibeom Lee and Michael C. Ashton found, in lexical studies across many languages, a six-factor structure rather than five: the extra factor, Honesty-Humility (sincerity, fairness, greed avoidance, modesty), predicts exploitation, workplace deviance and the "dark triad" better than Big Five Agreeableness.

FieldDetail
Full nameHEXACO Personality Inventory-Revised
Author(s), yearKibeom Lee and Michael C. Ashton; HEXACO-PI 2004; revised form (HEXACO-PI-R) around 2008 to 2009 (sources differ)
Versions200-item, 100-item and 60-item forms; self and observer reports
PublisherFree for research (authors' website)
Age rangeAdolescents and adults
Items200, 100 or 60 (5-point scale)
Subtests / scales / indicesH Honesty-Humility, E Emotionality, X eXtraversion, A Agreeableness (versus anger), C Conscientiousness, O Openness to Experience; 24 facets (4 per factor) plus an interstitial Altruism facet
Scoring and normsMean scores; research norms
MeasuresSix-factor personality
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

H in HEXACO is Honesty-Humility, not Hostility; E is Emotionality (close to, but not identical with, Neuroticism).

MBTI: Myers-Briggs Type Indicator

The MBTI turns Carl Gustav Jung's Psychological Types (1921) into a questionnaire. Jung described two attitudes (extraversion and introversion) and four functions (thinking, feeling, sensing, intuition). Katharine Cook Briggs and her daughter Isabel Briggs Myers added a fourth preference, Judging versus Perceiving, and sort each person into one of 16 types (e.g. INTJ). Items are mostly forced-choice between two words or two statements. It is popular in career counselling and team building, and is intended for normal people, not for diagnosis.

FieldDetail
Full nameMyers-Briggs Type Indicator
Author(s), yearKatharine Cook Briggs and Isabel Briggs Myers; first form 1943 (Briggs-Myers Type Indicator); research edition published by Educational Testing Service 1962; Consulting Psychologists Press from 1975; manual co-author Mary McCaulley (1985)
VersionsForm M (1998, Step I) and Form Q (2001, Step II), current; earlier Form F (166 items) and Form G (126 items); Step III (developmental, 222 items, uncertain)
PublisherThe Myers-Briggs Company (formerly CPP, Consulting Psychologists Press)
Age range14 and above (adolescents and adults)
ItemsForm M: 93 items. Form Q: 144 items (gives 20 facets, 5 per dichotomy). Form G: 126 items
Subtests / scales / indices4 dichotomies: E-I Extraversion-Introversion (energy direction), S-N Sensing-Intuition (perception), T-F Thinking-Feeling (judgement), J-P Judging-Perceiving (orientation to outer world); 16 types
Scoring and normsType assignment with a "preference clarity index"; not T scores of trait amount
MeasuresJungian type preferences
Indian adaptationUsed commercially in India in English; no standard Indian-normed version (uncertain)

Criticisms: it forces continuous, normally distributed traits into dichotomies, so people near the midpoint flip type on retest (large proportions get a different type within weeks, figures around one third to one half are often quoted); it has no neuroticism dimension; its predictive validity for job performance is weak; and the J-P dimension is not in Jung. Factor analyses show its four scales overlap with Big Five Extraversion, Openness (S-N), Agreeableness (T-F) and Conscientiousness (J-P).

Exam trap

Carl Jung did not create the MBTI and did not propose J-P. The authors were a mother and daughter, neither a trained psychologist. Form M = 93, Form Q = 144. MBTI is a type (category) measure; the Big Five are trait (dimension) measures.

CPI: California Psychological Inventory

Harrison G. Gough wanted to measure the "folk concepts" that ordinary people in every culture use to describe each other (dominance, responsibility, sociability, tolerance) in normal populations. He built it largely the empirical way, like the MMPI (and about half the original items came from the MMPI), so it is often called "the sane man's MMPI" or "the normal person's MMPI". Answers are true/false. The later model adds three vector scales that yield a cuboid typology of four lifestyle types.

FieldDetail
Full nameCalifornia Psychological Inventory
Author(s), yearHarrison G. Gough; copyright 1956, published 1957. Revised 1987 (462 items); CPI-434 third edition 1996 (Gough and Pamela Bradley); CPI-260 (about 2002, Gough and Bradley; sources differ)
VersionsCPI-434 (1996) and CPI-260, current; 1987 revision; 1957 original
PublisherConsulting Psychologists Press (now The Myers-Briggs Company)
Age rangeAbout 12 to 13 years and above (adolescents and adults)
ItemsOriginal: 480 items (18 scales). 1987: 462 items. CPI-434: 434 items. CPI-260: 260 items
Subtests / scales / indices20 folk scales (1987 onward): Dominance (Do), Capacity for Status (Cs), Sociability (Sy), Social Presence (Sp), Self-acceptance (Sa), Independence (In), Empathy (Em), Responsibility (Re), Socialization (So), Self-control (Sc), Good Impression (Gi), Communality (Cm), Well-being (Wb), Tolerance (To), Achievement via Conformance (Ac), Achievement via Independence (Ai), Intellectual Efficiency (Ie), Psychological-mindedness (Py), Flexibility (Fx), Femininity/Masculinity (F/M). Gi, Cm and Wb double as validity checks.
3 vector scales: v1 internality-externality, v2 norm-favouring vs norm-questioning, v3 self-realization; types Alpha, Beta, Gamma, Delta; 7 levels of realization.
Special purpose scales (e.g. Managerial Potential, Work Orientation)
Scoring and normsT scores (mean 50, SD 10)
MeasuresNormal interpersonal personality and social functioning
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

CPI is for NORMAL people even though it borrows MMPI method and items. The original had 18 scales and 480 items; the 20 folk scales belong to the 1987 and later editions. The Socialization (So) scale is a well-known predictor of delinquency.

EPPS: Edwards Personal Preference Schedule

Allen L. Edwards showed that people answer inventory items largely according to how socially desirable the item sounds. To neutralise this, he paired statements of roughly equal social desirability, each tapping a different need from Henry Murray's (1938) need system, and made the person choose one: a forced-choice format. Because every choice for one need is a choice against another, the scores are ipsative: they show the relative strength of needs within one person and always sum to the same total, so they cannot properly be compared between people.

FieldDetail
Full nameEdwards Personal Preference Schedule
Author(s), yearAllen L. Edwards, 1953 (first published); revised 1959
Versions1959 revision is the standard form
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation
Age rangeCollege students and adults
Items225 forced-choice pairs (210 distinct pairs plus 15 repeated pairs for the consistency score)
Subtests / scales / indices15 Murray needs: Achievement, Deference, Order, Exhibition, Autonomy, Affiliation, Intraception, Succorance, Dominance, Abasement, Nurturance, Change, Endurance, Heterosexuality, Aggression. Plus a Consistency score
Scoring and normsIpsative raw scores, percentile norms (college and adult)
MeasuresRelative strength of manifest needs
Indian adaptationIndian adaptations exist in research (adapter and year uncertain)
Exam trap

EPPS is based on Murray's needs (the same theory behind the TAT), but it is an objective, not a projective, test. Its forced choice was designed to control social desirability; its price is ipsative scores. 225 pairs, 15 needs (15 x 15 = 225 is a handy check).

GZTS: Guilford-Zimmerman Temperament Survey

J. P. Guilford and Wayne S. Zimmerman condensed Guilford's earlier factor-analytic inventories (STDCR, GAMIN, and the Inventory of Factors) into one survey of ten temperament traits for normal adults.

FieldDetail
Full nameGuilford-Zimmerman Temperament Survey
Author(s), yearJ. P. Guilford and Wayne S. Zimmerman, 1949
Versions1949 form (with later manual revisions)
PublisherSheridan Psychological Services (uncertain for current)
Age rangeAdolescents (high school) and adults
Items300 items (30 per trait)
Subtests / scales / indices10 traits: General Activity, Restraint, Ascendance, Sociability, Emotional Stability, Objectivity, Friendliness, Thoughtfulness, Personal Relations, Masculinity
Scoring and normsC scores / percentiles
MeasuresNormal temperament (factor-analytic)
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

GZTS is factor-analytic (J. P. Guilford), not empirically keyed. Mnemonic of trait initials: GRASEOFTPM.

STAI: State-Trait Anxiety Inventory

Charles D. Spielberger separated two meanings of anxiety: state anxiety, a temporary feeling of tension right now, and trait anxiety, a stable tendency to react to many situations with anxiety. The STAI measures both with two 20-item halves rated on a 4-point scale: for the state half, "how you feel right now"; for the trait half, "how you generally feel". It is used in stress research, pre-surgery studies and therapy outcome studies.

FieldDetail
Full nameState-Trait Anxiety Inventory
Author(s), yearCharles D. Spielberger, Richard L. Gorsuch and Robert E. Lushene, 1970 (Form X). Form Y 1983 (with Peter R. Vagg and Gerard A. Jacobs)
VersionsForm Y (1983), current; Form X (1970). STAIC for children (Spielberger, 1973)
PublisherConsulting Psychologists Press originally; now Mind Garden
Age rangeAdolescents (high school) and adults; STAIC about 9 to 12
Items40 items: 20 State (Y-1), 20 Trait (Y-2)
Subtests / scales / indicesS-Anxiety, T-Anxiety
Scoring and normsEach half 20 to 80; 4-point ratings with reverse-scored anxiety-absent items
MeasuresState and trait anxiety
Indian adaptationHindi versions used in Indian research (adapter and year uncertain)
Exam trap

Spielberger also made the STAXI (anger) and the Test Anxiety Inventory; do not credit the STAI to Taylor. Form Y replaced items in Form X that correlated with depression.

TMAS: Taylor Manifest Anxiety Scale

Janet A. Taylor needed a measure of "drive" for Hull-Spence learning experiments (eyelid conditioning): people high in anxiety (drive) were predicted to condition faster. Clinicians judged which MMPI items reflected manifest (observable) anxiety, and the items most agreed upon formed the scale: so it is a rationally selected scale made from MMPI items.

FieldDetail
Full nameTaylor Manifest Anxiety Scale
Author(s), yearJanet A. Taylor (later Janet Taylor Spence), 1953 (developed in her 1951 work)
Versions1953 form; children's version CMAS (Castaneda, McCandless and Palermo, 1956) later revised as RCMAS (Reynolds and Richmond, 1978)
PublisherPublished in the Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology (research scale)
Age rangeAdults (college students originally)
Items50 items from the MMPI (true/false), often embedded among filler items
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle anxiety score
Scoring and normsRaw score count of anxious answers
MeasuresManifest (chronic) anxiety as drive
Indian adaptationHindi translations used in research (uncertain)
Exam trap

TMAS items come from the MMPI but were chosen by clinicians' judgement (rational), not by criterion keying. It was built for learning theory (drive) research, not for diagnosis.

SCAT: Sinha's Comprehensive Anxiety Test

An Indian, Hindi-English self-report test of general (trait) anxiety widely used in Indian student and clinical research. The person answers yes/no to statements about anxious feelings and symptoms, and the total count is compared with Indian norms.

FieldDetail
Full nameSinha's Comprehensive Anxiety Test
Author(s), yearA. K. P. Sinha and L. N. K. Sinha; year uncertain (commonly given as 1995 for the current manual; earlier versions exist)
VersionsHindi and English forms
PublisherNational Psychological Corporation, Agra
Age rangeAdolescents and adults (exact range uncertain)
Items90 items, yes/no (commonly reported; verify against manual)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total anxiety score
Scoring and normsRaw score against Indian norms, with categories from very low to very high anxiety
MeasuresGeneral anxiety
Indian adaptationIndigenous Indian test
Exam trap

Do not confuse SCAT (Sinha) with the Sport Competition Anxiety Test (Rainer Martens, 1977), which shares the abbreviation SCAT.

Bell Adjustment Inventory

Hugh M. Bell's inventory is a rationally keyed yes/no questionnaire that measures how well a person is adjusted in specific life areas. It is old but remains popular in Indian school and college research through Hindi adaptations.

FieldDetail
Full nameBell Adjustment Inventory
Author(s), yearHugh M. Bell, 1934 (Student Form); Adult Form 1938/1939 (uncertain)
VersionsStudent Form; Adult Form (adds Occupational adjustment); revised 1962 student form (uncertain)
PublisherStanford University Press (original); Indian versions National Psychological Corporation, Agra (uncertain)
Age rangeHigh school and college students; adults (Adult Form)
ItemsStudent form 140 items (commonly cited; uncertain); adult form 160 items (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesStudent Form: Home, Health, Social, Emotional adjustment. Adult Form adds Occupational
Scoring and normsCount of "maladjusted" answers per area; higher = poorer adjustment
MeasuresArea-wise adjustment
Indian adaptationHindi adaptations by R. K. Ojha, and by S. M. Mohsin and Husain Shamshad (the Mohsin-Shamshad adaptation); years and item counts uncertain
Exam trap

Four areas in the student form are home, health, social, emotional; "occupational" belongs only to the adult form. High score = POOR adjustment.

PGI Health Questionnaire N-1

An Indian screening questionnaire for neuroticism and general psychological distress developed at PGIMER, Chandigarh, adapted from the Cornell Medical Index. It is a classic of the PGI battery tradition (alongside the PGI Memory Scale and the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction).

FieldDetail
Full namePGI Health Questionnaire N-1
Author(s), yearS. K. Verma, N. N. Wig and Dwarka Pershad, PGIMER Chandigarh; year commonly given as 1985 (uncertain)
VersionsN-1 (adult); N-2 (Hindi, for less literate) (details uncertain)

The "N" in N-1 refers to neuroticism. Its source is the Cornell Medical Index, not the MMPI.

Rep Test: Kelly's Role Construct Repertory Test

George A. Kelly, in The Psychology of Personal Constructs (1955), argued that each person acts like a scientist, building bipolar personal constructs ("kind vs selfish", "clever vs dull") to predict events (constructive alternativism). The Rep Test draws these constructs out. The person lists people who fill roles (a Role Title List: self, mother, a liked teacher, a disliked person, etc.), then is shown three of them at a time (a triad) and asked "In what important way are two of these alike and different from the third?" The answer gives one construct. In the grid form (repertory grid), every person is rated on every construct, giving a matrix that can be factor-analysed to show how the person's construct system is organised (for example how tightly or loosely constructs hang together).

FieldDetail
Full nameRole Construct Repertory Test (Rep Test); repertory grid technique
Author(s), yearGeorge A. Kelly, 1955. Grid analysis developed by Don Bannister and Fay Fransella (Grid Test of Thought Disorder, 1966)
VersionsList form, grid form; many idiographic variants (no fixed edition)
PublisherNot a commercial test (Kelly's book: W. W. Norton)
Age rangeOlder children to adults
ItemsNot fixed: Kelly's role title list had about 20 to 24 roles (sources differ); number of triads varies
Subtests / scales / indicesElements (people), constructs (bipolar dimensions); grid indices such as cognitive complexity (James Bieri, 1955), intensity and consistency (Bannister)
Scoring and normsIdiographic: no norms; analysed by correlation or factor analysis of the grid
MeasuresThe structure and content of a person's personal construct system
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

The Rep Test is idiographic and has no norms. The Grid Test of Thought Disorder (Bannister and Fransella) applied it to schizophrenic thought disorder (loose construing). Kelly, not Carl Rogers, used the triad method; George Kelly also invented fixed-role therapy.

Q-sort: Q-technique and the California Q-set

In a Q-sort the person sorts a deck of cards, each carrying a descriptive statement ("I am a submissive person"), into piles from "most like me" to "least like me", with a fixed number of cards per pile so the result forms a quasi-normal distribution (commonly 9 piles). William Stephenson (1953) introduced this Q-technique: instead of correlating tests across people (R-technique), it correlates people (or the same person under different instructions) across items. Carl Rogers and his colleagues (John Butler and Gerard Haigh, 1954) used it to measure the gap between the real self and the ideal self: the correlation between the two sorts rose during successful client-centred therapy. Jack Block's California Q-set is a standard 100-item deck sorted by an observer (clinician) to describe someone's personality.

FieldDetail
Full nameQ-sort (Q-technique); California Q-set (CQ-set)
Author(s), yearWilliam Stephenson, 1953 (The Study of Behavior: Q-Technique and Its Methodology; earlier papers from 1935). Applied by Carl Rogers and colleagues (Butler and Haigh, 1954). California Q-set: Jack Block, 1961 (book The Q-Sort Method in Personality Assessment and Psychiatric Research). California Child Q-set: Jeanne and Jack Block (1969/1980, uncertain)
VersionsMany decks; CQ-set (100 items); California Child Q-set (100 items); Q-sorts for attachment (Waters and Deane, 1985)
PublisherCQ-set: Consulting Psychologists Press (uncertain)
Age rangeSelf-sort: adolescents and adults; observer sorts can describe any age
ItemsVariable for general Q-sorts; California Q-set 100 items
Subtests / scales / indicesSelf vs ideal-self correlation; CQ prototypes (e.g. ego-resiliency, ego-control)
Scoring and normsForced quasi-normal distribution; correlations between sorts
MeasuresSelf-concept, self-ideal congruence, observer-described personality
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Stephenson invented the Q-technique; Carl Rogers popularised its clinical use; Block standardised the 100-item California Q-set. Q-technique (correlating persons) is the opposite of R-technique (correlating tests); Cattell's Q-data (questionnaire data) is a different idea that just shares the letter.

5. Aptitude, interest, creativity and attitude measures

This family covers tests that are not about psychopathology or global intelligence. An aptitude test estimates a person's potential to learn or succeed in a specific area in future (mechanical work, clerical work, numerical tasks); an achievement test, by contrast, measures what has already been learned. Because general intelligence tests give one broad score, guidance psychologists wanted multiple aptitude batteries that give a profile of separate abilities, which grew out of factor analysis (L. L. Thurstone's Primary Mental Abilities, 1938). The DAT and GATB are the two classic batteries.

Interest inventories ask what a person likes or dislikes (activities, school subjects, occupations), on the logic that people are happier and stay longer in work that matches their interests. Two construction routes matter: the empirical route (Edward K. Strong Jr.: compare the answers of people successfully employed in an occupation with "people in general") and the homogeneous or rational route (G. Frederic Kuder's broad interest areas; John L. Holland's six RIASEC types).

Creativity tests rest on J. P. Guilford's distinction (presidential address to the APA, 1950; Structure of Intellect model, 1956/1967) between convergent thinking, finding the single correct answer, and divergent thinking, producing many varied answers to an open problem. Divergent production is scored for fluency (number of responses), flexibility (number of different categories), originality (statistical rarity) and elaboration (amount of detail).

Attitude scales measure an evaluative disposition towards an object (a group, the church, an institution). The classic scaling methods differ in how statements are chosen and how a score is formed: Thurstone uses judges to place statements on an interval scale, Likert sums agreement ratings, Guttman builds a cumulative ladder, Emory Bogardus measures social distance, and Osgood maps connotative meaning. Implicit measures (the IAT) try to bypass self-report altogether.

High yield

Aptitude = future potential; achievement = past learning. Strong = empirical occupational keying; Kuder = forced-choice triads, ipsative; Holland = RIASEC hexagon (used inside the Strong as General Occupational Themes). Divergent thinking scoring: fluency, flexibility, originality, elaboration (FFOE).

DAT: Differential Aptitude Tests

The DAT was built for educational and vocational guidance of secondary school students: rather than one IQ, it gives a profile of eight abilities so that a counsellor can say, for example, "strong mechanical and spatial, weak verbal". Each subtest is a timed multiple-choice test. The sum of Verbal Reasoning and Numerical Ability works as an index of scholastic aptitude (general academic ability).

FieldDetail
Full nameDifferential Aptitude Tests
Author(s), yearGeorge K. Bennett, Harold G. Seashore and Alexander G. Wesman, 1947
Versions5th edition (1990), latest major US edition; DAT for Personnel and Career Assessment (DAT-PCA, 1991); earlier editions 1947, 1963, 1972 (Forms S and T), 1982 (Forms V and W). UK and other national versions exist
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation (now Pearson)
Age rangeGrades 7 to 12 (and adults for the PCA form)
ItemsVaries by subtest and edition; total item count uncertain
Subtests / scales / indices8 subtests: VR Verbal Reasoning, NA Numerical Ability, AR Abstract Reasoning, CSA Clerical Speed and Accuracy, MR Mechanical Reasoning, SR Space Relations, SpU Spelling, LU Language Usage. Composite: VR + NA (Scholastic Aptitude)
Scoring and normsPercentiles and stanines by grade and sex
MeasuresMultiple aptitudes for educational and vocational guidance
Indian adaptationIndian adaptation by J. M. Ojha (Manasayan, Delhi); year uncertain
Exam trap

Eight subtests, and Clerical Speed and Accuracy is the only purely speeded one. VR + NA is the scholastic aptitude composite, not "Abstract Reasoning". The DAT is a multiple aptitude battery, not an intelligence test, and not an interest inventory.

GATB: General Aptitude Test Battery

The GATB was built by the United States Employment Service to match job seekers to occupations. Each occupation was given an Occupational Aptitude Pattern (minimum scores on the key aptitudes for that job), so a counsellor could see which job families a person qualifies for.

FieldDetail
Full nameGeneral Aptitude Test Battery
Author(s), yearUnited States Employment Service (US Department of Labor), 1947
VersionsSeveral revisions (Forms A to D); a later "Ability Profiler" replaced it in the US (around 2000, uncertain)
PublisherUS Department of Labor
Age rangeHigh school seniors and adults
Items12 tests (item counts vary; uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indices12 tests (8 paper-and-pencil, 4 apparatus: pegboard and finger dexterity board) measuring 9 aptitudes: G General Learning Ability, V Verbal Aptitude, N Numerical Aptitude, S Spatial Aptitude, P Form Perception, Q Clerical Perception, K Motor Coordination, F Finger Dexterity, M Manual Dexterity
Scoring and normsStandard scores (mean 100, SD 20 in the original system); Occupational Aptitude Patterns
MeasuresVocational aptitudes
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

12 tests but 9 aptitudes. Q is clerical perception (not "quantitative"); K is motor coordination. GATB norming became controversial in the 1980s over race-norming of scores.

SII: Strong Interest Inventory (Strong Vocational Interest Blank)

Edward K. Strong Jr. found that people in the same occupation share a pattern of likes and dislikes, even about things unrelated to the job. He kept items that separated, say, satisfied engineers from "people in general" (empirical criterion keying), so a person's Occupational Scale score says how similar their interests are to those of people happily employed in that job. David Campbell later merged the men's and women's forms and added John Holland's RIASEC framework as the top level of interpretation.

FieldDetail
Full nameStrong Interest Inventory (formerly Strong Vocational Interest Blank; Strong-Campbell Interest Inventory)
Author(s), yearEdward K. Strong Jr., 1927 (SVIB for men; women's form 1933). David P. Campbell: Strong-Campbell Interest Inventory 1974 (revised 1981, 1985 with Jo-Ida Hansen). Strong Interest Inventory 1994; revisions 2004 and 2012
VersionsSII 2012 revision (current), 2004, 1994; SCII 1974/1981/1985; SVIB 1927
PublisherStanford University Press originally; Consulting Psychologists Press (now The Myers-Briggs Company)
Age rangeAbout 14 and above (high school, college, adults)
Items291 items (2004 and 2012); 317 items in 1994 and earlier SCII; SVIB 420 items (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesGeneral Occupational Themes (6): Holland's RIASEC: Realistic, Investigative, Artistic, Social, Enterprising, Conventional. Basic Interest Scales (30 in 2004/2012). Occupational Scales (about 130 occupations, scored separately for men and women, 260 scales in 2012; counts uncertain by edition). Personal Style Scales (5): Work Style, Learning Environment, Leadership Style, Risk Taking, Team Orientation
Scoring and normsStandard scores (mean 50, SD 10) against a General Representative Sample; Occupational scales compare with occupational criterion groups
MeasuresVocational interests
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Strong measures interests, not abilities: a high "physician" score means you like what physicians like, not that you would be a good doctor. The RIASEC themes inside the Strong come from John Holland, not from Strong. The Strong is the classic empirically keyed interest test; Kuder's early forms are homogeneous (rational) area scales.

Kuder Preference Record and KOIS

G. Frederic Kuder presented activities in sets of three and asked the person which they would like most and which least: a forced-choice triad. Items were grouped into broad, internally consistent interest areas, so the score tells the relative strength of each area within the person (ipsative). The later Kuder Occupational Interest Survey moved to occupational scales, comparing the person's pattern with people in specific occupations.

FieldDetail
Full nameKuder Preference Record (Vocational); Kuder Occupational Interest Survey; Kuder General Interest Survey
Author(s), yearG. Frederic Kuder, 1939 (Preference Record, first form; Form C widely used from 1948). KOIS (Form DD) 1966; Kuder General Interest Survey (Form E) 1963/1964
VersionsKuder Preference Record Vocational (Form C), Personal (Form A), KGIS Form E, KOIS Form DD; online Kuder Career Search (1999 onward, uncertain)
PublisherScience Research Associates (SRA); now Kuder Inc.
Age rangeGrade 6 or 9 upward depending on form (Form E from grade 6; KOIS grade 10 and adults)
ItemsForm C: 168 triads (504 activities) (commonly cited; uncertain); KOIS: 100 triads
Subtests / scales / indices10 interest areas (Form C): Outdoor, Mechanical, Computational, Scientific, Persuasive, Artistic, Literary, Musical, Social Service, Clerical. Plus a Verification (V) score for carelessness
Scoring and normsIpsative area scores, percentile profiles; KOIS uses lambda coefficients to occupational groups
MeasuresVocational interests
Indian adaptationIndian adaptations have been used in guidance research (uncertain)
Exam trap

Kuder = triads (most and least liked), Edwards EPPS = pairs. Both forced-choice formats produce ipsative scores. Ten areas in Form C: memorise "Outdoor Mechanical Computational Scientific Persuasive Artistic Literary Musical Social-service Clerical".

SDS: Holland's Self-Directed Search

John L. Holland proposed that people and work environments can each be described by six types arranged on a hexagon (RIASEC): adjacent types are similar (Realistic and Investigative), opposite types are least similar (Realistic and Social). People seek environments that match their type ("congruence"). The SDS is self-administered, self-scored and self-interpreted: the person scores their activities, competencies, occupations and self-estimates, takes the three highest types as a three-letter Holland code (e.g. SAE), and looks up matching jobs in the Occupations Finder.

FieldDetail
Full nameSelf-Directed Search
Author(s), yearJohn L. Holland, 1971 (theory 1959; Vocational Preference Inventory 1953)
VersionsSeveral editions (1977, 1985, 1994 4th edition); 5th edition 2013 (with Robert Messer; uncertain); Form E for low reading level; Career Explorer for younger students
PublisherPsychological Assessment Resources (PAR)
Age rangeAbout 11 or 12 (Career Explorer) to adults
ItemsSection counts vary by edition (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesSections: Occupational Daydreams, Activities, Competencies, Occupations, Self-Estimates; scored on 6 RIASEC types
Scoring and normsRaw summary scores; three-letter code; congruence, consistency and differentiation indices
MeasuresVocational personality type
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

The order around the hexagon is fixed: R, I, A, S, E, C. Holland is the author of the SDS and the RIASEC theory; the Strong only borrowed the themes. "Self-Directed" means it is self-scored, not that it measures self-direction.

TTCT: Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking

E. Paul Torrance turned J. P. Guilford's divergent thinking into a standard battery that could be used from kindergarten to adulthood. In the verbal battery the person writes, for example, as many unusual uses of a cardboard box as possible or ways to improve a toy; in the figural battery the person turns incomplete lines or circles into pictures. Responses are scored against norms for fluency, originality and other dimensions. It is the most widely used creativity test in the world and has long-term predictive studies (Torrance's longitudinal follow-ups).

FieldDetail
Full nameTorrance Tests of Creative Thinking
Author(s), yearE. Paul Torrance, 1966 (Minnesota Tests of Creative Thinking in earlier research versions). Streamlined figural scoring 1984 (with Orlow Ball); later norm updates (1990, 1998, 2008, 2017, uncertain)
VersionsTTCT-Verbal Forms A and B; TTCT-Figural Forms A and B; also Thinking Creatively in Action and Movement (1981) for young children
PublisherPersonnel Press / Ginn originally; Scholastic Testing Service
Age rangeKindergarten through adults
ItemsVerbal: 7 activities. Figural: 3 activities
Subtests / scales / indicesVerbal activities: Asking, Guessing Causes, Guessing Consequences (activities 1 to 3, "Ask-and-Guess"), Product Improvement, Unusual Uses, Unusual Questions (in some forms), Just Suppose. Figural activities: Picture Construction, Picture Completion, Lines (Form A) or Circles (Form B). Scores: Verbal: fluency, flexibility, originality (elaboration in older scoring). Figural (streamlined 1984): fluency, originality, elaboration, abstractness of titles, resistance to premature closure, plus 13 checklist "creative strengths"
Scoring and normsStandard scores and national percentiles; Creativity Index
MeasuresDivergent thinking (creative potential)
Indian adaptationIndian work by Baqer Mehdi and B. K. Passi built on Torrance's model (see below); translated TTCT used in research
Exam trap

The figural TTCT's streamlined scoring (1984) dropped flexibility and added abstractness of titles and resistance to premature closure. So "flexibility" is scored on the verbal TTCT but not on the streamlined figural TTCT. Torrance built on J. P. Guilford; Guilford did not write the TTCT.

AUT: Guilford Alternate Uses Task

The Alternate Uses Task asks the person to list as many uses as possible for a common object (brick, paper clip, newspaper) within a time limit. It comes from Guilford's Structure of Intellect research as a measure of divergent production of semantic classes or transformations, and it is now the most used divergent thinking task in experimental and neuroscience research.

FieldDetail
Full nameAlternate Uses Task (Guilford's Alternative Uses Test; "Unusual Uses")
Author(s), yearJ. P. Guilford and colleagues (Paul Christensen, Philip Merrifield, Robert Wilson), 1960 test form; theory in Guilford 1950 and 1967 (The Nature of Human Intelligence)
VersionsOriginal Sheridan Psychological Services form (two parts, uncertain); many research variants
PublisherSheridan Psychological Services (original); now mostly research use
Age rangeOlder children to adults
ItemsFew objects (commonly 3 to 6 per form; uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesFluency, flexibility, originality, elaboration
Scoring and normsCounts and rarity ratings; usually sample-based
MeasuresDivergent thinking
Indian adaptation"Unusual uses" subtests appear in Baqer Mehdi's and Passi's Indian batteries
Exam trap

Guilford's Structure of Intellect: operations x contents x products (120 abilities in 1967, later 150 and 180). Divergent production is one operation; convergent production is another. The "brick uses" task is Guilford's, not E. Paul Torrance's original idea, though Torrance included "Unusual Uses".

RAT: Remote Associates Test

Sarnoff A. Mednick (1962) defined creativity as forming associations between ideas that are normally far apart: a creative person has a "flat" associative hierarchy and reaches remote associations easily. Each RAT item gives three words and asks for a fourth word that links them all (for example "cottage, Swiss, cake": answer "cheese"). Because each item has one correct answer, the RAT actually requires convergent thinking to land on a remote associate.

FieldDetail
Full nameRemote Associates Test
Author(s), yearSarnoff A. Mednick, 1962 (theory paper in Psychological Review); test form with Martha T. Mednick (1967)
VersionsCollege and adult forms; compound remote associate problems (Bowden and Jung-Beeman, 2003) in insight research
PublisherHoughton Mifflin (1967 form, uncertain)
Age rangeCollege students and adults
ItemsCommonly 30 items per form (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle score (number correct)
Scoring and normsNumber correct within time limit
MeasuresAssociative creativity
Indian adaptationNone standard (language-bound)
Exam trap

Mednick's associative theory of creativity is tested with a single-answer (convergent) format. If an option says "the RAT is a divergent thinking test like the TTCT", it is wrong.

Baqer Mehdi's Tests of Creative Thinking

Baqer Mehdi produced the best-known Indian creativity battery, modelled on J. P. Guilford and E. Paul Torrance and standardised on Indian school children, in verbal (Hindi and English) and non-verbal forms.

FieldDetail
Full nameVerbal Test of Creative Thinking; Non-Verbal Test of Creative Thinking
Author(s), yearBaqer Mehdi, 1973
VersionsVerbal (Hindi and English) and Non-verbal forms
PublisherNational Psychological Corporation, Agra
Age rangeSchool children (roughly upper primary to secondary; exact range uncertain)
ItemsActivity-based, not item-based (count uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesVerbal (commonly reported): Consequences, Unusual Uses, New Relationships, Product Improvement. Non-verbal (commonly reported): Picture Construction, Incomplete Figures, Triangles and Ellipses. Verify subtest names against manual
Scoring and normsFluency, flexibility, originality (verbal); elaboration and originality on non-verbal; Indian norms

Indian adaptationIndigenous Indian test
Exam trap

Indian creativity pair: Baqer Mehdi (1973) and B. K. Passi (1979). Mehdi has separate verbal and non-verbal tests; Passi has six tests.

Passi Tests of Creativity

B. K. Passi's battery assesses creative abilities of Indian secondary school students through a mix of verbal and performance tasks, including tests of seeing problems and inquisitiveness, not just "unusual uses".

FieldDetail
Full namePassi Tests of Creativity
Author(s), yearB. K. Passi, 1979
VersionsSingle standard form
PublisherNational Psychological Corporation, Agra
Age rangeSecondary school students (exact range uncertain)
ItemsSix tests (item counts uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesCommonly listed: Seeing Problems Test, Unusual Uses Test, Consequences Test, Test of Inquisitiveness, Square Puzzle Test, Blocks Test (verify against manual)

Passi includes performance tasks (Square Puzzle, Blocks), so it is not purely verbal.

Attitude scaling methods

The next entries are scaling methods rather than single published tests, but they are examined in the same way: who, when, how many points, and what the key index is.

Thurstone scale: method of equal-appearing intervals

Louis L. Thurstone and Ernest J. Chave (1929, The Measurement of Attitude, on attitude toward the church) first wrote a large pool of opinion statements. Many judges (around 300 in the original work) sorted each statement into 11 piles from most unfavourable to most favourable, without giving their own opinion. Each statement's scale value is the median pile it was placed in; its ambiguity is the spread of judges' placements (the interquartile range, Q). Statements with low ambiguity and evenly spaced scale values (commonly about 20 to 22) form the final scale. A respondent simply ticks the statements they agree with, and their score is the median (or mean) scale value of those statements.

FieldDetail
Full nameThurstone scale (equal-appearing intervals)
Author(s), yearL. L. Thurstone (1928 paper "Attitudes can be measured") and E. J. Chave, 1929
VersionsAlso method of paired comparisons and successive intervals (Thurstone)
PublisherUniversity of Chicago Press (1929 book)
Age rangeNot applicable (method)
ItemsBuilt from a large pool (often over 100); final scale commonly about 20 statements
Subtests / scales / indicesScale value (median), Q value (ambiguity), 11-point judging continuum
Scoring and normsMedian scale value of endorsed items
MeasuresAttitude on an approximately interval scale
Indian adaptationMethod used widely in Indian attitude research
Exam trap

L. L. Thurstone uses judges and 11 categories; the respondent only agrees or disagrees. Likert uses no judges. Weakness: judges' own attitudes can shift where they place items (Carl Hovland and Muzafer Sherif, 1952).

Likert scale: method of summated ratings

Rensis Likert (1932, A Technique for the Measurement of Attitudes, Archives of Psychology) dropped the judges. Respondents rate each statement on a 5-point agreement scale (strongly agree, agree, undecided, disagree, strongly disagree); negatively worded items are reverse-scored; item scores are summed. Items are selected by item analysis: keep items that discriminate between high and low total scorers (or correlate with the total). It is quicker and usually more reliable than Thurstone, and it is the format behind most modern questionnaires.

FieldDetail
Full nameLikert scale (summated ratings)
Author(s), yearRensis Likert, 1932
Versions5-point original; 7-point and other variants
PublisherArchives of Psychology (1932 monograph)
Age rangeNot applicable
ItemsVariable
Subtests / scales / indicesTotal summated score; item-total discrimination
Scoring and normsSum (or mean) of ratings after reverse-scoring
MeasuresAttitude intensity and direction
Indian adaptationUniversal method
Exam trap

Strictly, the Likert scale is the summed set of items; one 5-point item is a "Likert-type item". It is ordinal at item level; total scores are usually treated as interval. Likert is "summated ratings"; L. L. Thurstone is "equal-appearing intervals".

Guttman scale: scalogram analysis (cumulative scaling)

Louis Guttman (1944) wanted a truly unidimensional, cumulative scale: items are ordered by strength so that anyone who agrees with a strong item also agrees with all weaker ones (like the rungs of a ladder, or like "I can walk 1 km, 5 km, 10 km"). Then a single total score tells you exactly which items the person endorsed. The fit is judged by the coefficient of reproducibility: the proportion of responses correctly predicted from the total score; 0.90 or above is the conventional standard for an acceptable Guttman scale.

FieldDetail
Full nameGuttman scale (scalogram analysis)
Author(s), yearLouis Guttman, 1944 (American Sociological Review); developed in the US Army research during World War II (The American Soldier studies, Stouffer et al., 1950)
VersionsMethod; later probabilistic successors (Mokken scaling, Rasch model)
PublisherNot applicable
Age rangeNot applicable
ItemsUsually a small number of graded items
Subtests / scales / indicesCoefficient of reproducibility (criterion 0.90)
Scoring and normsTotal number of items endorsed (cumulative)
MeasuresUnidimensional cumulative attitude
Indian adaptationMethod
Exam trap

Reproducibility 0.90 belongs to Guttman, not to Thurstone or Likert. The Bogardus social distance scale is a cumulative scale in the Guttman sense (accepting someone as a relative by marriage implies accepting them as a neighbour).

Bogardus Social Distance Scale

Emory S. Bogardus (1925) measured prejudice as the degree of closeness a person will accept with members of a group. The respondent marks, for each group, the closest relationship they would willingly admit someone of that group to.

FieldDetail
Full nameBogardus Social Distance Scale
Author(s), yearEmory S. Bogardus, 1925
VersionsRepeated national US surveys (1926, 1946, 1956, 1966); many adaptations, including caste and religion studies in India
PublisherJournal of Applied Sociology (1925)
Age rangeNot applicable
Items7 points per group
Subtests / scales / indices1 Close kinship by marriage; 2 My club as personal chums; 3 My street as neighbours; 4 Employment in my occupation; 5 Citizenship in my country; 6 Visitors only to my country; 7 Would exclude from my country
Scoring and normsSocial distance quotient (lower = closer)
MeasuresPrejudice / intergroup acceptance
Indian adaptationAdapted for caste, religion and regional groups in Indian social psychology research
Exam trap

Seven points; point 1 is the closest (marriage), point 7 is exclusion. It is the earliest of these attitude scales (1925), older than Thurstone (1929) and Likert (1932).

Semantic Differential

Charles E. Osgood, George Suci and Percy Tannenbaum (1957, The Measurement of Meaning) asked people to rate a concept (e.g. "my father", "Congress party") on many bipolar adjective pairs with 7 steps between them (good to bad, strong to weak, fast to slow). Factor analysis across cultures repeatedly found three dimensions of connotative meaning: Evaluation (good-bad), Potency (strong-weak) and Activity (active-passive). The evaluation dimension is usually taken as the attitude.

FieldDetail
Full nameSemantic Differential
Author(s), yearCharles E. Osgood, George J. Suci and Percy H. Tannenbaum, 1957 (Osgood's earlier papers 1952)
VersionsMethod; cross-cultural Atlas of Affective Meanings (Osgood, May and Miron 1975)
PublisherUniversity of Illinois Press (1957 book)
Age rangeNot applicable
ItemsVariable number of 7-point bipolar adjective scales
Subtests / scales / indicesEvaluation, Potency, Activity (EPA)
Scoring and normsMean ratings per factor; distance between concepts
MeasuresConnotative (affective) meaning and attitude
Indian adaptationIncluded Indian languages in Osgood's cross-cultural project (details uncertain)
Exam trap

EPA = Evaluation, Potency, Activity (not "Emotion, Power, Arousal"). Charles Osgood measured connotative meaning, not denotative (dictionary) meaning. 7-point scale.

IAT: Implicit Association Test

The IAT measures automatic associations by reaction time. The person sorts words or pictures into categories with two keys. If two concepts are strongly associated in memory (say "flower" and "pleasant"), sorting is faster when they share a key than when "flower" shares a key with "unpleasant". The difference in latencies is converted into a D score (improved scoring algorithm by Anthony Greenwald, Brian Nosek and Mahzarin Banaji, 2003). It is the best known measure of implicit attitudes and stereotypes (Project Implicit).

FieldDetail
Full nameImplicit Association Test
Author(s), yearAnthony G. Greenwald, Debbie E. McGhee and Jordan L. K. Schwartz, 1998 (Journal of Personality and Social Psychology)
VersionsStandard 7-block IAT; Brief IAT; Single-Category IAT; many content versions (race, gender-science, age)
PublisherResearch tool; Project Implicit (online)
Age rangeChildren (adapted) to adults
ItemsNot item-based; 7 blocks of sorting trials (commonly 20 to 40 trials per block)

Exam trap

The IAT is an indirect (implicit) measure based on reaction time, not a self-report scale. Criticisms: modest test-retest reliability (often around 0.5) and weak prediction of individual behaviour, so it is not suitable for diagnosing an individual's prejudice. Greenwald and Banaji's 1995 paper defined implicit social cognition; the IAT itself is 1998 with McGhee and Schwartz.
