6. Projective techniques

Every test in the earlier sections of this book works the same way: the item is clear, the possible answers are fixed, and the score is a count of how many answers fall in a keyed direction. A projective technique turns that logic upside down. The stimulus is deliberately ambiguous (an inkblot, a vague picture, half a sentence, a blank sheet of paper), the instructions are minimal ("What might this be?", "Tell me a story", "Finish the sentence"), and there is no right answer. Because the stimulus itself contains so little structure, whatever structure appears in the response is assumed to come from the person: their needs, fears, conflicts, defences, ways of perceiving and ways of organising experience. That assumption is called the projective hypothesis.

Definition

The projective hypothesis states that when a person is asked to impose meaning or organisation on an ambiguous, relatively unstructured stimulus, the response reflects the person's own enduring personality: their perceptual style, needs, motives, conflicts and defences. The less structured the stimulus, the more the response is determined by the person rather than by the stimulus.

6.0.1 Where the idea came from

The word "projection" comes from Sigmund Freud, who described it in the 1890s (in his papers on paranoia, 1894 to 1896, and most fully in the Schreber case, 1911) as a defence mechanism: an unacceptable impulse or feeling of one's own is attributed to someone else ("I hate him" becomes "he hates me"). Freud also allowed a broader, non-defensive sense in which people generally read their inner world into the outer world. Projective testing borrowed the broader sense. A person who tells a story about a boy sadly staring at a violin is not necessarily defending against anything; they are simply organising an ambiguous picture using their own concerns.

The phrase "projective methods" was coined by Lawrence K. Frank (1939) in his paper "Projective methods for the study of personality" in the Journal of Psychology. Frank argued that personality is a dynamic process of organising experience, and that the best way to see it is to give a person a "field" with little cultural structure and watch how they structure it, the way an X-ray reveals an inner structure without opening the body. Frank grouped together techniques that already existed: Hermann Rorschach's inkblots (1921), Carl Jung's word association (1910), and Christiana Morgan and Henry Murray's Thematic Apperception Test (1935). Henry Murray (1938, Explorations in Personality) supplied the motivational theory (needs and press) that most storytelling techniques still use.

6.0.2 Why ambiguity matters, and what the techniques share

Projective techniques share five features: (1) an ambiguous or unstructured stimulus; (2) a task that allows an almost unlimited variety of responses; (3) a disguised purpose, so the person usually does not know how the answer will be interpreted, which makes faking harder; (4) a global, holistic interpretation aiming at the whole personality rather than a single trait; and (5) heavy reliance on the trained clinician's judgement. They grew out of the psychodynamic tradition, which explains why so many of them talk about unconscious conflict, psychosexual stages and defence. In modern language many authors prefer the term performance-based personality tests (or "free-response measures") because the person performs a task in front of you and you observe how they do it, which is less theory-laden than "projection".

6.0.3 Gardner Lindzey's five-way classification (1959)

Gardner Lindzey (1959), in "On the classification of projective techniques" (Psychological Bulletin), sorted projective tests by the kind of response they require. This is the classification Indian MCQs ask about most often, and it appears in Anne Anastasi's and Frank Freeman's textbooks.

  1. Association: the person responds to a stimulus with the first word, image or percept that comes to mind. Examples: Rorschach Inkblot Test, Holtzman Inkblot Technique, Somatic Inkblot Series, Word Association Test (Francis Galton, Jung, Kent-Rosanoff), Hand Test.
  2. Construction: the person must create or construct a product, typically a story, from a picture. Examples: Thematic Apperception Test, Children's Apperception Test, Senior Apperception Technique, Roberts Apperception Test, Blacky Pictures, Make-A-Picture-Story.
  3. Completion: the person completes an unfinished stimulus. Examples: Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank, Sacks Sentence Completion Test, Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (completing a cartoon speech balloon), story completion methods.
  4. Choice or ordering: the person chooses among, or arranges in order, a set of alternatives according to some criterion (liking, appropriateness, attractiveness). Examples: Szondi Test (choose liked and disliked photographs), Tomkins-Horn Picture Arrangement Test, and picture-arrangement variants of the TAT.
  5. Expression: the person freely expresses themselves through a medium, and the manner (style) of production matters as much as the product. Examples: Draw-A-Person, House-Tree-Person, Kinetic Family Drawing, finger painting, play techniques, psychodrama, Bender Gestalt used projectively. Lowenfeld's World and Mosaic techniques are placed by different authors under expression or construction.
Memory hook

"A Clever Child Can Express": Association, Construction, Completion, Choice/ordering, Expression. Then attach one anchor test to each: Rorschach (A), TAT (Construction), Sentence completion (Completion), Szondi (Choice), Draw-A-Person (Expression).

6.0.4 The reliability and validity problem

Projective tests have been criticised since the 1950s, and you must be able to explain both sides.

  • Standardisation: many techniques have several competing administration and scoring systems (the Rorschach had five major American systems before 1974; the TAT still has no single official system), so two clinicians may not be giving the same test.
  • Reliability: interscorer reliability is acceptable only when a structured coding system is used (Exner's Comprehensive System, R-PAS, the Rotter ISB). Test-retest reliability is hard to estimate because stories and percepts change from occasion to occasion. Split-half reliability is usually not meaningful because cards are not equivalent items.
  • Validity: global, intuitive interpretation has poor validity. Classic studies by Loren and Jean Chapman (1967, 1969) showed illusory correlation: clinicians "saw" relationships (for example big eyes in a drawing and paranoia) that did not exist in the data, because the signs matched their expectations. Specific, empirically keyed variables fare better: Joni Mihura and colleagues' 2013 meta-analysis in Psychological Bulletin found good validity for some Rorschach variables (especially those indexing thought disorder and perceptual accuracy) and poor validity for others, which shaped R-PAS.
  • Norms: norms are often small, old, or American; cultural influence on content (what is "popular" or "normal") is large, which matters directly in India.
  • Examiner effects: the examiner's behaviour, sex, rapport and even seating position influence responses (Exner seated the examiner side by side to reduce cueing).
  • In their favour: they are hard to fake in a socially desirable direction, they bypass reading ability, they give rich idiographic material for case formulation, and they can reveal thought disorder and perceptual distortion that a self-report questionnaire, which depends on insight, cannot.
High yield

Frank 1939 coined the term; Sigmund Freud supplied the concept of projection; Murray supplied needs and press; Lindzey 1959 supplied the five categories. Projective = unstructured stimulus, unlimited response, disguised purpose, global interpretation. Main weakness: reliability and validity; main strength: resistance to faking and access to unconscious or unreportable material.

Rorschach: Rorschach Inkblot Test

The Rorschach is the best-known projective technique and the prototype of the "association" category. Hermann Rorschach, a Swiss psychiatrist working at Herisau, published it in his 1921 monograph Psychodiagnostik, based on about 400 subjects; he died in 1922, aged 37, before he could develop it further. The person is shown 10 symmetrical inkblots one at a time and asked "What might this be?" (the response or free association phase); the examiner records every response verbatim, then goes back through each response in the inquiry phase to find out where on the blot the person saw it (location) and what about the blot made it look that way (determinants). Rorschach's own insight was that how a person sees (whole versus detail, form versus colour, movement) is more informative than what they see, so the test is fundamentally a test of perception and problem-solving under ambiguity, not just a content test.

The 10 cards

Colour groupCardsNotes
Achromatic (black, white and grey only)I, IV, V, VI, VII5 cards. Card V (bat or butterfly) is the "easiest" popular; Card IV is often called the "father card" and Card VII the "mother card" in older lore (folk interpretation, not validated).
Black and red (achromatic plus red)II, III2 cards. The red was thought to provoke "colour shock".
Fully chromatic (multi-coloured pastels)VIII, IX, X3 cards. Card VIII has the most popular animal response (the side pink animals).

Scoring (coding) categories, built up from scratch

  • Location (where on the blot): W = whole blot; D = common detail (a frequently used area); Dd = unusual detail (a rarely used area); S = white space (always coded together with W, D or Dd, for example WS). Developmental quality (DQ) adds how well the parts are integrated: + (synthesised), o (ordinary), v/+ and v (vague).
  • Determinants (what feature of the blot produced the percept): F = form (shape) alone; M = human movement; FM = animal movement; m = inanimate movement; chromatic colour: FC (form dominant, colour secondary), CF (colour dominant), C (pure colour, no form), Cn (colour naming); C' = achromatic colour (black, white, grey used as colour); T = texture (shading felt as touch, fur); V = vista (shading felt as depth); Y = diffuse shading (light-dark without texture or depth); FD = form dimension (depth from size or perspective, not shading); Fr / rF = reflection; (2) = pair. Shading and achromatic codes also take FT/TF, FV/VF, FY/YF, FC'/C'F forms depending on whether form dominates.
  • Form quality (how well the percept fits the blot): in the Comprehensive System + (superior, elaborated), o (ordinary, common and accurate), u (unusual but acceptable), - (minus, distorted). Poor form quality (high X-%) indexes perceptual distortion and reality-testing problems.
  • Content: H (whole human), (H) (fictional human), Hd (human detail), A, Ad, An (anatomy), Bl (blood), Sx (sex), Xy (X-ray), Na (nature), Bt (botany), Cg (clothing), Fd (food), Fi (fire), Ex (explosion), Art, Ay (anthropology), Cl (clouds), Ge (geography), Hh (household), Ls (landscape), Sc (science), Id (idiographic) and so on.
  • Populars (P): responses given very frequently in the normative sample. The Comprehensive System lists 13 populars (for example a bat or butterfly on Card V, two humans on Card III, the animals on the sides of Card VIII).
  • Z (organisational activity): a weighted score given when a person organises parts of the blot into a meaningful relationship; ZSum and Zd (ZSum minus the expected ZSum) index the efficiency of information scanning (overincorporation versus underincorporation).
  • Special scores (Comprehensive System): cognitive slippage codes DV, INCOM, DR, FABCOM, ALOG, CONTAM (Level 1 and Level 2) summed as WSum6; plus AB, AG, COP, MOR, PER, CP, PSV and the later GHR/PHR (good and poor human representation).

Five classical American scoring systems (before Exner)

Rorschach's early death left the method without an owner, and five American schools developed in parallel: Samuel J. Beck (from his 1937 monograph onward; strongly empirical and normative), Bruno Klopfer (The Rorschach Technique, with Douglas Kelley, 1942; phenomenological, introduced "testing the limits"), Zygmunt Piotrowski (Perceptanalysis, 1957; known for his organic signs), Marguerite Hertz (Cleveland; frequency tables of form level), and David Rapaport and Roy Schafer (with Merton Gill, Diagnostic Psychological Testing, 1945 to 1946; psychoanalytic ego psychology; Schafer 1954 on psychoanalytic interpretation). John Exner's survey of these systems in the late 1960s found that they used different administration, codes and interpretations, so research results could not be compared. That problem is why the Comprehensive System was built.

FieldDetail
Full nameRorschach Inkblot Test (Rorschach Psychodiagnostic Plates; "Rorschach technique")
Author(s), yearHermann Rorschach (Swiss psychiatrist), Psychodiagnostik, 1921. Later systematised by Samuel Beck, Bruno Klopfer, Zygmunt Piotrowski, Marguerite Hertz, David Rapaport and Roy Schafer; integrated by John E. Exner Jr. (1974); R-PAS by Gregory Meyer and colleagues (2011)
VersionsThe 10 plates are unchanged since 1921. Administration and scoring "versions" are the systems: Comprehensive System (Exner 1974; Volume 1 reached its 4th edition in 2003) and R-PAS (2011)
PublisherPlates: originally Ernst Bircher (Bern), then Hans Huber (now Hogrefe). CS materials: Rorschach Workshops; R-PAS materials: Rorschach Performance Assessment System, LLC
Age rangeUsually from about 5 years to adulthood (Comprehensive System child norms start at age 5)
Items10 cards (5 achromatic I, IV, V, VI, VII; 2 black-red II, III; 3 chromatic VIII, IX, X). Number of responses (R) is free in classical systems
Subtests / scales / indicesCoding categories: location, developmental quality, determinants, form quality, content, populars, Z score, special scores. Interpretive indices depend on system (see Exner and R-PAS below)
Scoring and normsVaries by system; CS uses raw ratios and indices with American normative tables; R-PAS converts to standard scores (mean 100, SD 15) with international norms
MeasuresPerceptual accuracy and reality testing, thought disorder, cognitive style (ideation, processing, mediation), affect regulation, stress tolerance and coping resources, self-perception and interpersonal perception
Indian adaptationNo single standard Indian adaptation; the original plates are used. Indian normative and clinical studies exist, but a national Indian norm set is uncertain. Taught and used in clinical psychology departments such as NIMHANS (Bengaluru), CIP (Ranchi) and PGIMER (Chandigarh)
Exam trap

Count and colour questions: 10 cards in total; achromatic = 5 (I, IV, V, VI, VII), black-red = 2 (II, III), fully coloured = 3 (VIII, IX, X). A common distractor says "5 coloured and 5 black" (wrong: 5 achromatic but only 3 fully chromatic plus 2 partly red). Another trap: M is human movement (not "any movement"), FM animal, m inanimate. C' (C prime) is achromatic colour, not chromatic. S (white space) is never coded alone. Rorschach published in 1921, not 1911 (1911 is Eugen Bleuler's schizophrenia and Freud's Schreber case).

Exner CS: Rorschach Comprehensive System

John E. Exner Jr. combined the empirically best-supported elements of the five American systems into one standardised method, published in 1974 as The Rorschach: A Comprehensive System (Volume 1). Administration was fixed: examiner seated side by side with the client, the single standard question "What might this be?", a response phase followed by an inquiry phase, and verbatim recording. Responses are coded, and the codes are tallied into a Structural Summary, a page of frequencies, ratios, percentages and derived indices that is interpreted against normative data. Exner (who died in 2006) set a minimum of 14 responses: protocols with R under 14 were considered unreliable and the person was asked to give more.

FieldDetail
Full nameRorschach Comprehensive System (CS), Exner system
Author(s), yearJohn E. Exner Jr., 1974 (Volume 1); later volumes with Irving Weiner (interpretation, children and adolescents)
VersionsVolume 1: 1st edition 1974, 2nd 1986, 3rd 1993, 4th 2003. Indices revised over time (for example SCZI replaced by PTI around 2000)
PublisherWiley (books); Rorschach Workshops (materials, scoring programme RIAP)
Age rangeAbout 5 years to adult (separate child and adolescent reference data)
ItemsSame 10 Rorschach cards; minimum valid R = 14
Subtests / scales / indicesConstellations (special indices): PTI (Perceptual-Thinking Index, replaced SCZI, the Schizophrenia Index), DEPI (Depression Index), CDI (Coping Deficit Index), S-CON (Suicide Constellation, positive at 8 or more of 12 conditions), HVI (Hypervigilance Index), OBS (Obsessive Style Index). Core variables: R, Lambda (F responses divided by non-F responses; high Lambda suggests a simplifying, avoidant style), EB (Erlebnistypus = M : WSumC; introversive versus extratensive versus ambitent), EA (Experience Actual = M + WSumC, available resources), es (experienced stimulation = FM + m plus SumC' + SumT + SumY + SumV), D score and Adjusted D (EA minus es converted to a scaled score; stress tolerance and control), plus clusters such as X+%, X-%, WSum6, Afr, Egocentricity Index (3r + (2) / R)
Scoring and normsRaw scores, ratios and percentages compared with American normative tables (adult sample about 600 in the 1990s tables; later critiques by Wood, Nezworski and Garb that the norms overpathologised normal people led to international reference norms, Meyer, Erdberg and Shaffer 2007)
MeasuresClusters: controls and stress tolerance, affect, information processing, mediation (reality testing), ideation, self-perception, interpersonal perception, situational stress
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian clinical training; Indian CS norms: uncertain
Exam trap

EB is M versus weighted colour (WSumC), not M versus FM. EA = M + WSumC (resources); es = FM + m + all shading and achromatic (demands); D = EA minus es (scaled). Lambda involves pure F. The minimum R is 14 in the Comprehensive System; in R-PAS the target is 18 to 28. SCZI was renamed and revised as PTI; an MCQ may describe PTI as a "schizophrenia index" in older terms.

R-PAS: Rorschach Performance Assessment System

R-PAS was developed after Exner's death by members of his research council, who wanted to keep only variables with solid empirical support (drawing on the Mihura et al. 2013 meta-analysis) and to fix the biggest practical problem of the CS: the huge variation in the number of responses, which distorts almost every other score. Its key administrative change is R-Optimised administration: the person is asked to give two, maybe three responses per card; if they give only one, the examiner prompts for another, and if they give four, the examiner pulls the card back ("prompt and pull"). This keeps most protocols in the target range of about 18 to 28 responses. Results are reported as standard scores (mean 100, SD 15) against an international normative sample, on two pages of variables.

FieldDetail
Full nameRorschach Performance Assessment System (R-PAS)
Author(s), yearGregory J. Meyer, Donald J. Viglione, Joni L. Mihura, Robert E. Erard, Philip Erdberg, 2011 (manual)
VersionsManual 2011; online scoring platform updated since; a technical supplement and revised norms have been issued (exact later revision dates uncertain)
PublisherRorschach Performance Assessment System, LLC (Toledo, Ohio)
Age rangeAdults; child and adolescent reference values added later (age range of youth norms uncertain, commonly cited as 6 to 17)
ItemsSame 10 cards; 2 to 4 responses per card; target total R about 18 to 28
Subtests / scales / indicesVariables organised in five domains: Administration Behaviors and Observations (for example Pr, Pu, CT), Engagement and Cognitive Processing (for example Complexity, R, F%, Blend, Sy, MC), Perception and Thinking Problems (TP-Comp, EII-3, WSumCog, SevCog, FQ-%, WD-%), Stress and Distress (YTVC', m, MOR, SC-Comp), Self and Other Representation (ODL, SR, MAP/MAHP, PHR/GHR, M-, AGM, COP). Composites include TP-Comp (Thought and Perception), SC-Comp (Suicide Concern), EII-3 (Ego Impairment Index)
Scoring and normsStandard scores mean 100, SD 15, plus percentiles; complexity-adjusted scores; international normative reference sample
MeasuresSame broad domains as CS, restricted to empirically supported variables
Indian adaptationNone standard; Indian R-PAS research: uncertain
Exam trap

Exner (1974) = Comprehensive System, minimum R 14, raw scores, side-by-side seating. R-PAS (2011, Meyer, Viglione, Mihura, Erard, Erdberg) = R-Optimised "prompt and pull", 2 to 4 per card, target 18 to 28, standard scores mean 100 SD 15. Exner did not author R-PAS; he died in 2006. Holtzman (one response per card) is a third, separate approach.

HIT: Holtzman Inkblot Technique

Wayne H. Holtzman (with Joseph Thorpe, Jon Swartz and E. Wayne Herron) built the HIT in 1961 specifically to answer the psychometric criticisms of the Rorschach. Instead of 10 cards with a variable number of responses, it has many more cards and allows only one response per card, so every person contributes the same number of responses and each card can be treated like a test item. It has two parallel forms (A and B) of 45 cards each, which makes test-retest and alternate-form reliability possible, something the Rorschach cannot offer. Responses are scored on 22 standardised variables with norms.

FieldDetail
Full nameHoltzman Inkblot Technique (HIT)
Author(s), yearWayne H. Holtzman, with Joseph S. Thorpe, Jon D. Swartz and E. Wayne Herron, 1961
VersionsForms A and B (1961); a short group form and computer-scored variants were developed later (details uncertain)
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation (original)
Age rangeAbout 5 years to adult
Items45 inkblots per form, 2 parallel forms (A and B), plus 2 practice cards (X and Y); one response per card
Subtests / scales / indices22 scoring variables, for example reaction time, rejection, location, space, form definiteness, form appropriateness, colour, shading, movement, pathognomic verbalisation, integration, human, animal, anatomy, sex, abstract, anxiety, hostility, barrier, penetration, balance, popular
Scoring and normsNormative percentiles for multiple groups (normal children, college students, adults, schizophrenic and other clinical groups)
MeasuresSame broad constructs as Rorschach (perception, thought disorder, affect) plus body-image variables (barrier and penetration, from Seymour Fisher and Sidney Cleveland)
Indian adaptationUncertain; Indian studies have used it but no standard Indian norm set is known
Exam trap

45 cards per form, two forms, total 90 test cards (plus practice cards); one response per card. Do not confuse with the 10-card Rorschach or with the 45 cards being "one form". Its whole selling point is parallel forms and fixed R, i.e. better reliability.

SIS: Somatic Inkblot Series

The Somatic Inkblot Series is a set of inkblots deliberately drawn to suggest body parts, internal organs and anatomical images, so that the person's responses reveal body image, somatic preoccupation and the emotional meaning attached to the body. It was developed by Wilfred A. Cassell, an American psychiatrist, starting around 1980, and is used in psychosomatic medicine and with patients who express distress through bodily symptoms. Like the Rorschach, it is an association technique, but the stimuli are not neutral: they are built to "pull" bodily content.

FieldDetail
Full nameSomatic Inkblot Series (SIS)
Author(s), yearWilfred A. Cassell, about 1980; later work with the Indian psychologist B. L. Dubey is often cited (extent of co-authorship uncertain). The attribution of Indian work to Uday Jain is uncertain
VersionsSIS-I (short series) and SIS-II (longer series); exact card counts: sources differ, SIS-I is often cited as 20 cards (uncertain), SIS-II count uncertain
PublisherUncertain
Age rangeUncertain (adults mainly; child use reported)
ItemsUncertain (see Versions)
Subtests / scales / indicesContent categories for body parts, anatomy, pathology, plus Rorschach-style determinants; standard list uncertain
Scoring and normsUncertain
MeasuresBody image, somatic concern, psychosomatic conflict, trauma-related imagery
Indian adaptationIndian research with the SIS exists (B. L. Dubey is the most often named Indian collaborator); details uncertain
Exam trap

The Somatic Inkblot Series is by Cassell, not Holtzman and not Rorschach. Its blots are intentionally body-suggestive, unlike the "neutral" Rorschach blots. If an MCQ asks which inkblot test targets body image or psychosomatic conflict, this is the answer (the Holtzman barrier and penetration scores are a secondary distractor).

TAT: Thematic Apperception Test

The TAT is the prototype of the "construction" category. The person is shown pictures of people in ambiguous situations and asked to tell a dramatic story for each: what led up to the scene, what is happening now, what the characters are thinking and feeling, and how it ends. The theory (Henry Murray's personology, 1938) is that the storyteller identifies with a central character, the hero, and attributes to the hero their own needs (internal motives, for example need for achievement, affiliation, aggression, autonomy) while describing press (forces in the environment acting on the hero, for example rejection, danger, nurturance). The combination of a need and a press, with its outcome, is a thema. "Apperception" (a term from Johann Friedrich Herbart) means perceiving something in the light of past experience and existing mental contents, which is exactly what the test exploits.

FieldDetail
Full nameThematic Apperception Test (TAT)
Author(s), yearChristiana D. Morgan and Henry A. Murray, 1935 ("A method for investigating fantasies", Harvard Psychological Clinic); manual by Murray, 1943
Versions1935 original set; third and final revision of cards published with the 1943 Harvard manual (this is the set still used)
PublisherHarvard University Press
Age rangeMainly adolescents and adults (commonly from about 14 years); children are better tested with the CAT; sources differ on the lower limit
Items31 cards: 30 pictures plus 1 blank card (card 16). Cards are coded by number and letters: B (boys), G (girls), M (adult males), F (adult females), e.g. 3BM, 6GF, 12M. 11 cards are universal (no letter), for example 1 (boy with violin), 2, 4, 5, 10, 11, 14, 15, 16, 19, 20. Classically 20 cards per person in 2 sessions of 10 (cards 1 to 10, then the more unusual 11 to 20, at least a day apart); in practice clinicians use 8 to 12 selected cards
Subtests / scales / indicesNo subtests. Murray's need-press analysis (needs, press, thema, outcome, hero). Later scoring systems: Leopold Bellak's (1947 onward), David McClelland and John Atkinson's need for achievement scoring (1953), Phebe Cramer's Defense Mechanism Manual, Drew Westen's SCORS (Social Cognition and Object Relations Scale)
Scoring and normsNo single official norm system; mostly qualitative or content-coded. Story features: hero, needs, press, outcome, levels (fantasy versus action), defences, object relations
MeasuresMotives, interpersonal themes, conflicts, defences, object relations, mood and outlook
Indian adaptationUma Chowdhury (1960), Indian adaptation of the TAT (Indian TAT, published by Manasayan, Delhi), with Indian figures, dress and settings; number of cards in the Indian set uncertain
Exam trap

31 cards, not 30 and not 20: 30 pictures plus blank card 16 (on which the person imagines a picture and then tells a story). "20" is the number classically given to one person, in two sessions. Card 1 is the boy with the violin. Authors are Morgan AND Murray (1935); the 1943 manual is Murray's. Murray's theory term is press (environment), not "pressure". The Indian TAT is by Uma Chowdhury, not by Udai Pareek (Pareek adapted the Rosenzweig P-F).

CAT: Children's Apperception Test

Leopold Bellak and Sonya Sorel Bellak created the CAT in 1949 because young children found the TAT's adult human scenes hard to identify with. Following a suggestion attributed to Ernst Kris, they used animals in human-like situations (for example chicks eating at a table with a large hen in the background; a lion with a pipe and cane), on the idea that children identify more readily with animals and that animal pictures are less threatening. The pictures target feeding, sibling rivalry, parental relations, aggression, toilet training and fears of being alone at night.

FieldDetail
Full nameChildren's Apperception Test (CAT; animal form CAT-A)
Author(s), yearLeopold Bellak and Sonya Sorel Bellak, 1949
VersionsCAT (animal) 1949; CAT-S (supplement, 1952) with 10 further animal cards for specific situations such as illness, school, play, mother's pregnancy; CAT-H (human figures, 1965) for older children who find animals childish
PublisherC.P.S. Inc. (New York)
Age range3 to 10 years
Items10 cards (CAT and CAT-H); CAT-S 10 cards
Subtests / scales / indicesNo subtests; Bellak's analysis of hero, needs, conception of the environment, figures seen as, conflicts, anxieties, defences, superego adequacy, ego integration
Scoring and normsQualitative (Bellak TAT/CAT analysis blank); limited normative data
MeasuresChild's relationship with parents and siblings, drives, fears, defences
Indian adaptationUma Chowdhury also produced an Indian adaptation of the CAT (year: commonly cited as 1960s; uncertain), published by Manasayan, Delhi
Exam trap

CAT = animals, 10 cards, 3 to 10 years, the Bellaks 1949. CAT-H (1965) = human figures, not "humanistic". CAT-S = supplement. Do not attribute the CAT to Murray. Both Indian TAT and Indian CAT = Uma Chowdhury.

SAT: Senior Apperception Technique

Leopold Bellak and Sonya Sorel Bellak extended the apperception method to older adults in 1973. The pictures show situations typical of later life: loneliness, illness, family conflict, dependency, loss and also positive moments, so that the elderly person can project concerns about ageing.

FieldDetail
Full nameSenior Apperception Technique (SAT; sometimes "Senior Apperception Test")
Author(s), yearLeopold Bellak and Sonya Sorel Bellak, 1973
Versions1973 original; revisions uncertain
PublisherC.P.S. Inc.
Age rangeOlder adults (about 65 years and above)
Items16 picture cards
Subtests / scales / indicesNone; thematic analysis
Scoring and normsQualitative
MeasuresConcerns of ageing: loneliness, health, dependency, loss, family relations
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

The SAT (Bellaks, 1973) is distinct from the Gerontological Apperception Test (GAT, Robert and Rose Wolk, 1971). Both target the elderly; the authors differ.

RATC: Roberts Apperception Test for Children

Glen E. Roberts and Dorothea S. McArthur published the RATC in 1982 to give child storytelling a standardised, normed scoring system, which the TAT and CAT lack. The pictures show children in everyday situations (family confrontation, school, peer relations, parental affection, aggression), and the stories are scored on adaptive and clinical scales against norms.

FieldDetail
Full nameRoberts Apperception Test for Children (RATC); revised as Roberts-2
Author(s), yearGlen E. Roberts and Dorothea S. McArthur, 1982; Roberts-2 by Glen E. Roberts, 2005
VersionsRoberts-2 (2005); RATC (1982)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services (WPS)
Age rangeRATC 6 to 15 years; Roberts-2 6 to 18 years
ItemsRATC: 27 cards, of which 16 are administered (some cards have separate boy and girl versions). Roberts-2: 16 cards per child from parallel sets (White, Black, Hispanic figures)
Subtests / scales / indicesRATC: adaptive scales (for example reliance on others, support to others, support to the child, limit setting, problem identification, resolution) and clinical scales (for example anxiety, aggression, depression, rejection, unresolved), plus indicators such as atypical response; exact grouping in Roberts-2 differs (theme overview, available resources, problem identification, resolution, emotion, outcome, unusual responses): treat sub-lists as approximate
Scoring and normsNormalised T scores (mean 50, SD 10)
MeasuresChild's perception of common interpersonal situations, social understanding, adaptive and maladaptive coping
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

RATC is the "normed, T-scored" child apperception test, unlike the CAT. Authors are Roberts and McArthur (not Julian Rotter, not Rosenzweig). The figures are human children, not animals.

Blacky: Blacky Pictures

Gerald S. Blum (1950) built the Blacky Pictures to test Sigmund Freud's psychosexual theory directly. A series of cartoons shows a dog, Blacky, with Mama, Papa and a sibling, Tippy, in scenes designed to tap each psychosexual stage and conflict. The person tells a story about each cartoon and then answers multiple-choice and open inquiry questions, so it combines construction with structured questioning.

FieldDetail
Full nameBlacky Pictures (Blacky Test)
Author(s), yearGerald S. Blum, 1950
Versions1950 original; later Blacky Defense Preference Inquiry (Blum, 1956)
PublisherPsychodynamic Instruments (uncertain)
Age rangeChildren (about 5 years) to adults
Items12 cartoons (plus an introductory card introducing the family; whether this is counted varies)
Subtests / scales / indicesPsychosexual dimensions: oral eroticism, oral sadism, anal sadism, oedipal intensity, masturbation guilt, castration anxiety (males) or penis envy (females), positive identification, sibling rivalry, guilt feelings, ego ideal, love object (narcissistic or anaclitic); exact card-to-dimension mapping not given here
Scoring and normsSpontaneous story, inquiry answers and card preferences rated on each dimension; limited norms
MeasuresPsychosexual fixations and conflicts (Freudian)
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Blacky = dog cartoons, Gerald Blum 1950, psychosexual (Freudian) dimensions. Do not confuse it with the CAT (animals but Bellak, ego-psychological, not stage-by-stage). The "most explicitly psychoanalytic test" answer is usually Blacky.

RISB: Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank

Sentence completion is the prototype of the "completion" category. The person is given sentence stems ("I like...", "My father...", "I regret...") and asked to complete each with their real feelings. Julian B. Rotter and Janet E. Rafferty (1950) made it one of the most psychometrically respectable projectives by providing an objective scoring manual that rates each completion for conflict versus positive adjustment, giving a total overall adjustment score. Because the stems are semi-structured, it is the least ambiguous projective, and its purpose is fairly transparent.

FieldDetail
Full nameRotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (RISB)
Author(s), yearJulian B. Rotter and Janet E. Rafferty, 1950; 2nd edition Julian Rotter, Michael Lah and Janet Rafferty, 1992
Versions2nd edition 1992; 1st edition 1950
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation
Age rangeThree forms: high school, college, adult
Items40 sentence stems
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle overall adjustment score (no subtests)
Scoring and normsEach completion rated on a 7-point scale, 0 to 6: conflict responses C3, C2, C1 = 6, 5, 4; neutral N = 3; positive P1, P2, P3 = 2, 1, 0 (higher = more maladjusted). Omissions are prorated. Total range 0 to 240. A cut-off of 135 (college form) is often cited for screening maladjustment
MeasuresOverall adjustment versus maladjustment
Indian adaptationIndian translations and studies exist; standard Indian adaptation uncertain
Exam trap

Rotter ISB (1950, 40 stems, adjustment) is NOT the Rotter Internal-External Locus of Control Scale (I-E Scale, Julian Rotter, 1966): the I-E scale is an objective forced-choice questionnaire of 29 items (23 scored plus 6 fillers). Same author, different construct, different format. Also: in the ISB, a higher score means more conflict.

SSCT: Sacks Sentence Completion Test

Joseph M. Sacks and Sidney Levy (1950) designed the SSCT to give a clinician a quick map of the areas of a person's life where conflict lies. Stems are grouped into 15 attitude areas in four broad domains, and the clinician rates the degree of disturbance in each area rather than producing one total score.

FieldDetail
Full nameSacks Sentence Completion Test (SSCT)
Author(s), yearJoseph M. Sacks and Sidney Levy, 1950
VersionsAdult form (1950); child and adolescent adaptations by others exist (details uncertain)
PublisherUncertain (published in Abt and Bellak's Projective Psychology, 1950)
Age rangeAdolescents and adults
Items60 stems, 4 per area across 15 areas
Subtests / scales / indices4 groups, 15 areas. Family: mother, father, family unit. Sex: women, heterosexual relationships. Interpersonal relationships: friends and acquaintances, superiors, people supervised, colleagues. Self-concept: fears, guilt feelings, own abilities, past, future, goals
Scoring and normsEach area rated 0 (no significant disturbance), 1 (mild), 2 (severe), X (unknown); qualitative summary
MeasuresAreas of conflict and attitudes in family, sexual, interpersonal and self domains
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian clinical practice in translation; standard adaptation uncertain
Exam trap

Rotter ISB = 40 stems, one total adjustment score, cut-off 135. Sacks SSCT = 60 stems, 15 areas, 4 groups (family, sex, interpersonal, self-concept), area ratings 0 to 2. Swapping 40 and 60 is the classic distractor.

P-F Study: Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study

Saul Rosenzweig built the P-F Study from his own frustration-aggression theory. Each item is a cartoon of two people in a mildly frustrating everyday situation: one person says something frustrating, and the other person's speech balloon is empty. The respondent writes the first reply that comes to mind, which is assumed to show how they typically react to frustration. It is a "completion" technique, semi-projective and fairly structured, and is scored objectively on two dimensions: direction of aggression and type of reaction.

FieldDetail
Full nameRosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (P-F Study)
Author(s), yearSaul Rosenzweig; adult form 1945 (theory papers 1944 to 1945; often dated 1945 or 1948 in Indian texts; sources differ); children's form 1948; adolescent form 1976
VersionsAdult form, children's form, adolescent form; revised manuals into the 1970s
PublisherRana House / Psychological Assessment Resources (uncertain)
Age rangeChildren's form about 4 to 13 years; adolescent 12 to 18; adult 14 years and above
Items24 cartoon frustrating situations (ego-blocking and superego-blocking situations; commonly 16 ego-blocking and 8 superego-blocking)
Subtests / scales / indicesDirection: extrapunitive (E, aggression turned outward onto the environment), intropunitive (I, turned onto oneself), impunitive (M, aggression avoided, situation glossed over). Type: obstacle-dominance (O-D, the frustrating obstacle stands out), ego-defence (E-D, the self is defended), need-persistence (N-P, solution of the problem is sought). 3 by 3 = 9 scoring factors (plus 2 variants of E and I, giving 11). GCR (Group Conformity Rating): how often the person gives the modal response; trends and superego patterns also scored
Scoring and normsPercentage of each direction and type; GCR as a percentage; normative tables
MeasuresPatterns of reaction to everyday frustration (frustration tolerance, aggression direction, conformity)
Indian adaptationUdai Pareek (1959), Indian adaptation of the adult form, followed by the children's form (Pareek, with Rosenzweig and others, 1960s); published by Rupa Psychological Centre, Varanasi (uncertain)
Exam trap

Rosenzweig (P-F Study, frustration, cartoons, 24 items) is not Rorschach (inkblots, 10 cards). Extrapunitive = blame outward; intropunitive = blame self; impunitive = blame no one. GCR = Group Conformity Rating. The Indian adaptation is by Udai Pareek, not Uma Chowdhury.

WAT: Word Association Test (Galton, Jung, Kent-Rosanoff)

The word association method is the oldest projective technique. A list of stimulus words is read aloud one at a time, and the person replies with the first word that comes to mind while the examiner times the reaction. Francis Galton (1879) first tried it on himself with 75 words to study the contents of the mind. Carl Gustav Jung (Studies in Word Association, 1904 to 1910; his 1909 to 1910 Clark University lectures) turned it into a clinical tool with a list of 100 stimulus words, using disturbances in responding, called complex indicators, to detect emotionally charged "complexes": prolonged reaction time, failure to respond, repeating the stimulus word, misunderstanding the word, perseveration, very personal or odd responses, clang associations, and failure to reproduce the original answer on a second run (the reproduction test). Jung also combined it with the psychogalvanic (skin conductance) response. Grace H. Kent and Aaron J. Rosanoff (1910) took the opposite, normative route: 100 neutral common words with frequency tables of responses from 1,000 normal adults, so that a response could be scored as common or idiosyncratic, used originally to distinguish psychotic from normal subjects.

FieldDetail
Full nameWord Association Test (Jung's association method; Kent-Rosanoff Free Association Test)
Author(s), yearFrancis Galton, 1879; Carl Jung, 1904 to 1910 (100-word list); Grace Kent and Aaron Rosanoff, 1910; David Rapaport's 60-word list (1946) used diagnostically
VersionsJung list, Kent-Rosanoff list, Rapaport list; many local translations
PublisherKent-Rosanoff published in American Journal of Insanity (1910); no single commercial publisher
Age rangeOlder children to adults
ItemsGalton 75 words; Jung 100; Kent-Rosanoff 100; Rapaport 60
Subtests / scales / indicesJung: complex indicators, reaction time. Kent-Rosanoff: common versus individual (idiosyncratic) responses using frequency tables
Scoring and normsReaction time (in seconds), response content, frequency norms (Kent-Rosanoff from 1,000 normal adults)
MeasuresEmotional complexes (Jung); commonality of thought, thought disorder (Kent-Rosanoff)
Indian adaptationHindi and other Indian language word lists have been constructed by individual researchers; standard adaptation uncertain
Exam trap

Carl Jung's list (complex indicators, reaction time, emotional complexes) versus Kent-Rosanoff (frequency tables, normality versus psychosis, 1,000 normals). Both have 100 words and both date from around 1910, so examiners ask which one had norms (Kent-Rosanoff) and which one detected complexes (Jung). The originator of word association is Francis Galton (1879), not Jung. Wundt used association in the laboratory too, but the clinical method is Jung's.

DAP: Draw-A-Person (Machover)

In the Draw-A-Person, the person is given a blank sheet and a pencil and asked to "draw a person"; afterwards they are asked to draw a person of the opposite sex, and then questioned about the drawings. Karen Machover (1949, Personality Projection in the Drawing of the Human Figure) proposed the body-image hypothesis: the drawn figure represents the drawer's own body image and self-concept, so features like size, placement on the page, line pressure, erasures, omissions and the treatment of specific body parts (eyes, hands, mouth) are read as signs of personality and conflict. This is an "expression" technique.

FieldDetail
Full nameDraw-A-Person Test (Machover Figure Drawing Test)
Author(s), yearKaren Machover, 1949 (projective use). Distinct earlier and later developments: Florence Goodenough's Draw-A-Man for intelligence, 1926; Dale Harris's revision, Goodenough-Harris Drawing Test, 1963; Elizabeth Koppitz's emotional indicators, 1968; Jack Naglieri's DAP: Quantitative Scoring System, 1988, and DAP:SPED (Naglieri, McNeish and Bardos, 1991)
VersionsMachover's method has no formal editions; scored systems listed above
PublisherMachover: Charles C. Thomas (book, 1949); Koppitz: Grune and Stratton (1968)
Age rangeChildren (about 5 years) to adults; Koppitz emotional indicators for 5 to 12 years
Items1 or 2 drawings (person, then opposite-sex person) plus inquiry
Subtests / scales / indicesMachover: qualitative signs (size, placement, line quality, body parts, sequence, erasures, shading). Koppitz: 30 emotional indicators (for example poor integration, shading, gross asymmetry, tiny figure, big figure, transparencies, omission of eyes, arms or legs), presence of 2 or more taken as suggestive of emotional problems
Scoring and normsMachover: qualitative, no norms. Koppitz: count of emotional indicators. Goodenough-Harris: standard scores for intellectual maturity (mean 100, SD 15)
MeasuresMachover: self-concept, body image, conflict. Goodenough: intellectual maturity
Indian adaptationPramila Phatak's Indian norms for the Goodenough Draw-A-Man for Indian children (year uncertain, commonly cited around 1959 to 1961). For Machover projective use: none standard
Exam trap

Goodenough (1926) = Draw-A-Man = intelligence, scored by counting details. Machover (1949) = Draw-A-Person = personality projection and body image. Harris (1963) revised Goodenough, still intelligence. Koppitz (1968) = 30 emotional indicators in children's human figure drawings (Koppitz is also the developmental scorer of the Bender for children). Famous criticism: Chapman and Chapman's illusory correlation (big eyes and paranoia).

HTP: House-Tree-Person

John N. Buck (1948) asked people to draw, on separate sheets, a house, a tree and a person, on the idea that each object is familiar to everyone yet carries symbolic meaning: the house suggests home and family relationships, the tree suggests deeper, less conscious feelings about the self and life energy, and the person suggests the self-concept and interpersonal relations. After drawing, a structured post-drawing interrogation (PDI) asks the person about each drawing. Buck originally also scored the drawings for intelligence. Emanuel F. Hammer (1958, The Clinical Application of Projective Drawings) popularised and extended its clinical interpretation.

FieldDetail
Full nameHouse-Tree-Person Technique (H-T-P)
Author(s), yearJohn N. Buck, 1948; Emanuel F. Hammer (clinical extensions, 1958, and with Buck 1969); Robert Burns's Kinetic HTP (1987)
VersionsBuck's revised manual (with W. L. Warren, 1992, uncertain); Kinetic-House-Tree-Person (Burns, 1987)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services
Age rangeAbout 3 years and above
Items3 drawings in the achromatic phase (pencil), then 3 more in the chromatic phase (crayons), each followed by inquiry; PDI question count: sources differ (60 or more questions commonly cited; uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesHouse, Tree, Person drawings; achromatic and chromatic phases; qualitative signs (detail, proportion, perspective, line quality, colour use); Buck's quantitative IQ scoring (rarely used now)
Scoring and normsMainly qualitative; Buck's quantitative scoring gave an IQ estimate
MeasuresSelf-concept, family and home attitudes, inner resources, interpersonal relations; colour phase thought to tap deeper emotional reactions
Indian adaptationWidely used in Indian child guidance clinics; standard Indian norms uncertain
Exam trap

HTP is by John Buck (1948), not Hammer (Hammer extended it) and not Machover. The two phases are achromatic (pencil) first, then chromatic (crayon). "Kinetic" versions add action, see KFD.

KFD: Kinetic Family Drawing

Robert C. Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman (1970) noticed that ordinary family drawings showed stiff, lined-up figures with little information about relationships. They added movement: "Draw a picture of everyone in your family, including you, doing something." The actions, the distances between figures, who is near whom, who is omitted, and barriers between figures reveal the child's perception of family dynamics.

FieldDetail
Full nameKinetic Family Drawing (K-F-D)
Author(s), yearRobert C. Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman, 1970 (Kinetic Family Drawings); scoring manual, Actions, Styles and Symbols in K-F-D, 1972
VersionsRelated: Kinetic School Drawing (Prout and Phillips, 1974); Kinetic House-Tree-Person (Burns, 1987)
PublisherBrunner/Mazel (books)
Age rangeMainly children and adolescents (about 5 years and above); also adults
Items1 drawing plus inquiry
Subtests / scales / indicesActions (what each figure does), Styles (for example compartmentalisation, encapsulation, underlining, edging, lining at the top or bottom, folding), Symbols, figure characteristics (size, distance, omission, order)
Scoring and normsQualitative; semi-objective checklists developed later
MeasuresPerceived family relationships, closeness, conflict, isolation
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

The word "kinetic" means the family must be shown doing something; the authors are Burns and Kaufman (1970), not Buck. Compartmentalisation and encapsulation are KFD "styles" indicating emotional isolation.

Bender (projective use): Hutt Adaptation of the Bender-Gestalt Test

The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender, 1938) is primarily a visuo-motor and neuropsychological test (main entry in the neuropsychology section). Max L. Hutt, from the 1940s onward, treated the way a person copies the geometric designs as a projective, expressive sample of personality: the placement and sequence of designs on the page, use of space, size changes, line quality, and whether the person moves towards or away from the stimuli. Here it belongs to Lindzey's "expression" category.

FieldDetail
Full nameHutt Adaptation of the Bender-Gestalt Test (HABGT)
Author(s), yearOriginal test Lauretta Bender, 1938; Hutt adaptation Max L. Hutt, from 1945 (book editions 1960, 1969, 1977, 1985)
VersionsHutt 4th edition 1985; Bender-Gestalt II (Gary Brannigan and Scott Decker, 2003) is the current non-projective version
PublisherHutt: Grune and Stratton
Age rangeOlder children to adults
Items9 designs (A and 1 to 8), Hutt redrew them as a standard set
Subtests / scales / indicesPsychopathology Scale (17 factors), Adience-Abience Scale (approach versus avoidance of stimuli), configurational analysis
Scoring and normsFactor scores summed into a Psychopathology Scale; qualitative interpretation
MeasuresPersonality style and psychopathology inferred from visuo-motor behaviour
Indian adaptationThe Bender is widely used in India (see neuropsychology section); Hutt projective norms in India uncertain
Exam trap

Bender (1938) = 9 cards, visuo-motor maturation and organicity; Koppitz scored it developmentally for children; Hutt used it projectively. The test is not "Gestalt therapy"; the name comes from Gestalt perception principles (Max Wertheimer's figures).

Szondi: Szondi Test

Leopold Szondi, a Hungarian psychiatrist, published the test in 1947 (Experimentelle Triebdiagnostik) on the basis of his theory of genotropism: that latent recessive genes drive people's choices of love objects, friends, occupations and illnesses, so people are drawn to faces of people who share their hidden drive tendencies. The person is shown photographs of psychiatric patients in sets and chooses the two they like most and the two they dislike most in each set. It is the textbook example of Lindzey's "choice or ordering" category, and its theory is now regarded as unsupported.

FieldDetail
Full nameSzondi Test (Experimental Diagnostics of Drives)
Author(s), yearLeopold Szondi, 1947
Versions1947 original; English edition with Ulrich Moser and Marvin Webb (1959)
PublisherHans Huber (Bern)
Age rangeChildren to adults (uncertain lower limit)
Items48 photographs of psychiatric patients in 6 sets of 8; each set has one photo of each of 8 drive types. Ideally administered 10 times on separate days
Subtests / scales / indices8 factors (drive needs) grouped in 4 vectors: Sexual vector S (h homosexual, s sadist), Paroxysmal vector P (e epileptic, hy hysteric), Ego or Schizophrenic vector Sch (k catatonic, p paranoid), Contact vector C (d depressive, m manic)
Scoring and normsLikes (+) and dislikes (-) per factor; profiles across administrations
MeasuresClaimed: latent drive needs (genotropism)
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Szondi: 48 photos, 6 sets of 8, choose 2 liked and 2 disliked per set, 8 diagnostic categories, 4 vectors, "choice" category, theory of genotropism. Do not confuse with the Somatic Inkblot Series or with Rorschach's Swiss origin (Szondi was Hungarian, later in Zurich).

Lowenfeld: World Technique and Mosaic Test

Margaret Lowenfeld, a British paediatrician and child psychotherapist (Institute of Child Psychology, London), developed two non-verbal techniques. The World Technique (from 1929) gives the child a sand tray and a large collection of miniature objects (people, animals, houses, vehicles, trees) and asks them to make a "world"; it is the ancestor of Dora Kalff's sandplay therapy and of Charlotte Bühler's World Test. The Lowenfeld Mosaic Test (published as a book in 1954) gives the person a box of coloured geometric pieces (squares, diamonds, triangles of several kinds) in several colours and asks them to make anything they like on a tray; the pattern, its organisation, and whether it represents an object or is abstract are interpreted.

FieldDetail
Full nameLowenfeld World Technique; Lowenfeld Mosaic Test (LMT)
Author(s), yearMargaret Lowenfeld; World Technique from 1929 (book 1979, posthumous); Mosaic Test published 1954 (developed in the 1930s)
VersionsSingle versions; later research editions by others (uncertain)
PublisherUncertain (Newman Neame published the 1954 Mosaic book; uncertain)
Age rangeChildren (World); children to adults (Mosaic)
ItemsMosaic: a set of coloured pieces in 5 shapes and 6 colours (456 pieces commonly cited; uncertain). World: sand tray plus miniatures (no fixed count)
Subtests / scales / indicesNo subtests; mosaic products classified (for example representational, conceptual, pattern, incomplete, scattered)
Scoring and normsQualitative; limited norms
MeasuresPersonality organisation, cognitive style, emotional state, non-verbal expression in children
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Margaret Lowenfeld is the author of both the World Technique (sand tray) and the Mosaic Test. Victor Lowenfeld (art education, stages of children's art) is a different person. Lindzey placement differs across books (construction or expression): if the option list has only one of these, choose that.

MAPS: Make-A-Picture-Story

Edwin S. Shneidman (1947, better known later as the founder of suicidology) combined construction with play. Instead of fixed pictures, the person chooses cut-out figures, places them on background scenes (a living room, a street, a bathroom, a cave, a blank background and so on) and tells a story about the scene they have built. Because the person creates the stimulus as well as the story, choices of figures and placement add projective information beyond the TAT.

FieldDetail
Full nameMake-A-Picture-Story (MAPS)
Author(s), yearEdwin S. Shneidman, 1947 (manual 1948, sources differ on year)
VersionsSingle original version
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation
Age rangeAbout 6 years to adult
Items22 background pictures and 67 cut-out figures (commonly cited; treat as likely but verify)
Subtests / scales / indicesNone; TAT-style thematic analysis plus figure choice and placement
Scoring and normsQualitative
MeasuresNeeds, interpersonal themes, fantasy content
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

MAPS is by Shneidman (also known for "psychache" and suicidology), not Murray; the respondent builds the picture before telling the story. It is in the construction category.

Hand Test

The Hand Test, by Edward E. Wagner (1962), shows simple line drawings of hands in ambiguous positions and asks "What might this hand be doing?". The theory is that hands are the main instrument of action on the world, so the actions a person attributes to the hands reflect their action tendencies, i.e. behaviour that is close to the surface and likely to be expressed, including aggressive acting out. It is quick (about 10 minutes) and scored objectively.

FieldDetail
Full nameThe Hand Test
Author(s), yearEdward E. Wagner, 1962 (the brief named "Edwin Wagner"; standard sources give Edward E. Wagner)
VersionsRevised manual 1983; later manual revision (year uncertain)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services
Age rangeAbout 6 years to adult
Items10 cards: 9 drawings of hands plus 1 blank card (card 10), on which the person imagines a hand
Subtests / scales / indicesScoring categories grouped into: Interpersonal (affection, dependence, communication, exhibition, direction, aggression), Environmental (acquisition, active, passive), Maladjustive (tension, crippled, fear), Withdrawal (description, bizarre, failure). Summary scores include the Acting Out Ratio (AOR) and a Pathological score
Scoring and normsFrequencies and ratios with normative tables
MeasuresAction tendencies, aggression and acting-out potential, interpersonal orientation
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Two tests have a blank last-type card: TAT card 16 and Hand Test card 10. The Hand Test predicts acting out (AOR). Its author is Wagner (1962), and it is in the association category.
