6.y Lindzey classification map

Lindzey category (1959)What the person doesTests in this section
AssociationGives the first word, image or percept evokedRorschach (incl. Exner CS, R-PAS), Holtzman HIT, Somatic Inkblot Series, Word Association Test (Francis Galton, Jung, Kent-Rosanoff), Hand Test
ConstructionCreates a story or productTAT, CAT (and CAT-H, CAT-S), SAT, RATC / Roberts-2, Blacky Pictures, MAPS; Lowenfeld techniques in some books
CompletionFinishes an incomplete stimulusRotter ISB, Sacks SSCT, Rosenzweig P-F Study
Choice or orderingSelects or ranks alternativesSzondi Test (also Tomkins-Horn Picture Arrangement Test, not covered here)
ExpressionExpresses freely through a medium; style mattersDraw-A-Person, House-Tree-Person, Kinetic Family Drawing, Hutt Bender used projectively; Lowenfeld World and Mosaic in some books
Exam trap

Rapid-fire confusions to drill: (1) TAT has 31 cards and card 16 is blank; 20 is the number given per person. (2) Rorschach: 10 cards, 5 achromatic, 2 black-red, 3 chromatic. (3) Exner CS 1974, R minimum 14, versus R-PAS 2011, prompt and pull, R 18 to 28. (4) Rotter ISB (1950, 40 stems) versus Rotter I-E Scale (1966, 29 items, locus of control, objective). (5) Rosenzweig (frustration cartoons) is not Rorschach (inkblots). (6) Machover (personality) versus Goodenough (intelligence). (7) Kent-Rosanoff (norms) versus Carl Jung (complexes). (8) Indian TAT and CAT: Uma Chowdhury; Indian P-F: Udai Pareek. (9) Holtzman: 45 cards x 2 forms, one response each. (10) Szondi: 48 photographs, choice category. (11) Lindzey 1959 has five categories; sentence completion and P-F are completion, not association.

7. Neuropsychological tests and batteries

A neuropsychological test is a standardised task that samples one cognitive function (attention, memory, language, visuospatial skill, executive control, motor speed) so that the clinician can infer how well the brain systems underlying that function are working. The logic is borrowed from neurology: a lesion in a particular brain region produces a particular pattern of failures, so a carefully chosen set of tasks can reveal whether the brain is affected, which functions are impaired and spared, and how the person fails. Clinically, these tests are used in dementia, head injury, stroke, epilepsy, tumours, schizophrenia and other psychiatric disorders with cognitive deficits, ADHD and learning disorders, and for rehabilitation planning and capacity decisions.

Historical "organicity" testing. From the 1930s to the 1960s, clinical psychologists were mostly asked one crude question: "Is this patient organic (brain damaged) or functional (psychiatric)?" Single tests were used as general screens for "organicity": the Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender, 1938), the Benton Visual Retention Test (Arthur Benton, 1946) and the Memory-for-Designs Test (Frances Graham and Barbara Kendall, 1946; manual 1960). The assumption was that brain damage is a single, unitary thing that any sensitive test will detect. That assumption is now known to be wrong: brain damage varies by location, size, speed of onset and age, and a right parietal stroke and a left frontal tumour produce very different profiles. Modern neuropsychology therefore asks about specific functions, not "organicity" in general, although the old screening tests remain popular in Indian clinics and in MCQs.

Three approaches to assessment. (1) The fixed battery approach gives every patient the same, fixed set of tests, scored against norms, with summary indices. Examples are the Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery (Ward Halstead, 1947; Ralph Reitan) and the Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery (Charles Golden and colleagues, 1980). Strengths: comprehensive, good for research and for comparing patients; weaknesses: long (often 6 to 8 hours for the Halstead-Reitan) and not tailored to the referral question. (2) The flexible battery approach chooses a core set of tests and then adds tests depending on the referral question and on what the first results show (most clinicians work this way today; the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery is usually used as a flexible, domain-based set). (3) The process approach, developed by Edith Kaplan and colleagues at the Boston Veterans Administration Hospital (the "Boston Process Approach", from the 1970s and 1980s), argues that how a person reaches a score matters more than the score itself. Two patients may both score 20 on a copying task, one because of poor planning (piecemeal, fragmented copy, typical of right hemisphere or frontal damage) and the other because of missing details within an intact outline (typical of left hemisphere damage). The process approach records strategies, errors, time per item and the order of drawing. Products of this approach include the WAIS-R as a Neuropsychological Instrument (WAIS-R NI, Kaplan, Fein, Morris and Delis, 1991), the California Verbal Learning Test and the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (D-KEFS, Dean Delis, Edith Kaplan and Joel Kramer, 2001).

Luria's qualitative approach. The Russian neuropsychologist Alexander Romanovich Luria (1902 to 1977) did not use norms or total scores. He saw every complex behaviour as a functional system built from several links located in different brain areas, and he organised the brain into three functional units: (1) the unit for regulating tone and waking (brainstem and reticular formation), (2) the unit for receiving, analysing and storing information (posterior cortex: occipital, temporal, parietal), and (3) the unit for programming, regulating and verifying activity (frontal lobes). By watching exactly where a function broke down (syndrome analysis), Luria inferred which link was damaged. His methods were collected and published in English by the Danish psychologist Anne-Lise Christensen (1975) as Luria's Neuropsychological Investigation; Golden and colleagues later standardised Christensen's items into the Luria-Nebraska battery.

Domains to know. Orientation and general cognition (MMSE, MoCA, ACE-III); attention and speed (Digit Span, Trail Making A, Stroop word and colour pages, digit vigilance, cancellation); memory (WMS, PGI Memory Scale, Rey AVLT, CVLT, Rey complex figure recall, Benton Visual Retention Test); language (Boston Naming Test, Token Test, verbal fluency, BDAE, WAB); visuospatial and constructional skill (Bender-Gestalt, Rey figure copy, clock drawing, block design); executive function (WCST, Trail Making B, Stroop interference, Tower of London, verbal fluency); motor (finger tapping, grip strength, grooved pegboard); and severity or staging scales (GCS, CDR, GDS, Hachinski).

Definition

Executive functions are the higher-order control processes, mainly supported by the prefrontal cortex and its circuits, that let a person plan, start, shift, inhibit, monitor and adjust goal-directed behaviour. Classic tests: WCST (set shifting), Stroop (inhibition), Trail Making B (switching), Tower of London (planning), verbal fluency (generation and strategic retrieval).

Bender-Gestalt: Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test

The person copies simple geometric designs (dots, lines, curves, overlapping shapes) printed one per card, on a blank sheet, with no time limit and no eraser restrictions in most versions. The idea, drawn from Gestalt psychology, is that perceiving a design as an organised whole and reproducing it requires integrating visual perception with motor output (visual-motor integration); children's copies mature with age, and brain damage, intellectual disability or emotional disturbance distort the copies in characteristic ways (rotation, fragmentation, perseveration, overlapping difficulty, closure problems). Scoring systems count these errors (Pascal and Suttell for adults, Koppitz for children) or rate each drawing globally (Bender-Gestalt II). It is quick (about 10 minutes), non-verbal and culture-light, which is why it became one of the most used tests in the world and is part of the Indian PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction.

FieldDetail
Full nameBender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (BVMGT, "the Bender"); current version Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt Test, Second Edition (Bender-Gestalt II)
Author(s), yearLauretta Bender (American child neuropsychiatrist, Bellevue Hospital), 1938, monograph A Visual Motor Gestalt Test and Its Clinical Use (American Orthopsychiatric Association). The figures were adapted from those used by the Gestalt psychologist Max Wertheimer (1923) in his work on perceptual organisation. Scoring systems: Gerald Pascal and Barbara Suttell (1951, adults); Max Hutt (Hutt Adaptation of the Bender-Gestalt Test, from 1960; later editions 1969, 1977, 1985); Elizabeth Munsterberg Koppitz (Developmental Bender Scoring System, 1963; The Bender Gestalt Test for Young Children, Vol. II, 1975). Bender-Gestalt II: Gary Brannigan and Scott Decker, 2003.
VersionsBender-Gestalt II (2003, current); original Bender (1938) used with the Pascal-Suttell, Hutt and Koppitz scoring systems; Koppitz-2 (Cecil Reynolds, 2007) is a newer developmental scoring system for the original 9 designs
PublisherOriginal: American Orthopsychiatric Association; Bender-Gestalt II: Riverside Publishing (later distributed by PRO-ED; sources differ on current distributor)
Age rangeOriginal: about 4 years to adult (Koppitz norms 5 to 10 years in 1963, extended to 11 years in 1975); Bender-Gestalt II: 4 to 85+ years
ItemsOriginal: 9 cards, labelled Figure A and Figures 1 to 8. Bender-Gestalt II: 16 cards (the original 9 plus 7 new ones: 4 easier designs for young children and 3 harder designs for older children and adults)
Subtests / scales / indicesBender-Gestalt II: Copy phase and Recall phase (the person redraws as many designs as possible from memory, without warning); supplemental Motor Test and Perception Test (to separate a motor problem from a perceptual problem). Koppitz: 30 developmental scoring items (errors of distortion, rotation, integration, perseveration) plus emotional indicators (10 in 1963, 12 in 1975). Pascal-Suttell: about 100 or more scorable deviations (exact count: sources differ). Hutt: Psychopathology Scale (17 factors; count as given in Hutt's later editions, treat as uncertain)
Scoring and normsOriginal: error scores (higher = more deviation; Koppitz error scores fall with age). Bender-Gestalt II: Global Scoring System, each drawing rated 0 to 4; total converted to standard scores (mean 100, SD 15) for Copy and Recall; normed on about 4,000 people aged 4 to 85+
MeasuresVisual-motor integration (visuoconstruction); developmental maturity in children; screening for "organicity"; projective/emotional signs (Hutt, Koppitz emotional indicators, now viewed sceptically)
Indian adaptationNo single national standardisation; Indian studies have used Koppitz and Pascal-Suttell scoring, and the original 9-card Bender is a component of the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction (Pershad and Verma, PGIMER Chandigarh). Details of separate Indian norms: uncertain
Exam trap

9 vs 16 cards. Original Bender = 9 designs (A plus 1 to 8); Bender-Gestalt II = 16 designs. The figures came from Max Wertheimer (1923), not from Bender's own invention, and Bender was a psychiatrist (Lauretta, a woman). Koppitz = children (developmental scoring); Pascal and Suttell = adults; Hutt = projective/psychopathology adaptation. The original test had no recall phase; Recall, Motor and Perception were added in BG-II. Do not confuse Bender (copy, visual-motor) with Benton (memory, visual retention).

BVRT: Benton Visual Retention Test

The BVRT is a test of visual memory and visual perception in which the person looks at a card showing one to three geometric figures and then draws them from memory (or copies them). Each design typically has two large main figures and one small peripheral figure, so the test is sensitive both to memory loss and to unilateral spatial neglect (consistently missing the peripheral figure on one side). Because three equivalent forms exist, the test can be repeated (for example before and after treatment) with less practice effect. Two scores are obtained: the number correct (0 to 10) and the error score, with errors classified into six types; the expected score is adjusted for the person's estimated premorbid IQ and age.

FieldDetail
Full nameBenton Visual Retention Test (BVRT)
Author(s), yearArthur Lester Benton (American neuropsychologist, University of Iowa), 1946 (A Visual Retention Test for Clinical Use); later editions by Benton; 5th edition (1992) revised by Abigail Benton Sivan
Versions5th edition, 1992 (Sivan), current; earlier editions 1946, 1955 (revised), 1963, 1974 (4th; dates of intermediate editions: sources differ). A multiple-choice (recognition) version also exists
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation (now Pearson)
Age range8 years to adult; older-adult norms were added in later editions (exact upper limit: uncertain)
Items10 designs per form; three equivalent forms, C, D and E
Subtests / scales / indicesFour administrations: A = 10 second exposure, immediate reproduction (standard); B = 5 second exposure, immediate reproduction; C = copying with the card in view (no memory load); D = 10 second exposure, reproduction after a 15 second delay. Error types: omissions, distortions, perseverations, rotations, misplacements, size errors
Scoring and normsNumber correct (0 to 10) and total error score; expected scores given by age and estimated premorbid IQ; a score 3 or more points below expectation (or the error equivalent) is commonly read as suggesting impairment (cut-off conventions differ across editions)
MeasuresImmediate visual memory, visual perception and visuoconstruction (Administration C separates copying problems from memory problems), neglect
Indian adaptationUsed widely in Indian research and clinics with the original norms; a standard Indian norm set: uncertain
Exam trap

Benton vs Bender. Benton (1946) = visual retention (memory), 10 designs, Forms C, D, E, Administrations A to D. Bender (1938) = visual-motor copying, 9 cards. A trick option uses the letters: Forms are C, D, E; Administrations are A, B, C, D (Administration C is copy). Arthur Benton also gave us Judgment of Line Orientation, Facial Recognition and (with Kerry Hamsher) the Controlled Oral Word Association test.

HRB: Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery

The Halstead-Reitan is the classic fixed battery: a set of tests given to everyone in the same way, designed to detect brain damage and to suggest its lateralisation (left vs right) and whether it is focal or diffuse. Ward Halstead, working at the University of Chicago, studied patients with frontal lobe lesions and developed a set of tests of what he called "biological intelligence" (as distinct from psychometric IQ). His student Ralph Reitan, at Indiana University, selected the tests that best separated brain-damaged from control patients, added several, and validated the battery over decades. Interpretation uses four methods together: level of performance (cut-offs), pattern of performance across tests, pathognomonic signs (for example aphasia), and comparison of the right and left sides of the body.

FieldDetail
Full nameHalstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Test Battery (HRB, HRNB)
Author(s), yearWard C. Halstead, 1947 (Brain and Intelligence: A Quantitative Study of the Frontal Lobes); Ralph M. Reitan (from the 1950s onward), later with Deborah Wolfson (manual 1985, 2nd edition 1993)
VersionsAdult battery (15 years and older); Halstead Neuropsychological Test Battery for Older Children (9 to 14 years); Reitan-Indiana Neuropsychological Test Battery for Children (5 to 8 years)
PublisherReitan Neuropsychology Laboratory, Tucson, Arizona
Age rangeAdult version 15 years and older (children's versions as above)
ItemsVariable by component; e.g. adult Category Test 208 items in 7 subtests (Halstead's original was longer), Seashore Rhythm Test 30 pairs, Speech-Sounds Perception Test 60 nonsense words, Tactual Performance Test 10-block form board (Seguin-Goddard), Trail Making 25 circles per part
Subtests / scales / indicesCategory Test (abstraction, concept formation, learning from feedback); Tactual Performance Test (blindfolded placing of blocks with dominant hand, non-dominant hand, both hands; then draw the board from memory: Total Time, Memory, Localisation scores); Seashore Rhythm Test (same/different rhythm pairs: auditory attention); Speech-Sounds Perception Test (match spoken nonsense words to printed choices); Finger Tapping Test (motor speed, each hand); Trail Making Test A and B (added by Reitan); Reitan-Klove Sensory-Perceptual Examination (tactile, auditory, visual extinction on double simultaneous stimulation, finger recognition, fingertip writing); Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening Test; grip strength (hand dynamometer); lateral dominance examination. WAIS and MMPI are usually added as allied procedures
Scoring and normsHalstead Impairment Index = proportion of seven key scores in the impaired range (Category, TPT Total Time, TPT Memory, TPT Localisation, Seashore Rhythm, Speech-Sounds Perception, Finger Tapping), ranging 0 to 1.0; 0.5 or above suggests brain damage (0.4 is borderline in some guides). Reitan's General Neuropsychological Deficit Scale (GNDS) is a later summary score. Heaton, Grant and Matthews (1991) published demographically corrected T-score norms (mean 50, SD 10)
MeasuresPresence, lateralisation and severity of brain dysfunction; abstraction, motor, sensory, auditory, tactile and spatial functions
Indian adaptationNo standard Indian version; individual components (Finger Tapping, Trail Making, Category Test) have been used in Indian research and in the NIMHANS battery. Uncertain beyond this
Exam trap

Halstead = 1947 and the concept of "biological intelligence"; Reitan = the validator who added Trail Making. The Impairment Index cut-off is 0.5 (a proportion, not a T score). The Category Test is the single most sensitive HRB test to brain damage in general, while Finger Tapping is the classic lateralising test. Halstead-Reitan is quantitative and fixed; Alexander Luria's original approach is qualitative and flexible.

LNNB: Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery

The Luria-Nebraska is an attempt to combine Alexander Luria's rich qualitative items with American psychometric standardisation. Charles Golden and his colleagues at the University of Nebraska took the items in Anne-Lise Christensen's (1975) compilation of A. R. Luria's bedside methods, fixed the administration, and scored each item 0 (normal), 1 (borderline) or 2 (clearly abnormal). Items are grouped into clinical scales, and scale totals are converted to T scores where higher means more impairment. A "critical level", adjusted for age and education, marks the T score above which a scale is considered impaired. It takes about 2 to 3 hours, much shorter than the Halstead-Reitan, but it was criticised (for example by Spiers, 1981, and Adams, 1980) for being heavily verbal and for straying from Luria's qualitative spirit.

FieldDetail
Full nameLuria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery (LNNB)
Author(s), yearCharles J. Golden, Thomas A. Hammeke and Arnold D. Purisch; first reports 1978, manual 1980 (Form I); Form II 1985. Based on Anne-Lise Christensen (1975), Luria's Neuropsychological Investigation, which compiled the methods of A. R. Luria
VersionsForm I (1980), Form II (1985); Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery: Children's Revision (LNNB-C, Golden, 1987)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services (WPS)
Age rangeAdult forms: 15 years and older (some sources say 13 and older); LNNB-C: 8 to 12 years
ItemsForm I: 269 items; Form II: 279 items; LNNB-C: 149 items (child count: treat as uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesClinical scales (C1 to C11): Motor, Rhythm, Tactile, Visual, Receptive Speech, Expressive Speech, Writing, Reading, Arithmetic, Memory, Intellectual Processes; Form II adds C12 Intermediate Memory. Summary scales: Pathognomonic, Left Hemisphere, Right Hemisphere, Profile Elevation, Impairment; plus localisation scales (left and right frontal, sensorimotor, parietal-occipital, temporal) and factor scales
Scoring and normsItems 0, 1, 2; scale totals converted to T scores (mean 50, SD 10), higher = worse; critical level computed from age and education; two or three scales above the critical level suggests brain dysfunction
MeasuresBroad neuropsychological screening and localisation across motor, sensory, language, academic, memory and reasoning functions
Indian adaptationNone standard that is widely used; uncertain
Exam trap

Alexander Luria did not write the Luria-Nebraska. Chain: Luria (methods) to Christensen 1975 (compiled them) to Golden, Hammeke and Purisch 1978/1980 (standardised them at Nebraska). Form I = 269 items, Form II = 279 (the extra items form the Intermediate Memory scale). Unlike IQ-type scores, a high LNNB T score is bad. "Rhythm" appears in both the HRB (Seashore Rhythm) and LNNB (C2 Rhythm), so read the option carefully.

NNB: NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery (adults and children)

The NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery is the main Indian, domain-based set of neuropsychological tests, developed at the Department of Clinical Psychology, National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS), Bangalore (Bengaluru). Rather than a fixed battery with one summary index, it is a collection of individually normed tests, each tied to a specific cognitive function and, where possible, to a brain region (for example WCST and Tower of London for prefrontal functions, Rey figure for right hemisphere visual memory, AVLT for left temporal verbal memory). Norms are given by age, sex and education, which matters greatly in India where education varies widely. The clinician picks the tests relevant to the case, so in practice it functions as a flexible battery.

FieldDetail
Full nameNIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (adult); NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery for Children
Author(s), yearAdult: Shobini L. Rao, D. K. Subbakrishna and K. Gopukumar, NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery 2004 Manual. Children: Bhoomika R. Kar, Shobini L. Rao and B. A. Chandramouli (manual year usually cited as 2004; treat year as uncertain)
Versions2004 adult manual (current); test-by-test norm papers by the same group appeared from the 1990s onward (dates uncertain)
PublisherNIMHANS, Bangalore (NIMHANS Publication)
Age rangeAdult: roughly 16 to 65 years (exact bands: uncertain); children: roughly 5 to 15 years (uncertain)
ItemsNot a single item count; each test has its own items (uncertain to give one total)
Subtests / scales / indicesCommonly included (exact list in the manual: uncertain): motor speed (Finger Tapping Test); mental speed (Digit Symbol Substitution Test); attention (Colour Trails Test parts 1 and 2 for focused attention; Digit Vigilance Test for sustained attention); executive functions: phonemic fluency (COWA), category fluency (animal names), design fluency, verbal and visual working memory (N-back tasks), planning (Tower of London), set shifting (Wisconsin Card Sorting Test), response inhibition (Stroop test); comprehension (Token Test); learning and memory: Auditory Verbal Learning Test (Rey AVLT format), Complex Figure Test (Rey-Osterrieth, copy, immediate and delayed recall). Children's battery covers similar domains (motor, attention, executive, visuospatial, language, memory) with child-appropriate tasks
Scoring and normsRaw scores compared with Indian normative means and SDs or percentiles by age, sex and education; a score more than about 1.5 to 2 SD below the norm is read as a deficit (convention varies)
MeasuresDomain-specific cognitive functions with presumed localisation (frontal, temporal, parietal)
Indian adaptationIt is itself an Indian battery, normed on Indian (largely South Indian) samples
Exam trap

NIMHANS battery = Bangalore, Shobini Rao, 2004, domain-based. PGI-BBD = Chandigarh, Pershad and Verma (with Wig), composite of memory, intelligence and Bender. The NIMHANS battery uses the Colour Trails Test (a literacy-light variant of Trail Making by Louis D'Elia and colleagues, 1996), so do not assume it uses the alphabet-based Trail Making B. Children's battery is associated with Bhoomika Kar.

PGI-BBD: PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction

The PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction is the classic Indian composite battery from the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research (PGIMER), Chandigarh. Instead of new tests, it combines five existing Indian or adapted tests (memory, verbal intelligence, performance intelligence, perceptual acuity and visual-motor copying), so that the overall pattern can show whether there is brain dysfunction and how severe it is. Each component yields a "dysfunction" or impairment rating, and these are combined into a total dysfunction score. It is widely used in Indian hospitals, especially in North India, and is a favourite MCQ item.

FieldDetail
Full namePGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction (PGI-BBD)
Author(s), yearS. K. Verma, Dwarka Pershad and N. N. Wig (PGIMER, Chandigarh); handbook usually cited as Pershad and Verma (1990). Exact year of first publication: uncertain (late 1980s to 1990)
VersionsSingle version (handbook); uncertain whether later revisions exist
PublisherNational Psychological Corporation, Agra (commonly cited; treat as likely, not certain)
Age rangeAdults (roughly 20 to 70 years; uncertain)
ItemsSum of component tests; no single total item count (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesFive components: (1) PGI Memory Scale (Pershad and Wig); (2) Verbal Adult Intelligence Scale (VAIS; Pershad and Verma: information, digit span, arithmetic, comprehension); (3) Performance Adult Intelligence Scale (Bhatia's short battery of performance tests, as adapted: Koh's Block Design, Alexander's Passalong, Pattern Drawing, Immediate Memory, Picture Construction); (4) Nahor-Benson Test (perceptual acuity, drawing figures); (5) Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test
Scoring and normsEach component produces a dysfunction rating; total dysfunction score indicates presence and degree of brain dysfunction; Indian norms by age and education
MeasuresGlobal presence and severity of brain dysfunction (an "organicity" battery)
Indian adaptationIt is an Indian battery (PGIMER Chandigarh)
Exam trap

PGI-BBD = Chandigarh (PGIMER), not NIMHANS Bangalore. It contains the Bender-Gestalt and the PGI Memory Scale, and its performance part is built on Bhatia's battery (C. M. Bhatia, 1955). The perceptual test is Nahor-Benson, not Benton. "Which test is NOT part of PGI-BBD?" is a typical question: WCST, Rey AVLT and Tower of London are not.

PGIMS: PGI Memory Scale

The PGI Memory Scale is an Indian memory battery modelled loosely on the Wechsler Memory Scale but built for Indian adults, including those with limited education. Its ten subtests sample remote and recent memory, attention, immediate and delayed verbal recall, paired-associate learning, visual retention and recognition. Each subtest raw score is compared with norms, and the number and severity of low scores is summarised as a dysfunction rating rather than a single memory quotient. It is quick (about 30 to 40 minutes) and is one of the most commonly used memory tests in Indian clinical practice.

FieldDetail
Full namePGI Memory Scale (PGIMS)
Author(s), yearDwarka Pershad and N. N. Wig, 1977 (PGIMER, Chandigarh)
VersionsAdult version 1977 (current); a children's version (PGI Memory Scale for Children) exists, authors and year uncertain
PublisherNational Psychological Corporation, Agra (commonly cited; uncertain)
Age rangeAdults; norms usually cited for about 20 to 45 years with additional bands for older adults (uncertain)
Items10 subtests; total item count uncertain
Subtests / scales / indices(1) Remote memory; (2) Recent memory; (3) Mental balance (counting backwards, alphabet, serial tasks); (4) Attention and concentration (digits forward and backward); (5) Delayed recall (of a word list); (6) Immediate recall (of sentences); (7) Verbal retention for similar pairs; (8) Verbal retention for dissimilar pairs; (9) Visual retention (drawing designs from memory); (10) Recognition (of previously seen objects or pictures)
Scoring and normsRaw scores per subtest compared with norms by age and education; dysfunction rating summarises impairment
MeasuresVerbal and visual memory, attention, remote and recent memory in adults
Indian adaptationIndian test (PGIMER Chandigarh)
Exam trap

PGIMS has 10 subtests, year 1977, authors Pershad and Wig. The two verbal retention subtests are similar pairs and dissimilar pairs (paired-associate learning, like the WMS Associate Learning). "Mental balance" is an attention and mental control task, not an emotional measure.

WMS: Wechsler Memory Scale

The Wechsler Memory Scale is the most widely used memory battery in the world. It assesses memory the way the Wechsler intelligence scales assess intelligence: several subtests, each converted to scaled scores (mean 10, SD 3), combined into index scores (mean 100, SD 15). Modern editions deliberately separate auditory (verbal) from visual memory, and immediate from delayed recall, because different brain lesions affect these differently (left temporal damage tends to hit verbal memory, right temporal damage visual memory). Because it is co-normed with the WAIS, the clinician can directly compare a person's memory with their general ability, which is how a disproportionate memory deficit (as in amnesia or early Alzheimer's disease) is detected.

FieldDetail
Full nameWechsler Memory Scale (WMS); current edition Wechsler Memory Scale, Fourth Edition (WMS-IV)
Author(s), yearDavid Wechsler, 1945 (Form I); Form II by C. P. Stone, G. Girdner and R. Albrecht, 1946; WMS-R (Wechsler, 1987); WMS-III (Wechsler, 1997); WMS-IV (Wechsler, 2009, development team at Pearson)
VersionsWMS-IV 2009 (current as far as the sources used here show); WMS-III 1997; WMS-R 1987; WMS 1945
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation, now Pearson
Age rangeWMS-IV: Adult battery 16 to 69 years; Older Adult battery 65 to 90 years (overlap at 65 to 69). Original WMS: 20 to 64 years
ItemsVaries by subtest (WMS-IV total item count: uncertain). Original WMS had 7 subtests
Subtests / scales / indicesWMS-IV Adult battery: Logical Memory I and II (story recall), Verbal Paired Associates I and II, Designs I and II (visual-spatial memory for designs in a grid), Visual Reproduction I and II (drawing designs from memory), Spatial Addition (visual working memory), Symbol Span (visual working memory), optional Brief Cognitive Status Exam (BCSE). Older Adult battery drops Designs and Spatial Addition. Indexes: Auditory Memory (AMI), Visual Memory (VMI), Visual Working Memory (VWMI; adult battery only), Immediate Memory (IMI), Delayed Memory (DMI). Original 1945 WMS subtests: Personal and Current Information, Orientation, Mental Control, Logical Memory, Digits Forward and Backward, Visual Reproduction, Associate Learning
Scoring and normsSubtest scaled scores mean 10, SD 3; index scores mean 100, SD 15; original WMS gave a single Memory Quotient (MQ); WMS-IV co-normed with WAIS-IV (US sample of about 1,400)
MeasuresEpisodic memory (verbal and visual, immediate and delayed), visual working memory, recognition
Indian adaptationNo standard Indian WMS-IV norms that are widely used (uncertain); in India the PGI Memory Scale and NIMHANS battery memory tests are more common
Exam trap

WMS-IV has two batteries: Adult (16 to 69) and Older Adult (65 to 90). The Older Adult battery has no Visual Working Memory Index. WMS-IV dropped the Working Memory index of WMS-III (auditory working memory moved to the WAIS-IV) and added Designs, Spatial Addition and Symbol Span. Only the original WMS (1945) gave a single MQ. Year sequence: 1945, 1987, 1997, 2009.

RAVLT: Rey Auditory Verbal Learning Test

The AVLT measures how well a person learns and remembers a list of unrelated words over repeated trials. The examiner reads 15 common nouns (List A) and the person recalls as many as possible, five times in a row; this produces a learning curve. Then an interference list (List B) is read once and recalled, after which the person tries List A again without hearing it (sensitive to interference and forgetting). After a 20 to 30 minute delay there is another free recall and usually a recognition trial. Patterns are informative: poor learning with poor recognition suggests a storage (hippocampal) problem, while poor free recall with good recognition suggests a retrieval (frontal-subcortical) problem.

FieldDetail
Full nameRey Auditory Verbal Learning Test (RAVLT, AVLT)
Author(s), yearAndré Rey (Swiss psychologist, Geneva), 1941; expanded in L'examen clinique en psychologie (1964); English handbook and meta-norms by Michael Schmidt (1996)
VersionsMany alternate word lists and translations; WHO-UCLA AVLT (culture-fair version, Maj et al., 1993); Schmidt (1996) handbook
PublisherSchmidt handbook: Western Psychological Services; the list itself is widely available in the literature
Age rangeChildren to older adults (Schmidt norms roughly 7 to 89 years; uncertain)
ItemsList A: 15 words; List B: 15 words; recognition list (commonly 50 words including the 30 list words; formats differ)
Subtests / scales / indicesTrials I to V (List A), List B trial, Trial VI (post-interference recall of A), Trial VII (delayed recall, 20 to 30 min), recognition. Indices: total learning (sum of I to V), learning slope, proactive interference, retroactive interference, forgetting, recognition hits and false positives, serial position effects
Scoring and normsNumber of words recalled per trial; compared with age (and education) norms; typical healthy adults recall about 6 to 7 on Trial I rising to about 12 to 13 on Trial V (approximate)
MeasuresVerbal learning, immediate span, interference, delayed verbal memory, recognition
Indian adaptationAdapted (Indian word lists in English, Kannada, Hindi and others) as part of the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (Rao and colleagues); other Indian versions exist (details uncertain)
Exam trap

15 words, 5 trials, unrelated nouns (Rey AVLT) vs 16 words in 4 semantic categories (CVLT). Recall of List A after List B tests retroactive interference; poor List B recall because of List A is proactive interference. André Rey is also the Rey of the complex figure.

CVLT: California Verbal Learning Test

The CVLT is a list-learning test built on the process approach. Its list looks like a shopping list: 16 words from 4 semantic categories (for example fruits, tools, clothing, spices), presented in mixed order. The examiner can therefore see whether the person spontaneously groups words by category (semantic clustering, an active, frontal-dependent strategy) or just recalls them in the order heard (serial clustering, a more passive strategy). It also measures cued recall (giving the category name), recognition, intrusions and repetitions, so it gives a detailed picture of why memory fails.

FieldDetail
Full nameCalifornia Verbal Learning Test (CVLT); current CVLT-3
Author(s), yearDean C. Delis, Joel H. Kramer, Edith Kaplan and Beth A. Ober, 1987; CVLT-II 2000; CVLT-3 2017 (Delis, Kramer, Kaplan, Ober)
VersionsCVLT-3 (2017, current; includes standard, alternate and brief forms); CVLT-II (2000); CVLT (1987); CVLT-C for children (1994)
PublisherThe Psychological Corporation, now Pearson
Age rangeCVLT-3: 16 to 90 years; CVLT-C: 5 to 16 years
Items16 words (4 categories x 4 words) in List A; interference List B of 16 words; CVLT-3 brief form 9 words (3 categories; uncertain); CVLT-C 15 words in 3 categories
Subtests / scales / indicesTrials 1 to 5, List B, short-delay free recall, short-delay cued recall, long-delay (20 min) free recall, long-delay cued recall, yes/no recognition, optional forced-choice recognition. Process scores: semantic and serial clustering, primacy/recency, learning slope, consistency, intrusions, repetitions, discriminability
Scoring and normsTotal Trials 1 to 5 as T score or standard score; other measures as z scores or scaled scores (format differs by edition)
MeasuresVerbal learning and memory, learning strategy, retrieval vs storage deficits
Indian adaptationNone standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

CVLT words are semantically related (shopping list), which is precisely what lets it measure semantic clustering; Rey AVLT words are unrelated. Edith Kaplan is a co-author, which links CVLT to the process approach.

ROCF: Rey-Osterrieth Complex Figure Test

The person first copies a complex, abstract line drawing (a large rectangle with diagonals, a cross, triangles, circles and other details), then draws it from memory, without warning, after a short delay and again after about 30 minutes. The copy shows visuoconstructional ability and planning or organisation (does the person start with the big rectangle and fit details in, or build the figure piece by piece?), while the recall trials measure visual memory. Because it is hard to verbalise, it is often treated as a test of right hemisphere (non-verbal) memory, and the organisation of the copy is sensitive to executive dysfunction.

FieldDetail
Full nameRey-Osterrieth Complex Figure Test (ROCF, RCFT, "Rey figure")
Author(s), yearAndré Rey, 1941 (devised the figure); Paul-Alexandre Osterrieth, 1944 (standardised administration and scoring, norms for children and adults); John Meyers and Kelly Meyers, 1995 (Rey Complex Figure Test and Recognition Trial, RCFT)
VersionsStandard Rey-Osterrieth figure; Meyers and Meyers RCFT (1995) with recognition trial; alternate figures for retesting (Taylor figure, Lezak 1983; Medical College of Georgia figures); Boston Qualitative Scoring System (Stern et al., 1999)
PublisherMeyers RCFT: Psychological Assessment Resources (PAR); original figure widely available
Age rangeAbout 6 years to older adults (RCFT norms 6 to 89 years; uncertain for other systems)
ItemsOne figure; scored in 18 units
Subtests / scales / indicesCopy, immediate recall (about 3 minutes; some protocols use 30 seconds), delayed recall (about 30 minutes), recognition (RCFT)
Scoring and normsEach of the 18 units scored 2 (correct and well placed), 1 (correct but misplaced, or distorted but well placed), 0.5 (distorted and misplaced but recognisable) or 0; maximum 36. Also copy time and qualitative organisation scores
MeasuresVisuoconstruction, planning and organisation, non-verbal (visual) memory
Indian adaptationIncluded as the Complex Figure Test in the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery with Indian norms (Rao and colleagues)
Exam trap

18 units x 2 points = 36. Rey created it (1941), Osterrieth standardised it (1944). It tests both copy (construction, planning) and memory; a poor copy makes poor recall uninterpretable as a pure memory deficit.

WCST: Wisconsin Card Sorting Test

The WCST is the classic test of set shifting and abstract reasoning. Four stimulus cards are placed in front of the person (one red triangle, two green stars, three yellow crosses, four blue circles), and the person must match each response card to one of them. The examiner does not say what the rule is, only "right" or "wrong" after each card. The hidden rule is colour first, then form, then number, and after 10 consecutive correct matches the examiner silently changes the rule. A healthy person notices the "wrong" feedback and shifts; a patient with dorsolateral prefrontal dysfunction tends to keep sorting by the old rule (perseveration). Brenda Milner (1963) showed this deficit in patients with frontal lobe excisions, which made the WCST the iconic "frontal lobe" test, although damage elsewhere can also impair it.

FieldDetail
Full nameWisconsin Card Sorting Test (WCST)
Author(s), yearDavid A. Grant and Esta A. Berg, 1948 (University of Wisconsin); clinical manual by Robert K. Heaton, 1981; revised and expanded manual by Heaton, Chelune, Talley, Kay and Curtiss, 1993; WCST-64 by Kongs, Thompson, Iverson and Heaton, 2000
VersionsWCST 128-card standard (1993 manual, current); WCST-64 (2000, short form); computerised versions; Modified WCST (Nelson, 1976, 48 cards; removes ambiguous cards)
PublisherPsychological Assessment Resources (PAR)
Age range6.5 to 89 years (1993 norms)
Items4 stimulus cards and 128 response cards (two decks of 64); WCST-64 uses one deck of 64
Subtests / scales / indicesCategories completed (maximum 6), total errors, perseverative responses, perseverative errors, non-perseverative errors, conceptual level responses, trials to complete first category, failure to maintain set, learning to learn
Scoring and normsRaw scores converted to standard scores (mean 100, SD 15), T scores and percentiles, corrected for age and education; perseverative errors are the most clinically used index
MeasuresSet shifting (cognitive flexibility), abstraction, concept formation, use of feedback; executive function (dorsolateral prefrontal)
Indian adaptationIncluded in the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery with Indian norms (Rao and colleagues); widely used in Indian schizophrenia research
Exam trap

WCST = set shifting / cognitive flexibility, the hallmark error is perseveration, linked to the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex. Sort order: colour, form, number (C-F-N); shift after 10 correct; 128 cards, 6 categories. It is not a memory test and not a planning test (planning = Tower of London). Grant and Berg (1948), not Heaton (manual) or Milner (frontal lesion study).

TMT: Trail Making Test

The Trail Making Test is a paper-and-pencil task in which the person draws lines to connect circles in order as fast as possible. Part A uses numbers only (1 to 25), so it mainly measures visual scanning, attention and psychomotor speed. Part B alternates numbers and letters (1, A, 2, B, 3, C and so on), so the person must keep two sequences in mind and switch between them, which adds working memory and set shifting (an executive demand). Errors are pointed out immediately and the time keeps running, so the score is completion time in seconds. Comparing B with A (B minus A, or B/A ratio) removes the effect of simple speed and isolates the switching cost.

FieldDetail
Full nameTrail Making Test (TMT), Parts A and B
Author(s), yearOriginally part of the Army Individual Test Battery (1944), US Army; derived from earlier "Partington's Pathways" (Partington and Leiter, 1949, report; dating of the link: sources differ); incorporated into the Halstead-Reitan Battery by Ralph Reitan (validated 1955, 1958)
VersionsAdult TMT; Intermediate (children's) version in the Halstead battery for older children; Colour Trails Test (D'Elia, Satz, Uchiyama and White, 1996), a literacy-free version using coloured circles; D-KEFS Trail Making (2001); Comprehensive Trail Making Test (Reynolds, 2002)
PublisherPublic domain in the original form; Reitan Neuropsychology Laboratory version; Colour Trails: PAR
Age rangeAdult version about 15 years and older; children's versions younger
ItemsPart A: 25 circles numbered 1 to 25; Part B: 25 circles, numbers 1 to 13 and letters A to L, alternated; each part preceded by a short practice sample
Subtests / scales / indicesPart A time, Part B time, errors, B minus A difference, B/A ratio
Scoring and normsTime in seconds (higher = worse), norms corrected for age and education (e.g. Tombaugh, 2004); a B/A ratio above about 3 suggests a disproportionate switching deficit (convention, not universal)
MeasuresA: visual search, attention, processing speed. B: cognitive flexibility, set shifting, divided attention, working memory
Indian adaptationBecause letters vary across Indian scripts, the Colour Trails Test is used in the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (Indian norms); Indian TMT norms also published in research (details uncertain)
Exam trap

Trail B = executive (switching), Trail A = speed/attention. Part B stops at 13 and L (13 numbers + 12 letters = 25 circles). The test originated in the Army Individual Test Battery (1944), not with Reitan; Reitan adopted and validated it.

Stroop: Stroop Colour and Word Test

In the Stroop effect, people are slower and make more errors when naming the ink colour of a colour word printed in a conflicting colour (the word RED printed in blue ink; correct answer "blue"). Reading is so automatic that the word meaning intrudes, and the person must actively suppress it, so the test measures response inhibition and selective attention (interference control). Clinical versions have a word-reading page, a colour-naming page and the incongruent colour-word page; the interference score shows how much slower the person is on the conflict condition than expected from their basic speed. The anterior cingulate and lateral prefrontal cortex are the brain regions most associated with the task.

FieldDetail
Full nameStroop Colour and Word Test (Stroop test)
Author(s), yearJohn Ridley Stroop, 1935 (Journal of Experimental Psychology, "Studies of interference in serial verbal reactions"); clinical version by Charles J. Golden, 1978; revised Golden and Shawna Freshwater, 2002; other versions: Victoria Stroop (Regard, 1981), Comalli-Kaplan version, D-KEFS Colour-Word Interference (2001)
VersionsGolden Stroop Colour and Word Test (1978; adult manual revision 2002; children's version 2003); Victoria Stroop; computerised forms
PublisherGolden version: Stoelting (later PAR for the 2002 edition; sources differ)
Age rangeGolden adult version 15 to 90 years (approximately); children's version 5 to 14 years
ItemsGolden: 3 pages, each 100 items (5 columns of 20); each page timed for 45 seconds. Victoria Stroop: 3 cards of 24 items
Subtests / scales / indicesWord page (W: colour names in black ink), Colour page (C: XXXX in coloured ink), Colour-Word page (CW: incongruent); Interference score = CW minus predicted CW, where predicted CW = (W x C) / (W + C)
Scoring and normsItems completed in 45 s per page, converted to T scores (mean 50, SD 10) with age and education corrections
MeasuresResponse inhibition, selective attention, interference control, processing speed
Indian adaptationA Stroop task is part of the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (Indian norms; format details uncertain)
Exam trap

Stroop = inhibition (suppressing an automatic response), not set shifting (WCST) and not planning (Tower of London). J. Ridley Stroop, 1935. Golden version: 3 pages x 100 items, 45 seconds each. The "emotional Stroop" (threat words in anxiety) is a research variant, not the clinical neuropsychological test.

TOL: Tower of London

The Tower of London is a planning task: the person must rearrange coloured balls on pegs to match a goal picture in the minimum number of moves, thinking the moves through before acting. The standard apparatus has three pegs of unequal length that hold 1, 2 and 3 balls respectively, and three balls (red, green, blue). Problems need from 2 to 5 moves, and the harder ones require the person to make a move that seems to go away from the goal (a subgoal), which is what taxes planning. Tim Shallice designed it to study patients with frontal lobe lesions, who make more moves, break rules and plan less before starting.

FieldDetail
Full nameTower of London (TOL)
Author(s), yearTim Shallice, 1982 (Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London B, "Specific impairments of planning"); TOL-DX by William Culbertson and Eric Zillmer (2001; 2nd edition 2005)
VersionsOriginal Shallice (12 problems; count as reported, treat as uncertain); TOL-DX (adult and child forms); Cambridge Neuropsychological Test Automated Battery (CANTAB) Stockings of Cambridge (computerised variant)
PublisherTOL-DX: Multi-Health Systems (MHS)
Age rangeTOL-DX: 7 to 15 years (child) and 20 to 80 years (adult) (uncertain)
ItemsShallice: 12 problems; TOL-DX: 10 problems (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesNumber of problems solved in minimum moves, total moves, initiation (planning) time, execution time, rule violations, time violations
Scoring and normsStandard scores (TOL-DX); NIMHANS version uses Indian norms for problems solved in minimum moves and times
MeasuresPlanning, problem solving, goal-subgoal organisation (prefrontal, particularly left anterior frontal in Shallice's data)
Indian adaptationPart of the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (Rao and colleagues)
Exam trap

Tower of London (Shallice 1982): unequal pegs, same-size coloured balls, planning. Tower of Hanoi: equal pegs, discs of graded sizes, a bigger disc may never go on a smaller one. Both measure planning, but the Tower of Hanoi is also used to show preserved procedural learning in amnesia.

TOH: Tower of Hanoi

The Tower of Hanoi is a mathematical puzzle turned into a neuropsychological test. Discs of decreasing size are stacked on the first of three equal pegs, and the person must move the whole stack to another peg, moving one disc at a time and never placing a larger disc on a smaller one. The minimum number of moves for n discs is 2n minus 1 (7 moves for 3 discs, 15 for 4). It measures planning and problem solving, and because amnesic patients improve across sessions without remembering having done it, it is also a classic demonstration of intact procedural (implicit) memory.

FieldDetail
Full nameTower of Hanoi (TOH)
Author(s), yearPuzzle invented by the French mathematician Édouard Lucas, 1883; adapted for psychological research by many investigators (e.g. Simon, 1975, problem-solving research); no single clinical author
Versions3-disc, 4-disc and 5-disc versions; Tower of Toronto (Saint-Cyr and colleagues, 1988, uses colours); computerised versions
PublisherNo standard clinical publisher (uncertain)
Age rangeChildren to older adults (no single normative range)
ItemsNumber of discs defines difficulty; minimum moves 2n minus 1
Subtests / scales / indicesMoves made, time, rule violations, trials to criterion
Scoring and normsMoves above minimum and time; norms vary by study
MeasuresPlanning, problem solving, procedural learning
Indian adaptationNone standard
Exam trap

Minimum moves = 2n minus 1. The Tower of Hanoi is a puzzle (Lucas, 1883), not a test authored by Shallice. Amnesic patients (e.g. H.M.-type cases) learn it without conscious recollection: procedural memory spared.

COWA / FAS: Controlled Oral Word Association (verbal fluency)

Verbal fluency tests ask the person to say as many words as possible in 60 seconds that begin with a given letter (phonemic or letter fluency, e.g. F, A, S), excluding proper nouns, numbers and the same word with different endings, or that belong to a category (semantic or category fluency, e.g. animals). Letter fluency demands a strategic, self-generated search and is especially sensitive to left frontal damage; category fluency leans more on the semantic store in the temporal lobes and drops early in Alzheimer's disease. The score is the number of correct words, and perseverations and rule breaks are noted.

FieldDetail
Full nameControlled Oral Word Association Test (COWA, COWAT); "FAS test"
Author(s), yearArthur L. Benton and Kerry deS. Hamsher, as part of the Multilingual Aphasia Examination (1976 first edition; 1978 and 1989 revisions; dates: sources differ). Letter fluency tasks go back to Thurstone's Word Fluency (1938); the F-A-S letters are associated with Benton's earlier work (1960s)
VersionsLetter sets F-A-S and C-F-L (equivalent forms); category fluency (animals); D-KEFS Verbal Fluency (2001)
PublisherMultilingual Aphasia Examination: AJA Associates (Iowa); FAS widely used in public domain form
Age rangeChildren (about 6 years) to older adults; MAE adult norms 16 to 69 (uncertain)
Items3 letters x 60 seconds each; category fluency usually 1 category x 60 s
Subtests / scales / indicesTotal correct words (sum of 3 letters), perseverations, rule violations; clustering and switching (Troyer, 1997) as process scores
Scoring and normsRaw total adjusted for age, sex and education; percentiles
MeasuresExecutive word generation, lexical retrieval, semantic memory (category version)
Indian adaptationIndian versions use language-appropriate letters (Kannada, Hindi and other scripts); the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery includes phonemic (COWA) and category (animal names) fluency with Indian norms
Exam trap

Letter fluency = frontal (executive); category fluency = temporal (semantic), impaired early in Alzheimer's disease. COWA belongs to Benton (Multilingual Aphasia Examination), not to the Boston group.

Digit Span

The examiner reads strings of digits at a rate of one per second, and the person repeats them; the strings grow by one digit until the person fails both trials of a length. In Digits Forward the person repeats in the same order (simple auditory attention and short-term span; normal adult span about 7, plus or minus 2, after George Miller, 1956). In Digits Backward the person reverses the order, which requires holding and manipulating information (working memory; normal adult span about 5). WAIS-IV added Digit Span Sequencing (repeat in ascending numerical order). It appears in the WAIS, WISC, original WMS, PGI Memory Scale and many screening tools.

FieldDetail
Full nameDigit Span (forward, backward, sequencing)
Author(s), yearJoseph Jacobs, 1887 (first systematic memory-span study); included by Binet and Simon (1905), and by David Wechsler in the Wechsler-Bellevue (1939) and later scales
VersionsWAIS-IV / WAIS-5 Digit Span; WISC-V Digit Span; Digit span in WMS-R/WMS-III; many stand-alone versions
PublisherPearson (Wechsler versions); others public domain
Age rangeChildren to older adults (depending on host battery)
ItemsDepends on version (WAIS-IV: 8 items x 2 trials per task; uncertain for other editions)
Subtests / scales / indicesForward, Backward, Sequencing (WAIS-IV); longest span and total raw score
Scoring and normsWithin Wechsler scales: scaled score mean 10, SD 3; span lengths as raw indicators
MeasuresForward: attention and auditory span. Backward and sequencing: working memory
Indian adaptationIncluded in Indian adaptations (e.g. PGI Memory Scale, VAIS, Indian Wechsler adaptations such as Malin's WISC)
Exam trap

Forward = attention / span, backward = working memory (central executive). Digit span is typically preserved in pure amnesia (short-term store intact) even when long-term memory is severely impaired.

BNT: Boston Naming Test

The Boston Naming Test is a confrontation naming test: the person sees black-and-white line drawings, from very common objects (bed, tree) to rare ones (abacus, trellis), and must name each one. If the person misperceives the picture, a semantic (stimulus) cue is given; if they still cannot name it, a phonemic cue (the first sound) is given. Naming difficulty (anomia) is present in almost all aphasias and in Alzheimer's disease, and whether the phonemic cue helps can separate retrieval failure from loss of the word's meaning.

FieldDetail
Full nameBoston Naming Test (BNT)
Author(s), yearEdith Kaplan, Harold Goodglass and Sandra Weintraub, 1983 (experimental 85-item version 1978); second edition 2001 (Kaplan, Goodglass, Weintraub)
VersionsBNT-2 (2001, current) includes the 60-item test, a 15-item short form and a multiple-choice version; 1983 edition; many short forms (e.g. 30-item)
PublisherLea and Febiger (1983); Lippincott Williams and Wilkins (2001); later PRO-ED (sources differ)
Age rangeAbout 5 years to older adults (child and adult norms; uncertain bounds)
Items60 line drawings, graded in difficulty (experimental 1978 version: 85)
Subtests / scales / indicesSpontaneous correct, correct after semantic cue, correct after phonemic cue, error types (semantic and phonemic paraphasias, perceptual errors)
Scoring and normsNumber correct spontaneously plus after semantic cue (maximum 60); age and education norms
MeasuresConfrontation naming, word retrieval (anomia)
Indian adaptationSeveral pictures are culturally unfamiliar in India (e.g. beaver, pretzel); Indian-language adaptations and culture-appropriate naming tests exist in research (standard version: uncertain)
Exam trap

BNT = 60 items, 1983, Kaplan, Goodglass and Weintraub (the Boston group, same people as the BDAE). It is a naming test, not a comprehension test (comprehension = Token Test).

Token Test

The Token Test measures auditory comprehension of spoken commands that grow in length and grammatical complexity, using plastic tokens that differ in shape (circles and squares), colour (five colours) and size (large and small). Commands range from "Touch the red circle" to "Put the red circle on the green square" and "Before touching the yellow circle, pick up the red square". Because the vocabulary is tiny and concrete, failure reflects difficulty processing language structure and verbal working memory, not vocabulary, so it is very sensitive to mild receptive language problems in aphasia.

FieldDetail
Full nameToken Test
Author(s), yearEnnio De Renzi and Luigi A. Vignolo, 1962 (Brain); short version by De Renzi and Faglioni, 1978
VersionsOriginal (1962); Short Token Test (De Renzi and Faglioni, 1978); Token Test in the Multilingual Aphasia Examination (Benton and Hamsher); Token Test for Children (DiSimoni, 1978; 2nd edition McGhee and colleagues, 2007)
PublisherOriginal in journal; versions in various batteries
Age rangeAdults; children's version about 3 to 12 years (uncertain)
Items20 tokens (2 shapes x 5 colours x 2 sizes) in the full version; number of commands: original commonly cited as 62 in 5 parts, short version 36 (treat both as uncertain; counts differ across versions)
Subtests / scales / indicesGraded parts of increasing complexity (Part VI of the long form has syntactically complex commands)
Scoring and normsNumber of commands correctly executed; cut-offs for aphasia vary by version
MeasuresAuditory (receptive) language comprehension, verbal working memory
Indian adaptationA Token Test is included in the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (comprehension); other Indian-language versions exist (details uncertain)
Exam trap

Token Test = comprehension (receptive language), authors De Renzi and Vignolo (1962). Do not confuse with the Boston Naming Test (naming) or token economy (behaviour therapy).

BDAE and WAB: Boston Diagnostic Aphasia Examination and Western Aphasia Battery

These are comprehensive aphasia batteries used by speech-language pathologists and neuropsychologists to describe a patient's language profile and classify the aphasia syndrome. Both assess spontaneous speech (fluency, grammar, paraphasias), auditory comprehension, repetition, naming, reading and writing. The combination of fluency, comprehension and repetition maps onto classic syndromes: Broca's (non-fluent, comprehension relatively spared, repetition poor), Wernicke's (fluent, comprehension poor, repetition poor), conduction (fluent, comprehension good, repetition poor), transcortical types (repetition preserved), global and anomic aphasia.

FieldDetail
Full nameBoston Diagnostic Aphasia Examination (BDAE); Western Aphasia Battery (WAB)
Author(s), yearBDAE: Harold Goodglass and Edith Kaplan, 1972; 2nd edition 1983; 3rd edition 2000 (Goodglass, Kaplan and Barbara Barresi). WAB: Andrew Kertesz, 1982 (University of Western Ontario, Canada); WAB-Revised 2006
VersionsBDAE-3 (2000; standard, short and extended forms); WAB-R (2006), earlier WAB (1982)
PublisherBDAE-3: Lippincott Williams and Wilkins (later PRO-ED; sources differ); WAB-R: Pearson (Psychological Corporation)
Age rangeAdults (WAB-R 18 to 89; uncertain); BDAE adults
ItemsMany subtests; total item counts uncertain
Subtests / scales / indicesBDAE: conversational and expository speech, auditory comprehension, oral expression, reading, writing, praxis; Aphasia Severity Rating Scale (0 to 5). WAB: spontaneous speech (information content, fluency), auditory verbal comprehension, repetition, naming and word finding (yield the Aphasia Quotient, AQ, maximum 100), plus reading, writing, apraxia, constructional and calculation tasks (with AQ give the Cortical Quotient); WAB-R adds a Language Quotient and a Bedside WAB-R
Scoring and normsBDAE: percentiles and severity rating; WAB: AQ below 93.8 classified as aphasic; taxonomy from fluency, comprehension, repetition and naming scores
MeasuresPresence, type and severity of aphasia
Indian adaptationWAB has been adapted into several Indian languages in speech and hearing institutes (e.g. Kannada, Malayalam, Hindi versions; details uncertain)
Exam trap

BDAE = Goodglass and Kaplan (Boston, 1972); WAB = Kertesz (1982, Canada). The WAB produces the Aphasia Quotient; the BDAE has a 0 to 5 severity rating. Repetition is the feature that separates transcortical aphasias (preserved) from Broca's, Wernicke's and conduction aphasias (impaired).

MMSE: Mini-Mental State Examination

The MMSE is a 5 to 10 minute bedside screening test of global cognition, the most widely used cognitive screen in the world. It covers orientation, registration and recall of three words, attention (serial sevens or spelling WORLD backwards), naming, repetition, a three-stage command, reading, writing a sentence and copying intersecting pentagons. It was designed to be quick and to cover "only the cognitive aspects of mental functions", hence "mini". It is strongly affected by education and language and has a ceiling effect, so it misses mild cognitive impairment and is insensitive to executive deficits.

FieldDetail
Full nameMini-Mental State Examination (MMSE, "Folstein test")
Author(s), yearMarshal F. Folstein, Susan E. Folstein and Paul R. McHugh, 1975 (Journal of Psychiatric Research); MMSE-2 by Folstein, Folstein, White and Messer, 2010
VersionsMMSE-2 (2010; Brief 16 points, Standard 30 points, Expanded 90 points; Blue and Red alternate forms); original MMSE (1975); Hindi Mental State Examination (HMSE, Ganguli et al., 1995)
PublisherPsychological Assessment Resources (PAR) holds the copyright since 2001
Age rangeAdults (MMSE-2 norms 18 years and older)
Items30 points (about 11 items or groups of questions; 30 scored points)
Subtests / scales / indicesOrientation to time (5), orientation to place (5), registration (3), attention and calculation (5), recall (3), naming (2), repetition (1), three-stage command (3), reading (1), writing (1), copying (1)
Scoring and normsTotal 0 to 30; commonly 24 or above normal, 23 or below suggests impairment (cut-off 23/24); rough severity bands: 21 to 24 mild, 10 to 20 moderate, below 10 severe (conventions differ); education-adjusted cut-offs recommended
MeasuresGlobal cognition, dementia screening and tracking
Indian adaptationHindi Mental State Examination (HMSE): Mary Ganguli and colleagues, 1995, developed in the Indo-US Cross-National Dementia Epidemiology Study at Ballabgarh (Haryana) for rural, largely illiterate Hindi-speaking elders (items that need literacy were replaced); other Indian-language MMSE versions exist (e.g. Bengali, Kannada; details uncertain)
Exam trap

MMSE: Folstein, Folstein and McHugh 1975, 30 points, cut-off 23/24. It has no clock drawing (that is MoCA) and only one visuospatial item (intersecting pentagons). It is less sensitive to MCI than the MoCA. HMSE = Ganguli 1995, for illiterate rural Indians.

MoCA: Montreal Cognitive Assessment

The MoCA is a 10-minute, one-page, 30-point screening test designed specifically to detect mild cognitive impairment (MCI), which the MMSE often misses. It adds harder memory demands (5 words, delayed recall) and executive and visuospatial tasks (a mini trail-making task, cube copy, clock drawing, verbal fluency, abstraction). In the original validation, the MoCA detected about 90% of MCI cases compared with about 18% for the MMSE at standard cut-offs. Free to use clinically, though training and certification are now required for official use.

FieldDetail
Full nameMontreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA)
Author(s), yearZiad S. Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005 (Journal of the American Geriatrics Society); developed in Montreal, Canada
VersionsMoCA 7.1 (original form) and alternate forms 7.2, 7.3; MoCA 8.x versions; MoCA-Blind, MoCA Basic (for low education/illiteracy), 5-minute MoCA; many translations
PublisherMoCA Test Inc. (Nasreddine)
Age rangeAdults, typically 55 to 85 years in validation (uncertain bounds; used across adulthood)
Items30 points
Subtests / scales / indicesVisuospatial/executive (trail-making 1, cube copy 1, clock 3) = 5; naming (lion, rhinoceros, camel) = 3; attention (digits forward and backward 2, letter tapping 1, serial sevens 3) = 6; language (sentence repetition 2, letter F fluency 1) = 3; abstraction = 2; delayed recall of 5 words = 5; orientation = 6
Scoring and normsTotal 0 to 30; 26 or above normal; add 1 point if formal education is 12 years or fewer (maximum stays 30)
MeasuresGlobal cognition with emphasis on executive function and memory; MCI and early dementia screening
Indian adaptationValidated Hindi, Kannada, Malayalam, Tamil and other Indian-language versions (various authors; details uncertain)
Exam trap

MoCA cut-off 26 (normal is 26 or above) vs MMSE 24. Education bonus: +1 for 12 years or fewer. MoCA is more sensitive for MCI; MMSE has a ceiling effect. MoCA includes clock, cube, trail-making; MMSE includes pentagons.

ACE-III: Addenbrooke's Cognitive Examination

The ACE is a longer (about 15 to 20 minutes) cognitive screen from Addenbrooke's Hospital, Cambridge, developed to detect dementia and to help separate Alzheimer's disease from frontotemporal dementia. It gives a 100-point total plus five domain scores, so the profile matters as well as the total (for example, low fluency and language relative to memory suggests frontotemporal dementia). The original ACE and ACE-R contained the MMSE items, so an MMSE score could be extracted; ACE-III replaced them because of MMSE copyright.

FieldDetail
Full nameAddenbrooke's Cognitive Examination, Third Edition (ACE-III)
Author(s), yearACE: Perunthottathu S. Mathuranath, Peter Nestor, German Berrios, W. Rakowicz and John R. Hodges, 2000 (Neurology); ACE-R: Eneida Mioshi, Kate Dawson, Joanna Mitchell, Robert Arnold and John Hodges, 2006; ACE-III: Sharpley Hsieh, Samantha Schubert, Christopher Hoon, Eneida Mioshi and John Hodges, 2013
VersionsACE-III (2013, current); ACE-R (2006); ACE (2000); Mini-ACE (M-ACE, 30 points, 2015)
PublisherFreely available for clinical use from the developers (FRONTIER, Sydney; Cambridge)
Age rangeAdults (validation mostly older adults)
Items100 points
Subtests / scales / indicesAttention 18, Memory 26, Fluency 14, Language 26, Visuospatial 16 (= 100)
Scoring and normsTotal 0 to 100; common cut-offs 88 (high sensitivity) and 82 (high specificity)
MeasuresGlobal cognition and domain profile; dementia detection; AD vs FTD
Indian adaptationMathuranath and colleagues at SCTIMST, Thiruvananthapuram, developed a Malayalam ACE (his later affiliation: uncertain); ACE-III validated in Hindi, Kannada, Malayalam, Tamil, Telugu, Bengali and others (various authors, about 2016 onward; details uncertain)
Exam trap

ACE = 100 points, five domains (attention, memory, fluency, language, visuospatial). First author of the original ACE (2000) is P. S. Mathuranath, an Indian neurologist, a fact Indian papers like. ACE-III (2013) removed the embedded MMSE.

CDT: Clock Drawing Test

The person is asked to draw a clock face with all the numbers and set the hands to a specified time, classically "ten past eleven" (which forces the person to inhibit the pull of the "10" towards the number 10 and place the minute hand at 2). Drawing a clock requires comprehension, planning, visuospatial organisation, number knowledge, abstract representation of time and inhibition, so it is a fast, education-light screen for both visuospatial and executive dysfunction. Hemineglect shows as numbers crowded on one side. It is used alone, inside MoCA, and in the Mini-Cog (Soo Borson and colleagues, 2000: 3-word recall plus clock).

FieldDetail
Full nameClock Drawing Test (CDT)
Author(s), yearUsed informally in neurology since early twentieth century; scoring systems by Kenneth Shulman (1986, 1993), Trey Sunderland and colleagues (1989, 10-point), Rouleau and colleagues (1992), Mendez and colleagues (1992), and others; no single author
VersionsFree-drawn (command) and pre-drawn circle; copy condition; CLOX (Royall, 1998: CLOX1 command and CLOX2 copy)
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults, mainly older adults
ItemsOne drawing; score range depends on system (e.g. Shulman 0 to 5 or 1 to 6; Sunderland 1 to 10; MoCA 3 points)
Subtests / scales / indicesContour, numbers, hands, centre (MoCA); qualitative error categories
Scoring and normsSystem-specific cut-offs; strongly affected by education and literacy
MeasuresVisuospatial construction, executive planning, conceptual knowledge of time, neglect
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian dementia screening, but low literacy reduces validity; no single standard Indian norm (uncertain)
Exam trap

"Ten past eleven" is the classic setting because it tests executive inhibition (stimulus-bound patients put the hand on 10). The clock is part of MoCA and Mini-Cog, not the MMSE.

GCS: Glasgow Coma Scale

The Glasgow Coma Scale is a quick bedside rating of level of consciousness, used in head injury and any acute brain insult. The clinician scores the best response in three areas: eye opening, verbal response and motor response, and adds them. It is not a cognitive test but appears in neuropsychology because severity of traumatic brain injury (TBI), and therefore expected cognitive outcome, is classified using the GCS together with duration of post-traumatic amnesia.

FieldDetail
Full nameGlasgow Coma Scale (GCS)
Author(s), yearGraham Teasdale and Bryan Jennett, 1974 (The Lancet), University of Glasgow
VersionsGCS (1974; the 1974 motor scale had 5 levels, the flexion item was split to give 6 levels from 1976); GCS-Pupils (GCS-P, 2018) subtracts a pupil reactivity score; Paediatric GCS for young children; Glasgow Outcome Scale (Jennett and Bond, 1975) is a separate outcome scale
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults and older children; modified paediatric version for preverbal children
Items3 components: Eye opening 1 to 4, Verbal 1 to 5, Motor 1 to 6
Subtests / scales / indicesEye (4 spontaneous, 3 to sound/speech, 2 to pain/pressure, 1 none); Verbal (5 oriented, 4 confused, 3 inappropriate words, 2 incomprehensible sounds, 1 none); Motor (6 obeys commands, 5 localises pain, 4 normal flexion/withdrawal, 3 abnormal flexion (decorticate), 2 extension (decerebrate), 1 none)
Scoring and normsTotal 3 to 15; TBI severity: 13 to 15 mild, 9 to 12 moderate, 8 or below severe (8 or below usually equals coma)
MeasuresLevel of consciousness; TBI severity
Indian adaptationUsed universally; no adaptation needed
Exam trap

Minimum GCS is 3, not 0 (each component starts at 1). Maximum 15. E4 V5 M6. Severe TBI is 8 or below. Decorticate = 3, decerebrate = 2 on the motor scale.

CDR: Clinical Dementia Rating

The CDR is a clinician-rated staging scale for dementia severity, based on a semi-structured interview with the patient and an informant. The clinician rates six domains of cognitive and functional performance, each on a 5-point scale. A global CDR is derived by an algorithm that gives memory the primary weight, and the CDR Sum of Boxes (adding the six domain scores) gives a finer-grained measure used in research and trials.

FieldDetail
Full nameClinical Dementia Rating (CDR)
Author(s), yearCharles P. Hughes, Leonard Berg, Warren L. Danziger, Lawrence A. Coben and Ronald L. Martin, 1982 (British Journal of Psychiatry), Washington University, St Louis; revised scoring rules by John C. Morris, 1993
VersionsCDR (1982; Morris 1993 update); CDR plus NACC FTLD module (adds behaviour and language domains, 2008 onward)
PublisherWashington University Knight Alzheimer Disease Research Center (copyrighted; free for clinical use)
Age rangeOlder adults
Items6 domains
Subtests / scales / indicesMemory; Orientation; Judgment and Problem Solving; Community Affairs; Home and Hobbies; Personal Care
Scoring and normsEach domain rated 0 (none), 0.5 (questionable), 1 (mild), 2 (moderate), 3 (severe) (Personal Care has no 0.5); global CDR 0 to 3; Sum of Boxes 0 to 18
MeasuresDementia stage (cognitive and functional)
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian dementia research and clinics; Indian-language versions exist (details uncertain)
Exam trap

CDR 0.5 = questionable dementia (often equated with MCI). Memory is the primary domain in the global algorithm. Six domains, Hughes et al. 1982.

HIS: Hachinski Ischemic Score

The Hachinski Ischemic Score is a brief clinical checklist used to distinguish vascular (multi-infarct) dementia from degenerative dementia such as Alzheimer's disease. Features typical of strokes (abrupt onset, stepwise deterioration, fluctuating course, history of strokes, focal neurological signs) score points, some weighted 2 and others 1. A high score points to vascular dementia. It predates modern neuroimaging and does not identify mixed dementia well.

FieldDetail
Full nameHachinski Ischemic Score (HIS, Hachinski Ischaemia Scale)
Author(s), yearVladimir Hachinski and colleagues (Iliff, Zilhka, du Boulay, McAllister, Marshall, Russell, Symon), 1975 (Archives of Neurology)
VersionsOriginal 13 items (1975); modified versions (e.g. Rosen et al., 1980, 8 items)
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults with dementia
Items13 items, total 0 to 18
Subtests / scales / indicesScore 2 each: abrupt onset, fluctuating course, history of strokes, focal neurological symptoms, focal neurological signs. Score 1 each: stepwise deterioration, nocturnal confusion, relative preservation of personality, depression, somatic complaints, emotional incontinence, history of hypertension, evidence of associated atherosclerosis
Scoring and norms7 or above suggests vascular (multi-infarct) dementia; 4 or below suggests primary degenerative (Alzheimer type) dementia; 5 to 6 indeterminate or mixed
MeasuresVascular vs degenerative aetiology
Indian adaptationUsed as is; none standard
Exam trap

High Hachinski = vascular; low = Alzheimer's. Cut-offs 7 or above and 4 or below. Year 1975, the same year as the MMSE.

GDS: Global Deterioration Scale

The Global Deterioration Scale stages primary degenerative dementia (Alzheimer's disease) into seven stages, from no cognitive decline to very severe decline, based on the clinician's overall judgment of cognitive and functional abilities. Each stage has a description of typical features, so the clinician picks the stage that best fits the patient. It is often used with Reisberg's Functional Assessment Staging (FAST, 1988), which describes the parallel loss of daily-living skills.

FieldDetail
Full nameGlobal Deterioration Scale for Assessment of Primary Degenerative Dementia (GDS, "Reisberg scale")
Author(s), yearBarry Reisberg, Steven H. Ferris, Mony J. de Leon and Thomas Crook, 1982 (American Journal of Psychiatry), New York University
VersionsGDS (1982); companion FAST (Reisberg, 1988) and Brief Cognitive Rating Scale (BCRS)
PublisherPublic domain (journal publication)
Age rangeOlder adults
Items7 stages (single global rating)
Subtests / scales / indicesStage 1 no cognitive decline; 2 very mild (subjective complaints, age-associated); 3 mild cognitive decline (MCI); 4 moderate cognitive decline (mild dementia); 5 moderately severe (needs help with choosing clothes, early dementia midstage); 6 severe (needs help with dressing, bathing; may forget spouse's name); 7 very severe (loss of speech and motor abilities)
Scoring and normsStage 1 to 7; dementia begins at stage 4 in Reisberg's descriptions
MeasuresStage of degenerative dementia
Indian adaptationUsed as is; none standard
Exam trap

Two different "GDS" tests: Global Deterioration Scale (Reisberg 1982, 7 stages, dementia staging) vs Geriatric Depression Scale (Jerome Yesavage and colleagues, 1982/1983, 30 yes/no items; short form 15). Same year, same initials: read the expansion in the question.
