Memory hook

"Bender Bends lines, Benton Banks them in memory." For the WCST sort order think "C-F-N: Colour First, Next form, then number." For GCS think "4 eyes, 5 words (jive), 6 moves": E4 V5 M6.

8. Clinical rating scales and symptom inventories

A rating scale gives a number to how much of a symptom a person has. A diagnostic interview answers a yes/no question (does this person meet criteria for disorder X?). A rating scale answers "how much?" (how severe is the depression today, and has it come down since last month?). This is why rating scales are the workhorses of drug trials, outcome monitoring and audit, while diagnosis rests on interviews and clinical judgement. Most of the scales below were built in a burst between about 1959 and 1990, when psychopharmacology needed a way to show that a drug reduced symptoms more than a placebo did.

The first split to learn is who does the rating. In a clinician-rated (observer-rated) scale, a trained interviewer asks questions, observes the patient and assigns the score: HAM-D, HAM-A, MADRS, BPRS, PANSS, SANS, SAPS, YMRS, Y-BOCS, CARS, ISAA, CIWA-Ar and CGI all work this way. In a self-report inventory, the person reads the items and marks the answers: BDI, BAI, PHQ-9, GAD-7, Zung SDS, GHQ, SCL-90-R, AUDIT and EPDS. Clinician ratings can capture things the patient cannot report (blunted affect, poverty of speech, lack of insight) and work with patients who cannot read; self-reports are cheap, fast and capture inner states (guilt, hopelessness) without interviewer bias, but depend on literacy, honesty and insight. A third group is informant-rated (parent or teacher): CBCL, Conners scales, M-CHAT-R/F, and the ADI-R interview of a parent.

The second split is purpose. A screening tool is short and sensitive, meant to pick out possible cases in a population (GHQ-12, PHQ-9, GAD-7, AUDIT, CAGE, M-CHAT-R/F, EPDS): a positive screen means "assess further", never "diagnosis made". A severity or outcome measure tracks how bad symptoms are and how they change with treatment (HAM-D, MADRS, PANSS, Y-BOCS, YMRS, CIWA-Ar, CGI). A diagnostic instrument maps symptoms onto criteria (SCID, MINI, ADOS-2 together with ADI-R; see section 9). A disability measure rates functioning rather than symptoms (IDEAS, WHODAS 2.0, GAF, DAQ). Examiners love asking "which of these is a screening instrument?" and "which is clinician-rated?", so file every scale on both axes.

Definition

Anchor points are the written descriptions attached to each score on an item (for example, on HAM-D item 1, "0 = absent ... 4 = patient reports virtually only these feeling states in spontaneous verbal and non-verbal communication"). Well-defined anchors are what make two raters agree (inter-rater reliability). Sensitivity to change is the ability of a scale to move when the patient improves; MADRS was built specifically to be more sensitive to change than HAM-D.

8.1 Psychosis and schizophrenia

BPRS: Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale

The BPRS is the grandparent of modern psychosis ratings. John Overall and Donald Gorham (1962) wanted a short scale that could quickly measure change in hospitalised patients on the new antipsychotic drugs. After a semi-structured interview of about 20 minutes, the clinician rates each symptom on a 7-point severity scale. Some items are rated from what the patient says (anxiety, guilt), others from observation (blunted affect, motor retardation, mannerisms). It covers positive symptoms, negative symptoms, mood and anxiety, so it is a broad "general psychopathology" measure rather than a schizophrenia-only scale.

FieldDetail
Full nameBrief Psychiatric Rating Scale
Author(s), yearJohn E. Overall and Donald R. Gorham (1962); expanded version by David Lukoff, Keith Nuechterlein and Joseph Ventura (1986)
VersionsOriginal 16-item (1962); 18-item standard version (two items, excitement and disorientation, were added in the 1960s; the 18-item form is the one usually meant); BPRS-E (Expanded) 24 items, Lukoff, Nuechterlein and Ventura (1986), with manual and anchor points; a children's version (BPRS-C) also exists
PublisherPublished in the journal literature (Psychological Reports 1962); public domain in practice
Age rangeAdults (adolescents in practice)
Items16 (original), 18 (standard), 24 (BPRS-E)
Subtests / scales / indicesTotal score; common factor groupings: thought disturbance, anergia (withdrawal-retardation), affect (anxious-depression), hostility-suspiciousness, activation. Sample items: somatic concern, anxiety, emotional withdrawal, conceptual disorganisation, guilt feelings, tension, mannerisms and posturing, grandiosity, depressive mood, hostility, suspiciousness, hallucinatory behaviour, motor retardation, uncooperativeness, unusual thought content, blunted affect, excitement, disorientation
Scoring and normsEach item 1 (not present) to 7 (extremely severe) in the common version, so 18-item total = 18 to 126 (some versions score 0 to 6, total 0 to 108: check which version a question means)
MeasuresGlobal severity of psychopathology and change over treatment
Indian adaptationUsed widely in Indian drug trials in English and translated forms; no single standard Indian adaptation (uncertain)
Exam trap

BPRS = 18 items (the standard answer), originally 16, expanded 24. Do not confuse with PANSS (30). PANSS was built partly by taking the 18 BPRS items and adding items from the Psychopathology Rating Schedule, which is why PANSS contains BPRS inside it.

PANSS: Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale

The PANSS was built to measure the two "syndromes" of schizophrenia that Timothy Crow and Nancy Andreasen were describing around 1980: positive symptoms (things added to normal experience, like hallucinations and delusions) and negative symptoms (things taken away, like blunted affect and social withdrawal). Stanley Kay, Abraham Fiszbein and Lewis Opler (1987) combined the 18 BPRS items with 12 items from the Psychopathology Rating Schedule, giving 30 items rated after a 30 to 40 minute semi-structured interview (the SCI-PANSS) plus informant reports. It is the gold-standard outcome measure in antipsychotic trials.

FieldDetail
Full namePositive and Negative Syndrome Scale
Author(s), yearStanley R. Kay, Abraham Fiszbein and Lewis A. Opler (1987, Schizophrenia Bulletin)
VersionsPANSS (1987); structured interview SCI-PANSS; children's version Kiddie-PANSS; supplementary aggression risk profile (PANSS-S, 3 extra items, not part of the 30)
PublisherMulti-Health Systems (MHS)
Age rangeAdults (Kiddie-PANSS for children)
Items30: 7 positive, 7 negative, 16 general psychopathology
Subtests / scales / indicesPositive scale (P1 delusions, P2 conceptual disorganisation, P3 hallucinatory behaviour, P4 excitement, P5 grandiosity, P6 suspiciousness/persecution, P7 hostility); Negative scale (N1 blunted affect, N2 emotional withdrawal, N3 poor rapport, N4 passive/apathetic social withdrawal, N5 difficulty in abstract thinking, N6 lack of spontaneity and flow of conversation, N7 stereotyped thinking); General psychopathology scale (G1 to G16, e.g. somatic concern, anxiety, guilt, depression, disorientation, poor attention, lack of judgement and insight, preoccupation); Composite scale (positive minus negative, range minus 42 to plus 42)
Scoring and normsEach item 1 (absent) to 7 (extreme). Positive 7 to 49, Negative 7 to 49, General 16 to 112, Total 30 to 210
MeasuresSeverity of positive, negative and general symptoms in schizophrenia; treatment response (commonly 20 to 50 percent reduction from baseline)
Indian adaptationWidely used in Indian research; translated versions exist (uncertain which is standard)
Exam trap

PANSS = 30 items, 7 + 7 + 16, minimum score 30 (not 0, because each item starts at 1), maximum 210. "Lack of judgement and insight" is a general item, not a negative item. "Difficulty in abstract thinking" is a negative item, even though it sounds cognitive.

SANS: Scale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms

Nancy Andreasen (1982, with the full scale published 1984) built SANS because negative symptoms were poorly defined and nobody agreed on how to rate them. She grouped them into five symptom complexes, each with several specific items and one global rating. The clinician rates from interview and observation over the past month. SANS is the classic answer to "which scale measures negative symptoms in detail?"

FieldDetail
Full nameScale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms
Author(s), yearNancy C. Andreasen (1982 paper in Archives of General Psychiatry; scale published 1983/1984, University of Iowa)
VersionsSingle standard version; companion SAPS (1984)
PublisherUniversity of Iowa (author-distributed)
Age rangeAdults
Items25 (including 5 global ratings)
Subtests / scales / indices5 subscales: affective flattening or blunting (8 items including global), alogia (5), avolition-apathy (4), anhedonia-asociality (5), attention (3)
Scoring and normsEach item 0 (none) to 5 (severe); totals reported as sum of items or sum of global ratings (sources and studies differ)
MeasuresNegative symptoms of schizophrenia
Indian adaptationUsed in English and translated forms in Indian studies; no single standard adaptation (uncertain)
Exam trap

The five SANS domains are the "five A's": Affective flattening, Alogia, Avolition-apathy, Anhedonia-asociality, Attention. "Attention" being a negative symptom domain surprises people and is a favourite odd-one-out question. SANS is 0 to 5, PANSS is 1 to 7.

SAPS: Scale for the Assessment of Positive Symptoms

SAPS is the companion to SANS, also by Nancy Andreasen (1984). It rates positive symptoms in four domains. Like SANS, each domain ends in a global rating, and the rater uses interview plus observation over the past month.

FieldDetail
Full nameScale for the Assessment of Positive Symptoms
Author(s), yearNancy C. Andreasen (1984)
VersionsSingle standard version
PublisherUniversity of Iowa (author-distributed)
Age rangeAdults
Items34 (including 4 global ratings); this is the commonly cited count, but some secondary sources give 35, so treat as "34 in most sources"
Subtests / scales / indices4 domains: hallucinations, delusions, bizarre behaviour, positive formal thought disorder
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 5
MeasuresPositive symptoms of schizophrenia
Indian adaptationAs for SANS (uncertain)
Exam trap

SANS 25 items / 5 domains; SAPS 34 items / 4 domains. Both are Andreasen, both 0 to 5. Do not attribute them to Kay (PANSS) or to Crow (who proposed the Type I / Type II classification, not a scale).

8.2 Depression, anxiety and suicide

HAM-D: Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression

Max Hamilton (1960), a psychiatrist at Leeds, designed this scale to quantify the severity of depression in patients already diagnosed as depressed. It is explicitly not a diagnostic or screening tool. The clinician interviews the patient (about 20 to 30 minutes) and rates symptoms over the past week. It leans heavily on somatic and vegetative symptoms (sleep, appetite, weight, somatic anxiety, libido), which reflects the hospitalised melancholic patients of 1960 and is also its main criticism. Interview guides such as the SIGH-D (Janet Williams, 1988) were later written to improve reliability.

FieldDetail
Full nameHamilton Rating Scale for Depression (HRSD, HAM-D, HDRS)
Author(s), yearMax Hamilton (1960, Journal of Neurology, Neurosurgery and Psychiatry); revised 1967
VersionsHAM-D17 (scored version); HAM-D21 (17 plus 4 items: diurnal variation, depersonalisation/derealisation, paranoid symptoms, obsessional symptoms, which describe the type of depression but are not added to severity); 24-item and 6-item (HAM-D6, Per Bech) variants; GRID-HAMD (2008); SIGH-D interview guide
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults
Items17 (standard scored), 21 (extended)
Subtests / scales / indicesTotal score; items include depressed mood, guilt, suicide, insomnia (early, middle, late: 3 separate items), work and activities, retardation, agitation, psychic anxiety, somatic anxiety, gastrointestinal and general somatic symptoms, genital symptoms, hypochondriasis, weight loss, insight
Scoring and normsMixed item ranges: some items 0 to 4, others 0 to 2. HAM-D17 total 0 to 52. Common bands (sources differ): 0 to 7 normal, 8 to 13 mild, 14 to 18 moderate, 19 to 22 severe, 23 and above very severe. Remission usually defined as 7 or less
MeasuresSeverity of depression in diagnosed patients, change with treatment
Indian adaptationWidely used in Indian research and trials; Hindi versions in use (uncertain who standardised)
Exam trap

HAM-D is clinician-rated; BDI is self-report. Both measure depression severity, which is exactly why examiners pair them. HAM-D17 range is 0 to 52 (not 0 to 68, which would be 17 x 4, because several items only go to 2). HAM-D was published in 1960; HAM-A came first, in 1959.

HAM-A: Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale

Max Hamilton's anxiety scale (1959, British Journal of Medical Psychology) predates the depression scale. Like HAM-D it is clinician-rated and meant to measure severity in people with anxiety, not to diagnose. Items split into psychic anxiety (mental agitation and distress) and somatic anxiety (physical complaints), and the scale is criticised for its heavy somatic loading, which makes it hard to separate anxiety from depression or physical illness.

FieldDetail
Full nameHamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (HARS, HAM-A)
Author(s), yearMax Hamilton (1959)
VersionsOriginal; structured interview guide SIGH-A (Shear et al., 2001)
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults (children in some studies)
Items14
Subtests / scales / indicesPsychic anxiety (anxious mood, tension, fears, insomnia, intellectual/cognitive, depressed mood, behaviour at interview) and somatic anxiety (muscular, sensory, cardiovascular, respiratory, gastrointestinal, genitourinary, autonomic)
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 4; total 0 to 56. Common bands (sources differ): 17 or less mild, 18 to 24 mild to moderate, 25 to 30 moderate to severe
MeasuresSeverity of anxiety
Indian adaptationUsed widely; no single standard adaptation (uncertain)
Exam trap

HAM-A 14 items, 0 to 56 (14 x 4, because every item goes 0 to 4). Contrast HAM-D17 0 to 52. HAM-A is 1959, HAM-D 1960.

MADRS: Montgomery-Åsberg Depression Rating Scale

Stuart Montgomery and Marie Åsberg (1979, British Journal of Psychiatry) selected the 10 items from Åsberg's larger Comprehensive Psychopathological Rating Scale (CPRS) that changed most with antidepressant treatment. The goal was a scale more sensitive to change than HAM-D and less contaminated by somatic symptoms, which matters when testing drugs whose side effects are themselves somatic. It is clinician-rated after a clinical interview.

FieldDetail
Full nameMontgomery-Åsberg Depression Rating Scale
Author(s), yearStuart A. Montgomery and Marie Åsberg (1979)
VersionsClinician version; self-rated MADRS-S (Svanborg and Åsberg, 1994)
PublisherJournal publication; widely available
Age rangeAdults
Items10
Subtests / scales / indicesApparent sadness, reported sadness, inner tension, reduced sleep, reduced appetite, concentration difficulties, lassitude, inability to feel, pessimistic thoughts, suicidal thoughts
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 6; total 0 to 60. Common bands (sources differ): 0 to 6 normal, 7 to 19 mild, 20 to 34 moderate, 35 and above severe
MeasuresDepression severity and change with treatment
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian trials (uncertain standard adaptation)
Exam trap

MADRS = 10 items x 0 to 6 = 0 to 60; designed for sensitivity to change. "Apparent sadness" is the only purely observed item.

BDI: Beck Depression Inventory

Aaron T. Beck (1961) built the BDI from the statements depressed patients actually made in therapy. Each of the 21 items is a group of four statements ordered by severity (for example, "I do not feel sad" to "I am so sad or unhappy that I can't stand it"), and the person picks the one that fits best. The original was read aloud by an interviewer; the 1978 revision (BDI-IA) and the 1996 BDI-II are fully self-administered. BDI-II was rewritten to match DSM-IV criteria for major depression, which is why some items changed. It reflects Beck's cognitive theory: many items capture the negative triad (self, world, future).

FieldDetail
Full nameBeck Depression Inventory
Author(s), yearAaron T. Beck, C. H. Ward, M. Mendelson, J. Mock and J. Erbaugh (1961); BDI-IA Beck (1978, copyrighted 1979); BDI-II Aaron Beck, Robert Steer and Gregory Brown (1996)
VersionsBDI-II (1996, current); BDI-IA (1978); BDI original (1961); BDI-FastScreen for medical patients (7 items, formerly BDI-PC); BDI short form (13 items, Beck and Beck 1972)
PublisherPearson (formerly The Psychological Corporation)
Age rangeBDI-II: 13 years and above
Items21 in BDI, BDI-IA and BDI-II
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total; factor analyses commonly give cognitive-affective and somatic-vegetative factors. BDI-II added agitation, worthlessness, concentration difficulty and loss of energy, and dropped weight loss, body image change, somatic preoccupation and work difficulty; sleep and appetite items now allow increase or decrease
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 3; total 0 to 63. BDI-II time frame: past two weeks including today (BDI-IA: past week). BDI-II cut-offs: 0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate, 29 to 63 severe
MeasuresSeverity of depressive symptoms (self-report)
Indian adaptationHindi and other Indian-language translations are used in research; no single officially standardised Indian edition (uncertain)
Exam trap

BDI-II: 21 items, 0 to 63, 2-week window, 1996, Aaron Beck, Steer and Brown, ages 13+. Cut-off 14 = mild starts. It is a severity measure, not a diagnostic test. Same 21 items and 0 to 63 range as the BAI, so questions often swap them.

BAI: Beck Anxiety Inventory

Aaron Beck and colleagues (Beck, Epstein, Brown and Steer, 1988) built the BAI to measure anxiety in a way that overlaps as little as possible with depression, a known weakness of older anxiety scales. Many of its items are somatic (numbness, feeling hot, wobbliness in legs, heart pounding), reflecting panic-type physiological arousal. The person rates how much each symptom bothered them.

FieldDetail
Full nameBeck Anxiety Inventory
Author(s), yearAaron T. Beck, Norman Epstein, Gary Brown and Robert Steer (1988); manual Beck and Steer (1990, revised 1993)
VersionsBAI (single standard adult version); a youth anxiety inventory exists within the separate Beck Youth Inventories
PublisherPearson (Psychological Corporation)
Age range17 years and above (commonly stated; sources differ)
Items21
Subtests / scales / indicesTotal; factors usually somatic and cognitive (subjective) anxiety
Scoring and normsEach item 0 (not at all) to 3 (severely); total 0 to 63. Manual bands: 0 to 7 minimal, 8 to 15 mild, 16 to 25 moderate, 26 to 63 severe. Time frame: sources differ (past week in the 1988 paper, past month in the later manual)
MeasuresSeverity of anxiety, emphasising physiological symptoms
Indian adaptationTranslated versions used in research (uncertain)
Exam trap

BAI is 1988, BDI 1961; both 21 items, 0 to 63. BAI's distinctive feature is its heavy somatic content and its design to discriminate anxiety from depression.

BHS: Beck Hopelessness Scale

Aaron Beck saw hopelessness (negative expectations about the future) as the link between depression and suicide. The BHS (Beck, Weissman, Lester and Trexler, 1974) asks 20 true/false statements about the future. Follow-up studies by Beck and colleagues (1985) found that a score of 9 or more predicted eventual suicide in psychiatric patients better than depression scores did, which made the BHS a classic suicide-risk tool.

FieldDetail
Full nameBeck Hopelessness Scale
Author(s), yearAaron T. Beck, Arlene Weissman, David Lester and Larry Trexler (1974)
VersionsSingle version; manual Beck and Steer (1988)
PublisherPearson
Age range17 years and above (commonly stated)
Items20 true/false (9 keyed false, 11 keyed true)
Subtests / scales / indicesThree factors in the 1974 paper: feelings about the future, loss of motivation, future expectations
Scoring and norms0 to 20. Common bands: 0 to 3 minimal, 4 to 8 mild, 9 to 14 moderate, 15 to 20 severe; 9 or more flags suicide risk
MeasuresHopelessness, negative expectancy about the future; indirect suicide risk
Indian adaptationTranslated versions used (uncertain)
Exam trap

BHS is the only Beck scale here that is true/false (dichotomous). It has 20 items, not 21. Hopelessness, not depression, is the stronger predictor of suicide in Aaron Beck's research.

SSI / BSS: Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation

The Scale for Suicide Ideation (SSI; Aaron Beck, Maria Kovacs and Arlene Weissman, 1979) is a clinician-rated interview that measures how intense, specific and current a person's suicidal thinking is: wish to live, wish to die, duration and frequency of thoughts, deterrents, preparation and so on. The later Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation (BSS; Beck and Steer, 1991) is a self-report version of the same content. The first five items act as a screen: if the person denies any active or passive wish, the rest is skipped.

FieldDetail
Full nameScale for Suicide Ideation (SSI, 1979); Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation (BSS, 1991)
Author(s), yearAaron T. Beck, Maria Kovacs and Arlene Weissman (1979); BSS Beck and Robert Steer (1991)
VersionsSSI (clinician interview); BSS (self-report); SSI-W (worst point) variant
PublisherBSS: Pearson
Age range17 years and above (commonly stated)
Items19 scored items in SSI; BSS 21 items (19 scored plus 2 on past suicide attempts)
Subtests / scales / indicesActive suicidal desire, preparation, passive suicidal desire (factor structure varies)
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 2; total 0 to 38; no fixed cut-off, any positive ideation warrants clinical assessment
MeasuresIntensity of current suicidal ideation
Indian adaptationUncertain
Exam trap

The 1979 SSI is clinician-rated; the 1991 BSS is self-report. Do not mix up with the Beck Hopelessness Scale (1974), which measures expectations about the future, not suicidal ideation itself.

PHQ-9: Patient Health Questionnaire-9

The PHQ-9 (Kurt Kroenke, Robert Spitzer and Janet Williams, 2001) is the depression module of the Patient Health Questionnaire, itself a self-report descendant of PRIME-MD (Primary Care Evaluation of Mental Disorders, Spitzer et al., 1994). Its nine items are simply the nine DSM-IV criteria for a major depressive episode, each rated for frequency over the past two weeks. Because it maps onto criteria, it can be scored as a severity score or as a provisional diagnostic algorithm, and it is the standard primary-care depression screen worldwide.

FieldDetail
Full namePatient Health Questionnaire-9
Author(s), yearKurt Kroenke, Robert L. Spitzer and Janet B. W. Williams (2001)
VersionsPHQ-9; PHQ-2 (first two items: anhedonia and depressed mood, 0 to 6, cut-off 3); PHQ-A for adolescents; full PHQ includes other modules; PHQ-15 for somatic symptoms
PublisherDeveloped with a grant from Pfizer; free to use, no permission required
Age rangeAdults (PHQ-A 11 to 17)
Items9 (plus an unscored functional difficulty question)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total; item 9 asks about thoughts of death or self-harm and must be followed up whenever positive
Scoring and normsEach item 0 (not at all) to 3 (nearly every day); total 0 to 27. Bands: 5 mild, 10 moderate, 15 moderately severe, 20 severe; 10 or more is the usual cut-off for probable major depression
MeasuresScreening and severity of depression
Indian adaptationHindi and several regional-language versions validated in Indian primary care studies (uncertain which is standard)
Exam trap

PHQ-9 = 9 DSM criteria, 0 to 27, 2 weeks, cut-off 10. Same authors as GAD-7. The PHQ is self-report; its parent PRIME-MD was clinician-administered.

GAD-7: Generalized Anxiety Disorder 7-item scale

Robert Spitzer, Kurt Kroenke, Janet Williams and Bernd Löwe (2006) built the GAD-7 as the anxiety partner of the PHQ-9. It was designed for generalised anxiety disorder but also screens reasonably for panic, social anxiety and PTSD. Same format: frequency over the past two weeks.

FieldDetail
Full nameGeneralized Anxiety Disorder 7-item scale
Author(s), yearRobert L. Spitzer, Kurt Kroenke, Janet B. W. Williams and Bernd Löwe (2006)
VersionsGAD-7; GAD-2 (first two items)
PublisherFree to use (Pfizer-funded development)
Age rangeAdults (adolescent use reported)
Items7
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 3; total 0 to 21. Bands: 5 mild, 10 moderate, 15 severe; 10 or more usual cut-off
MeasuresScreening and severity of generalised anxiety
Indian adaptationHindi and regional versions used in research (uncertain)
Exam trap

GAD-7 is 2006, PHQ-9 2001. GAD-7 maximum is 21, PHQ-9 maximum 27. Both use 5/10/15 bands.

Zung SDS: Zung Self-Rating Depression Scale

William Zung (1965, Duke University) produced one of the earliest self-report depression scales. Twenty statements cover affective, physiological and psychological symptoms; half are worded positively and half negatively to reduce acquiescent responding, so half the items are reverse-scored. Zung later produced a matching Self-Rating Anxiety Scale (SAS, 1971).

FieldDetail
Full nameZung Self-Rating Depression Scale
Author(s), yearWilliam W. K. Zung (1965)
VersionsSDS (1965); Zung Self-Rating Anxiety Scale (1971) is a separate scale
PublisherPublic domain in practice
Age rangeAdults
Items20 (10 positively, 10 negatively worded)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total
Scoring and normsEach item 1 to 4 ("a little of the time" to "most of the time"); raw 20 to 80; SDS Index = raw divided by 80 x 100 (25 to 100). Common raw bands: 20 to 49 normal, 50 to 59 mild, 60 to 69 moderate, 70 and above severe (sources differ)
MeasuresDepression severity (self-report)
Indian adaptationTranslated versions used (uncertain)
Exam trap

Zung SDS minimum score is 20, not 0, because items run 1 to 4. It is self-rated; the "SDS index" converts the raw score to a percentage.

8.3 OCD, mania and autism

Y-BOCS: Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale

Wayne Goodman and colleagues at Yale and Brown universities (Goodman, Price, Rasmussen, Mazure and others, 1989) built the Y-BOCS to rate OCD severity independent of the type of obsessions and compulsions. First the clinician goes through a symptom checklist (over 60 examples of obsessions and compulsions) to identify the patient's target symptoms. Then 10 core severity items are rated: the same five questions are asked once about obsessions and once about compulsions (time occupied, interference, distress, resistance, degree of control). So a person washing for hours and a person checking for hours can get the same severity score.

FieldDetail
Full nameYale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale
Author(s), yearWayne K. Goodman, Lawrence H. Price, Steven A. Rasmussen, Carolyn Mazure, R. L. Fleischmann, C. L. Hill, George Heninger and Dennis Charney (1989, Archives of General Psychiatry, two papers)
VersionsY-BOCS (1989); Y-BOCS-II (Storch et al., 2010; items rescored 0 to 5, resistance item replaced, avoidance incorporated); CY-BOCS for children and adolescents (Scahill et al., 1997); self-report forms exist
PublisherAuthor-distributed; freely available for clinical use
Age rangeAdults; CY-BOCS about 6 to 17 years
Items10 core severity items (5 obsession, 5 compulsion) plus symptom checklist; additional investigational items (11 onwards, e.g. insight, avoidance) are not part of the total
Subtests / scales / indicesObsessions subtotal (items 1 to 5) and compulsions subtotal (items 6 to 10); each five covers time, interference, distress, resistance, control
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 4; subtotals 0 to 20 each; total 0 to 40. Common bands: 0 to 7 subclinical, 8 to 15 mild, 16 to 23 moderate, 24 to 31 severe, 32 to 40 extreme. Treatment response often defined as 25 to 35 percent reduction
MeasuresSeverity of OCD regardless of symptom content
Indian adaptationUsed routinely at NIMHANS and other Indian centres, often in translation (uncertain standard adaptation)
Exam trap

Y-BOCS has 10 core items (5 + 5), 0 to 40, plus a separate checklist that is not scored into severity. Clinician-rated semi-structured interview. "Resistance" is scored as effort made to resist, so more effort = lower score.

YMRS: Young Mania Rating Scale

Robert Young, Biggs, Ziegler and Meyer (1978, British Journal of Psychiatry) modelled the YMRS on the HAM-D: a clinician-rated scale of mania severity in patients already diagnosed. Ratings come from the patient's report of the past 48 hours plus the clinician's observation during the interview (observation is weighted more heavily). Four items judged most important in mania are double-weighted (scored 0 to 8) to compensate for poor cooperation in severely manic patients.

FieldDetail
Full nameYoung Mania Rating Scale
Author(s), yearRobert C. Young, J. T. Biggs, V. E. Ziegler and D. A. Meyer (1978)
VersionsYMRS; parent version P-YMRS for youth
PublisherPublic domain in practice
Age rangeAdults (used in adolescents)
Items11
Subtests / scales / indicesElevated mood, increased motor activity-energy, sexual interest, sleep, irritability*, speech (rate and amount)*, language-thought disorder, content*, disruptive-aggressive behaviour*, appearance, insight (* = double-weighted 0 to 8)
Scoring and normsSeven items 0 to 4, four items 0 to 8; total 0 to 60. Score of 12 or less often used for remission (sources differ)
MeasuresSeverity of manic symptoms
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian trials (uncertain)
Exam trap

YMRS = 11 items, 4 double-weighted (irritability, speech, content, disruptive-aggressive behaviour), 0 to 60, past 48 hours, clinician-rated. Do not confuse with MADRS (10 items, also 0 to 60).

CARS: Childhood Autism Rating Scale

Eric Schopler, Robert Reichler and Barbara Rochen Renner developed CARS within the TEACCH programme at the University of North Carolina (first published 1980 with DeVellis and Daly; manual 1986/1988). The clinician observes the child (and uses parent report and records) and rates 15 areas of behaviour against age-typical behaviour. Its practical strength is that it separates autism from other developmental disabilities and grades severity, in about 30 minutes, using ordinary observation.

FieldDetail
Full nameChildhood Autism Rating Scale
Author(s), yearEric Schopler, Robert J. Reichler, R. F. DeVellis and K. Daly (1980); manual Schopler, Reichler and Barbara Rochen Renner (1986/1988); CARS-2 Schopler, Mary Van Bourgondien, Glenna Wellman and Steven Love (2010)
VersionsCARS-2 (2010): CARS2-ST (Standard: under 6 years, or older with IQ below about 80 or notably impaired communication), CARS2-HF (High-Functioning: 6 years and above, IQ about 80 or above, fluent speech), CARS2-QPC (parent/caregiver questionnaire, unscored); CARS (1980/1988)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services (WPS)
Age range2 years and above
Items15
Subtests / scales / indicesRelating to people; imitation; emotional response; body use; object use; adaptation to change; visual response; listening response; taste, smell and touch response; fear or nervousness; verbal communication; non-verbal communication; activity level; level and consistency of intellectual response; general impressions
Scoring and normsEach item 1 (within normal limits) to 4 (severely abnormal), with half points (1.5, 2.5, 3.5) allowed; total 15 to 60. Original bands: below 30 non-autistic, 30 to 36.5 mild to moderate autism, 37 to 60 severe. CARS2-HF uses a lower cut-off (about 28; sources differ)
MeasuresPresence and severity of autism
Indian adaptationCARS was widely used in India for diagnosis and certification before ISAA; it was a key reference instrument in developing ISAA
Exam trap

CARS = 15 items, 1 to 4 with half points, total 15 to 60, cut-off 30, authors Schopler, Reichler, Renner (TEACCH). CARS-2 (2010) = ST and HF forms. Not to be confused with ISAA (40 items, cut-off 70).

ISAA: Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism

ISAA was developed by the National Institute for the Mentally Handicapped (NIMH, Secunderabad, now NIEPID, the National Institute for the Empowerment of Persons with Intellectual Disabilities) in a Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment funded project, with the report released around 2009. It was built partly from CARS items and adapted to Indian cultural contexts. Its main use is disability certification: Government of India guidelines (2016 notification and later RPwD Act 2016 guidelines) prescribe ISAA for assessing and certifying autism, with the score converted to a percentage of disability. Ratings combine observation, clinical evaluation, testing through interaction, and parent or informant interview.

FieldDetail
Full nameIndian Scale for Assessment of Autism
Author(s), yearNational Institute for the Mentally Handicapped (NIMH), Secunderabad (2009)
VersionsSingle version (2009)
PublisherNIMH (NIEPID), Secunderabad; Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment
Age rangeCommonly stated as 3 to 22 years (the standardisation range; sources differ)
Items40
Subtests / scales / indices6 domains: social relationship and reciprocity (9 items), emotional responsiveness (5), speech, language and communication (9), behaviour patterns (7), sensory aspects (6), cognitive component (4)
Scoring and normsEach item 1 (rarely, up to 20 percent of the time) to 5 (always, 81 to 100 percent); total 40 to 200. Below 70 no autism; 70 to 106 mild autism (40 to 60 percent disability); 107 to 153 moderate (61 to 80 percent); above 153 severe (above 80 percent)
MeasuresPresence and severity of autism; percentage disability for certification
Indian adaptationIt is itself the Indian instrument
Exam trap

ISAA = 40 items, 6 domains, 1 to 5, cut-off 70, by NIMH Secunderabad (not NIMHANS Bengaluru, a classic distractor). It is the legally prescribed tool for autism certification in India. The percentage bands (mild 40 to 60 percent) are sometimes stated slightly differently across sources.

ADOS-2: Autism Diagnostic Observation Schedule, Second Edition

The ADOS is a semi-structured, standardised observation in which the examiner sets up play activities and conversational "presses" (for example, a pretend birthday party, a joint-attention task, a description of a picture) designed to elicit social communication and restricted or repetitive behaviours. The examiner chooses one module according to the person's expressive language level and age, administers it in about 40 to 60 minutes, and codes behaviour into an algorithm. With the ADI-R it forms the international research "gold standard" for autism diagnosis.

FieldDetail
Full nameAutism Diagnostic Observation Schedule, Second Edition
Author(s), yearCatherine Lord, Michael Rutter, Pamela DiLavore, Susan Risi, Katherine Gotham and Somer Bishop (2012); Toddler Module with Rhiannon Luyster; original ADOS Lord et al. (1989); PL-ADOS DiLavore, Lord and Rutter (1995); ADOS-G Lord et al. (2000)
VersionsADOS-2 (2012); ADOS (2000/2001, sometimes called ADOS-G); PL-ADOS (1995); ADOS (1989)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services (WPS)
Age range12 months to adulthood
ItemsActivities and codes vary by module (uncertain to give one count)
Subtests / scales / indicesToddler Module (12 to 30 months, no consistent phrase speech); Module 1 (31 months and older, little or no phrase speech); Module 2 (phrase speech, not verbally fluent); Module 3 (fluent speech, children and young adolescents); Module 4 (fluent speech, older adolescents and adults). Algorithm domains: Social Affect (SA) and Restricted and Repetitive Behaviour (RRB)
Scoring and normsAlgorithm totals against cut-offs (autism, autism spectrum, non-spectrum); Comparison Score 1 to 10 (Modules 1 to 3); Toddler Module gives "ranges of concern"
MeasuresObserved social communication and restricted/repetitive behaviour for ASD diagnosis
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian research centres with WPS training; no Indian norms (uncertain)
Exam trap

ADOS-2 = 5 modules (Toddler + 1 to 4), chosen by language level not just age. ADOS is observation of the child; ADI-R is an interview of the parent.

ADI-R: Autism Diagnostic Interview, Revised

The ADI-R is a long, standardised, semi-structured interview with a parent or caregiver about the child's developmental history and current behaviour, especially around ages 4 to 5 years when autistic features are often most marked. It takes about 1.5 to 2.5 hours and its scores feed an algorithm keyed to ICD-10 and DSM-IV criteria.

FieldDetail
Full nameAutism Diagnostic Interview, Revised
Author(s), yearMichael Rutter, Ann Le Couteur and Catherine Lord (WPS manual 2003); ADI-R published in the literature by Lord, Rutter and Le Couteur (1994); original ADI Le Couteur et al. (1989)
VersionsADI-R (1994; WPS 2003); ADI (1989)
PublisherWestern Psychological Services (WPS)
Age rangeChildren and adults with a mental age above about 2 years
Items93
Subtests / scales / indicesThree domains: language and communication; reciprocal social interaction; restricted, repetitive and stereotyped behaviours and interests; plus age of onset (abnormality evident by 36 months)
Scoring and normsItems coded 0 to 3, recoded for algorithm; domain cut-offs
MeasuresDevelopmental history of autistic features
Indian adaptationUncertain
Exam trap

ADI-R = 93 items, parent interview, Michael Rutter, Le Couteur and Lord. ADOS-2 = observation. Both are WPS.

M-CHAT-R/F: Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers, Revised with Follow-Up

The M-CHAT is a parent-completed yes/no screener for autism in toddlers. It descends from the CHAT (Simon Baron-Cohen, Jane Allen and Christopher Gillberg, 1992), which had a parent section and a GP observation section for 18-month-olds. Diana Robins, Deborah Fein, Marianne Barton and James Green modified it in 2001 (M-CHAT, 23 items, parent-only), and Robins, Fein and Barton revised it (M-CHAT-R/F, 2009; validation 2014) to 20 clearer items plus a structured Follow-Up interview for medium-risk scores, which cuts false positives sharply.

FieldDetail
Full nameModified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers, Revised with Follow-Up
Author(s), yearDiana L. Robins, Deborah Fein and Marianne Barton (2009; validation paper 2014); M-CHAT Robins, Fein, Barton and Green (2001); CHAT Baron-Cohen, Allen and Gillberg (1992)
VersionsM-CHAT-R/F (2009); M-CHAT (2001, 23 items); CHAT (1992)
PublisherFree for clinical and research use (mchatscreen.com)
Age range16 to 30 months
Items20 yes/no (M-CHAT 23)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle risk total; Follow-Up interview for items failed
Scoring and normsScore 0 to 20: 0 to 2 low risk; 3 to 7 medium risk (administer Follow-Up; if still 2 or more, refer); 8 to 20 high risk (refer directly)
MeasuresRisk of autism (screening only)
Indian adaptationHindi and other translations available and studied (uncertain)
Exam trap

M-CHAT-R/F = 20 items, 16 to 30 months, parent-completed screen; original M-CHAT 23 items; CHAT (Baron-Cohen 1992) at 18 months.

8.4 General psychopathology screens

GHQ: General Health Questionnaire

David Goldberg (1972, Maudsley Monograph, "The Detection of Psychiatric Illness by Questionnaire") designed the GHQ to detect non-psychotic psychiatric disorder (the anxiety-depression "common mental disorders") in community and primary-care settings. It asks about recent changes from the person's usual state ("Have you recently lost much sleep over worry?" with options "not at all / no more than usual / rather more than usual / much more than usual"). Because it asks about change, it is good at picking up recent distress but can miss long-standing chronic problems. The 60-item original was shortened several times; GHQ-12 is the default screening version worldwide.

FieldDetail
Full nameGeneral Health Questionnaire
Author(s), yearDavid Goldberg (1972); GHQ-28 Goldberg and Valerie Hillier (1979); user's guide Goldberg and Paul Williams (1988)
VersionsGHQ-60 (1972), GHQ-30, GHQ-28 (1979, scaled), GHQ-20, GHQ-12
PublisherGL Assessment (formerly NFER-Nelson)
Age rangeAdolescents and adults
Items60, 30, 28, 20 or 12 depending on version
Subtests / scales / indicesGHQ-28 has 4 subscales of 7 items each: somatic symptoms, anxiety and insomnia, social dysfunction, severe depression. Other versions give a single total
Scoring and normsGHQ scoring (0-0-1-1: the two "healthy" options score 0, the two "unhealthy" options score 1; GHQ-12 range 0 to 12; this is the bimodal method used for caseness); Likert scoring (0-1-2-3; GHQ-12 range 0 to 36; better for measuring severity); C-GHQ (chronic scoring, Goodchild and Duncan-Jones 1985). GHQ-12 cut-offs vary by study (commonly 2/3 or 3/4)
MeasuresLikely presence of non-psychotic psychiatric disorder (caseness), not a diagnosis
Indian adaptationHindi GHQ standardised by S. Gautam, M. Nijhawan and P. Kamal (1987, Indian Journal of Psychiatry); GHQ-12 widely used in Indian community surveys
Exam trap

GHQ-12 is the screening default. GHQ-28 (Goldberg and Hillier, 1979) is the one with 4 subscales. "GHQ scoring" means 0-0-1-1; Likert is 0-1-2-3. GHQ detects non-psychotic disorder, so it is not a psychosis screen.

SCL-90-R: Symptom Checklist-90-Revised

Leonard Derogatis developed the SCL-90 (Derogatis, Lipman and Covi, 1973) from the Hopkins Symptom Checklist (HSCL), and revised it as the SCL-90-R (1975; manuals 1977, 1983, 1994). The person rates how much each of 90 symptoms distressed them in the past 7 days. Scores are organised into 9 primary symptom dimensions plus 3 global indices, converted to T scores against normative groups (adult non-patient, psychiatric outpatient, inpatient, adolescent). It is a broadband self-report symptom profile, useful for screening and outcome monitoring.

FieldDetail
Full nameSymptom Checklist-90-Revised
Author(s), yearLeonard R. Derogatis (SCL-90 1973 with Lipman and Covi; SCL-90-R 1975, manual 1977; 3rd edition manual 1994)
VersionsSCL-90-R; SCL-90 (1973); Brief Symptom Inventory BSI (53 items, Derogatis 1975/1993); BSI-18 (2001); parent instrument HSCL (58 items)
PublisherPearson (formerly NCS)
Age range13 years and above
Items90 (BSI 53, BSI-18 18)
Subtests / scales / indices9 primary dimensions: Somatization (SOM), Obsessive-Compulsive (O-C), Interpersonal Sensitivity (I-S), Depression (DEP), Anxiety (ANX), Hostility (HOS), Phobic Anxiety (PHOB), Paranoid Ideation (PAR), Psychoticism (PSY); 7 additional items (appetite, sleep, guilt, thoughts of death) scored only in global indices. 3 global indices: GSI (Global Severity Index, mean of all items, best single indicator), PSDI (Positive Symptom Distress Index, intensity among endorsed items), PST (Positive Symptom Total, number of items endorsed)
Scoring and normsEach item 0 (not at all) to 4 (extremely); T scores mean 50 SD 10; a GSI T score of 63 or above (or any two dimensions at 63 or above) is the manual's "caseness" rule
MeasuresCurrent psychological symptom distress
Indian adaptationTranslated and used in Indian research (uncertain standard adaptation)
Exam trap

SCL-90-R = 9 dimensions, 3 global indices (GSI, PSDI, PST), past 7 days, 0 to 4. GSI is the best single summary. BSI = 53 items, same 9 dimensions. Do not confuse with the MMPI's 10 clinical scales.

8.5 Substance use

AUDIT: Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test

The AUDIT was developed by a World Health Organization collaborative project across six countries to identify hazardous and harmful drinking early, in primary care, before dependence sets in. Thomas Babor and John Saunders led the work (manual 1989; validation paper Saunders, Aasland, Babor, de la Fuente and Grant, 1993; second edition manual Babor, Higgins-Biddle, Saunders and Monteiro, 2001). Ten questions cover how much the person drinks, signs of dependence and alcohol-related harm in the past year.

FieldDetail
Full nameAlcohol Use Disorders Identification Test
Author(s), yearWHO; Thomas F. Babor, John B. Saunders and colleagues (1989; Saunders et al. 1993); 2nd edition manual 2001
VersionsAUDIT (10 items); AUDIT-C (first 3 consumption items); clinician and self-report formats
PublisherWorld Health Organization (free)
Age rangeAdults (adolescent use reported)
Items10
Subtests / scales / indicesItems 1 to 3 hazardous consumption; items 4 to 6 dependence symptoms; items 7 to 10 harmful use
Scoring and normsItems 1 to 8 scored 0 to 4; items 9 and 10 scored 0, 2 or 4; total 0 to 40. Cut-off 8 or more indicates hazardous or harmful use. WHO risk zones: 0 to 7 (zone I, education), 8 to 15 (zone II, simple advice), 16 to 19 (zone III, brief counselling and monitoring), 20 to 40 (zone IV, referral for diagnosis and treatment)
MeasuresHazardous drinking, harmful use, possible dependence
Indian adaptationValidated translations in several Indian languages (uncertain standard)
Exam trap

AUDIT = WHO, 10 items, 0 to 40, cut-off 8, past year, targets early (hazardous) drinking. CAGE is 4 items and targets lifetime problems; MAST is 25 items.

CAGE Questionnaire

John Ewing (published 1984 in JAMA, though he reported developing it around 1968) made CAGE a four-question bedside screen. Each letter stands for one question: Cut down (ever felt you should cut down?), Annoyed (have people annoyed you by criticising your drinking?), Guilty (ever felt bad or guilty about drinking?), Eye-opener (ever had a drink first thing in the morning to steady nerves or get rid of a hangover?).

FieldDetail
Full nameCAGE Questionnaire (Cut down, Annoyed, Guilty, Eye-opener)
Author(s), yearJohn A. Ewing (1984; developed around 1968)
VersionsCAGE; CAGE-AID (adapted to include drugs)
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults
Items4 (yes/no)
Subtests / scales / indicesNone
Scoring and norms0 to 4; 2 or more is clinically significant
MeasuresLifetime alcohol problems (screen)
Indian adaptationUsed widely in Indian general hospital settings (uncertain standard)
Exam trap

CAGE = 4 items, cut-off 2, Ewing 1984. "E" is Eye-opener, the most specific item for dependence.

MAST: Michigan Alcoholism Screening Test

Melvin Selzer (1971, American Journal of Psychiatry) designed MAST as a structured screen for alcoholism, asking about lifetime drinking-related consequences (social, legal, occupational, medical). Items are weighted: some answers count more than others.

FieldDetail
Full nameMichigan Alcoholism Screening Test
Author(s), yearMelvin L. Selzer (1971)
VersionsMAST (25 items); Short MAST, SMAST (13 items, Selzer, Vinokur and van Rooijen 1975); Brief MAST (10 items, Pokorny et al. 1972); MAST-G geriatric
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults
Items25 (some sources list 24; 25 is the usual count)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total
Scoring and normsWeighted yes/no items (weights 0 to 5); common cut-off 5 or more indicates alcoholism, 4 suggestive (sources differ on bands)
MeasuresLifetime alcohol problems
Indian adaptationUncertain
Exam trap

MAST = Selzer 1971, 25 items, weighted. SMAST = 13. Not to be confused with SADQ (dependence severity) or CIWA-Ar (withdrawal).

CIWA-Ar: Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol, revised

CIWA-Ar is a clinician-rated scale used at the bedside to measure the severity of alcohol withdrawal and to guide symptom-triggered benzodiazepine dosing. It was shortened from the 15-item CIWA-A (Shaw et al., 1981) by John Sullivan, Karen Sykora, Joseph Schneiderman, Claudio Naranjo and Edward Sellers (1989, British Journal of Addiction) at the Addiction Research Foundation, Toronto. It takes about 5 minutes and is repeated every few hours.

FieldDetail
Full nameClinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol, revised
Author(s), yearJ. T. Sullivan, K. Sykora, J. Schneiderman, C. A. Naranjo and E. M. Sellers (1989); CIWA-A Shaw et al. (1981)
VersionsCIWA-Ar (1989); CIWA-A (1981); CIWA-B for benzodiazepine withdrawal is separate
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults
Items10
Subtests / scales / indicesNausea and vomiting; tremor; paroxysmal sweats; anxiety; agitation; tactile disturbances; auditory disturbances; visual disturbances; headache or fullness in head; orientation and clouding of sensorium
Scoring and normsNine items 0 to 7, orientation 0 to 4; total 0 to 67. Common bands (sources differ): below 8 to 10 minimal or mild, 8 to 15 moderate, above 15 severe (risk of complications)
MeasuresSeverity of alcohol withdrawal
Indian adaptationRoutinely used in Indian deaddiction units (no separate adaptation)
Exam trap

CIWA-Ar = 10 items, max 67 (not 70, because orientation tops at 4). It measures withdrawal, not dependence or screening.

SADQ: Severity of Alcohol Dependence Questionnaire

Tim Stockwell and colleagues (Stockwell, Hodgson, Edwards, Taylor and Rankin, 1979) at the Addiction Research Unit, Institute of Psychiatry, London, built the SADQ around Griffith Edwards and Milton Gross's (1976) alcohol dependence syndrome. The person describes a recent typical heavy-drinking period, and the scale rates how dependent they are, which helps decide whether medically supervised detoxification is needed.

FieldDetail
Full nameSeverity of Alcohol Dependence Questionnaire
Author(s), yearTim Stockwell, Ray Hodgson, Griffith Edwards, Colin Taylor and Howard Rankin (1979)
VersionsSADQ (1979); SADQ-C (community version, 1983)
PublisherPublic domain in practice
Age rangeAdults
Items20
Subtests / scales / indices5 sections of 4 items: physical withdrawal; affective withdrawal; relief drinking; frequency of alcohol consumption; speed of onset of withdrawal after abstinence
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 3; total 0 to 60. Below 16 mild, 16 to 30 moderate, 31 or more severe dependence
MeasuresSeverity of alcohol dependence
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian deaddiction research (uncertain)
Exam trap

SADQ = 20 items, 0 to 60, measures dependence severity (built on Edwards and Gross 1976).

FTND: Fagerström Test for Nicotine Dependence

Karl-Olov Fagerström (1978) published the 8-item Fagerström Tolerance Questionnaire (FTQ). Todd Heatherton, Lynn Kozlowski, Richard Frecker and Fagerström (1991) revised it into the 6-item FTND, which is psychometrically stronger. The single best item is time to first cigarette after waking. In 2012 Fagerström proposed renaming it the Fagerström Test for Cigarette Dependence (FTCD).

FieldDetail
Full nameFagerström Test for Nicotine Dependence
Author(s), yearKarl-Olov Fagerström (FTQ 1978); Heatherton, Kozlowski, Frecker and Fagerström (FTND 1991)
VersionsFTND (1991), renamed FTCD (2012); FTQ (1978); smokeless tobacco version (FTND-ST) exists
PublisherPublic domain
Age rangeAdults
Items6 (FTQ 8)
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total; Heaviness of Smoking Index (HSI) uses 2 items (time to first cigarette, cigarettes per day)
Scoring and normsTotal 0 to 10; higher = more dependent (bands vary, e.g. 6 or more high dependence)
MeasuresPhysical nicotine dependence
Indian adaptationSmokeless-tobacco adaptations used in India (uncertain)
Exam trap

FTQ 1978 = 8 items; FTND 1991 = 6 items, 0 to 10.

8.6 Global, functional and disability measures

CGI: Clinical Global Impression

The CGI is the simplest rating scale in psychiatry: the clinician, drawing on all their experience with the relevant patient population, gives a single global judgement. It was published by William Guy in the ECDEU Assessment Manual for Psychopharmacology (1976) for NIMH drug trials and is still used in nearly every trial alongside the detailed scales.

FieldDetail
Full nameClinical Global Impression scale
Author(s), yearWilliam Guy (1976, ECDEU Assessment Manual, US NIMH)
VersionsCGI; disorder-specific variants (e.g. CGI-BP for bipolar disorder)
PublisherUS Department of Health, Education and Welfare (public domain)
Age rangeAll ages
Items3 global items
Subtests / scales / indicesCGI-S (Severity of illness): 1 normal, not at all ill to 7 among the most extremely ill; CGI-I (Global Improvement): 1 very much improved to 7 very much worse, 4 no change; Efficacy Index (therapeutic effect versus side effects, 4 x 4 grid)
Scoring and normsCGI-S and CGI-I on 1 to 7 (0 = not assessed)
MeasuresOverall severity, change, and benefit-risk of treatment
Indian adaptationUsed unchanged
Exam trap

CGI-I is rated relative to baseline, CGI-S relative to other patients with the disorder. Both are 1 to 7; a CGI-I of 1 or 2 is the usual definition of "responder".

GAF: Global Assessment of Functioning (and WHODAS 2.0)

The GAF is a single 0 to 100 style rating of a person's overall psychological, social and occupational functioning, with descriptive anchors every 10 points (for example 91 to 100 superior functioning, 41 to 50 serious symptoms such as suicidal ideation or serious impairment, 1 to 10 persistent danger of hurting self or others). It grew out of Lester Luborsky's Health-Sickness Rating Scale (1962) and the Global Assessment Scale (GAS; Jean Endicott, Robert Spitzer, Joseph Fleiss and Jacob Cohen, 1976), and served as Axis V of the multiaxial system in DSM-III-R (1987) and DSM-IV (1994). DSM-5 (2013) dropped the multiaxial system and the GAF because it mixed symptoms with disability and had poor reliability, and recommended the WHO Disability Assessment Schedule 2.0 (WHODAS 2.0) instead.

FieldDetail
Full nameGlobal Assessment of Functioning; WHO Disability Assessment Schedule 2.0
Author(s), yearGAF: American Psychiatric Association (DSM-III-R 1987, DSM-IV 1994), from the GAS (Endicott et al. 1976); WHODAS 2.0: World Health Organization (Üstün et al., 2010)
VersionsGAF (DSM-IV-TR 2000 last); WHODAS 2.0 36-item and 12-item forms (plus 12+24 version), interviewer, self and proxy formats
PublisherAPA (GAF); WHO (WHODAS 2.0, free)
Age rangeAdults (C-GAS for children, Shaffer et al. 1983)
ItemsGAF: 1 rating; WHODAS 2.0: 36 or 12
Subtests / scales / indicesWHODAS 2.0 six domains: cognition (understanding and communicating), mobility, self-care, getting along (with people), life activities (household, work, school), participation in society
Scoring and normsGAF 1 to 100 in 10-point bands, with 0 meaning inadequate information (so it is often written "0 to 100"); WHODAS 2.0 item 1 (none) to 5 (extreme), converted to 0 to 100 summary
MeasuresGlobal functioning (GAF); disability across life domains (WHODAS)
Indian adaptationWHODAS translations used in Indian research; for official Indian disability certification IDEAS is used instead
Exam trap

GAF was removed in DSM-5; WHODAS 2.0 (36 or 12 items, 6 domains) replaces it in DSM-5 Section III. GAF was Axis V, not Axis IV (Axis IV = psychosocial and environmental problems).

IDEAS: Indian Disability Evaluation and Assessment Scale

IDEAS was developed by the Rehabilitation Committee of the Indian Psychiatric Society (2002) so that people with mental illness could be certified for disability benefits under the Persons with Disabilities Act 1995, and the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment adopted it (2002). The psychiatrist or rehabilitation professional rates disability (not symptoms) in four everyday domains, based on interview with the patient and family, and adds a score for duration of illness. Under the RPwD Act 2016 guidelines it remains the basis of mental illness disability certification (with later modifications, uncertain in detail).

FieldDetail
Full nameIndian Disability Evaluation and Assessment Scale
Author(s), yearRehabilitation Committee of the Indian Psychiatric Society (2002)
VersionsIDEAS (2002); modified guidelines under RPwD Act 2016 (details uncertain)
PublisherIndian Psychiatric Society; notified by Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment
Age rangeAdults (commonly stated; illness should usually be of at least 2 years' duration for certification)
Items4 domain ratings plus a duration of illness score
Subtests / scales / indicesSelf-care; interpersonal activities (social relationships); communication and understanding; work (employment, housework or education)
Scoring and normsEach domain 0 (no disability) to 4 (profound); total domain score plus duration of illness score (1 to 4) gives the Global Disability Score; commonly cited bands: 0 no disability, 1 to 6 mild (below 40 percent), 7 to 13 moderate (40 to 70 percent), 14 to 19 severe (71 to 99 percent), 20 profound (100 percent). Exact bands: verify against the current notification
MeasuresDisability due to mental illness (for certification)
Indian adaptationIt is itself the Indian instrument
Exam trap

IDEAS = Indian Psychiatric Society, 2002, 4 domains, used for mental illness disability certification. ISAA (NIMH Secunderabad) is for autism; intellectual disability certification uses IQ / adaptive tests. Mixing up IDEAS and ISAA is the commonest Indian trap here.

DAQ: Dysfunction Analysis Questionnaire

The DAQ is an Indian self-report measure of everyday dysfunction (how much the person's functioning in major life areas is disturbed), developed at PGIMER, Chandigarh. Sources commonly credit Dwarka Pershad, S. K. Verma and A. Malhotra; the exact author list and year are uncertain (1985 is commonly cited). It is used in Indian clinical research to measure functional change with treatment, complementing symptom scales.

FieldDetail
Full nameDysfunction Analysis Questionnaire
Author(s), yearDwarka Pershad, S. K. Verma and A. Malhotra, PGIMER Chandigarh (year uncertain; 1985 commonly cited)
VersionsSingle version (uncertain)
PublisherIndian publisher, distributed through PGIMER / National Psychological Corporation (uncertain)
Age rangeAdults (uncertain)
ItemsCommonly cited as 50 (10 per area) (uncertain)
Subtests / scales / indicesCommonly cited 5 areas: social, vocational, personal, familial, cognitive (uncertain)
Scoring and norms5-point ratings converted to area and total dysfunction scores (uncertain)
MeasuresDysfunction in major life areas
Indian adaptationIndian original
Exam trap

DAQ is a PGIMER Chandigarh instrument (Pershad and Verma's group), not NIMHANS. If a question asks for its item count, 50 items across 5 areas is the usual textbook answer, but treat it as less certain than the international scales.

8.7 Perinatal and child scales

EPDS: Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale

John Cox, Jenifer Holden and Ruth Sagovsky (1987, British Journal of Psychiatry) built the EPDS in Edinburgh and Livingston because standard depression scales over-count fatigue, sleep and appetite changes that are normal after childbirth. The EPDS therefore deliberately omits somatic items. The mother rates how she has felt in the past 7 days. It is used in pregnancy as well as postpartum, and in fathers.

FieldDetail
Full nameEdinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale
Author(s), yearJohn L. Cox, Jenifer M. Holden and Ruth Sagovsky (1987)
VersionsEPDS; EPDS-3A anxiety subscale (items 3, 4, 5)
PublisherFree to use with acknowledgement (Royal College of Psychiatrists copyright)
Age rangePregnant and postnatal women (also used in men)
Items10
Subtests / scales / indicesSingle total; item 10 (thoughts of self-harm) requires immediate follow-up if positive
Scoring and normsEach item 0 to 3 (some reverse-scored); total 0 to 30. Original cut-off 12/13 for probable depression; 9/10 for possible depression in screening (sources differ)
MeasuresPerinatal depression (screen)
Indian adaptationValidated in several Indian languages, including Tamil, Kannada and Hindi studies (uncertain which is standard)
Exam trap

EPDS = 10 items, 0 to 30, past 7 days, no somatic items, Cox, Holden and Sagovsky 1987.

Conners Rating Scales

C. Keith Conners (1969) created teacher and parent rating scales to measure behaviour change in children on stimulant medication. Over five decades they became the best-known ADHD rating scales, with parent, teacher and self-report forms giving T scores for inattention, hyperactivity-impulsivity, and related problems (oppositional, conduct, learning, executive functioning). Multi-informant ratings matter because DSM ADHD requires symptoms in two or more settings.

FieldDetail
Full nameConners Rating Scales (Conners 4, Conners 3, Conners' Rating Scales-Revised)
Author(s), yearC. Keith Conners (teacher scale 1969, parent scale 1970); CRS-R (1997); Conners 3 (2008); Conners 4 (2022, published posthumously by MHS; Conners died in 2017)
VersionsConners 4 (2022, current); Conners 3 (2008); Conners' Rating Scales-Revised (1997); original CTRS and CPRS (1969, 1970) and the 10-item Abbreviated Symptom Questionnaire; Conners Early Childhood (2009) for 2 to 6 years; Conners CBRS (2008)
PublisherMulti-Health Systems (MHS)
Age rangeConners 3 and 4: parent and teacher forms 6 to 18 years; self-report 8 to 18
ItemsVaries by form and edition: original teacher scale 39 items, parent 93 items; long and short forms in later editions (exact counts for Conners 3 and 4 forms: uncertain, check manual)
Subtests / scales / indicesConners 3 content scales: inattention, hyperactivity/impulsivity, learning problems, executive functioning, aggression, peer relations, family relations; DSM symptom scales (ADHD types, ODD, CD); validity scales; Conners 3 Global Index. Conners 4 adds a focus on impairment and a critical items section (details uncertain)
Scoring and normsT scores mean 50 SD 10; T of 60 or above elevated, 70 or above very elevated (Conners 3 convention)
MeasuresADHD and associated behaviour problems
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian child clinics; Indian norms not standard (uncertain)
Exam trap

Conners = ADHD; editions 1969 / 1997 / 2008 / 2022; published by MHS. Do not confuse with the Conners Continuous Performance Test (CPT), a computerised attention task by the same author.

CBCL: Child Behavior Checklist (ASEBA)

Thomas Achenbach (University of Vermont) began in the 1960s factor-analysing children's problem behaviours and found two broad groupings: internalizing (anxious, withdrawn, somatic: problems directed inward) and externalizing (aggressive, rule-breaking: problems directed outward). The CBCL, completed by a parent, became the core of the Achenbach System of Empirically Based Assessment (ASEBA), which also includes a Teacher's Report Form (TRF) and a Youth Self-Report (YSR, 11 to 18). Each problem item is rated 0 (not true), 1 (somewhat or sometimes true) or 2 (very true or often true) for the past 6 months.

FieldDetail
Full nameChild Behavior Checklist
Author(s), yearThomas M. Achenbach; CBCL manual with Craig Edelbrock (1983); 1991 profiles; current school-age forms Achenbach and Leslie Rescorla (2001); preschool forms Achenbach and Rescorla (2000)
VersionsCBCL/6-18 (2001); CBCL/1.5-5 (2000); earlier CBCL/4-18 (1991) and CBCL/2-3 (1992)
PublisherASEBA, Research Center for Children, Youth and Families, University of Vermont
Age range1.5 to 5 years and 6 to 18 years
ItemsCBCL/6-18: 113 numbered problem items (item 56 has sub-parts a to h, so some sources count about 118 to 120), plus competence items; CBCL/1.5-5: 99 problem items plus an open item (sources say 99 or 100)
Subtests / scales / indicesCBCL/6-18 syndrome scales (8): anxious/depressed, withdrawn/depressed, somatic complaints, social problems, thought problems, attention problems, rule-breaking behaviour, aggressive behaviour. Broadband: Internalizing, Externalizing, Total Problems. DSM-oriented scales (6). Competence scales: activities, social, school
Scoring and normsT scores (mean 50 SD 10). Syndrome scales: 65 to 69 borderline, 70 and above clinical; Internalizing, Externalizing and Total: 60 to 63 borderline, above 63 clinical
MeasuresBroad emotional and behavioural problems
Indian adaptationThe Indian CPMS (below) was adapted from the CBCL; CBCL itself has been translated and used in Indian studies
Exam trap

CBCL = Achenbach, internalizing vs externalizing, rated 0-1-2 over 6 months. TRF = teacher, YSR = youth (11 to 18). Its Indian derivative is CPMS (Malhotra).

CPMS: Childhood Psychopathology Measurement Schedule

Savita Malhotra and colleagues at PGIMER, Chandigarh adapted the CBCL for Indian children, producing the CPMS (commonly cited as Malhotra, Varma, Verma and Malhotra, 1988; year uncertain). Items are answered yes/no by a parent or informant, and factor analysis yielded eight Indian-relevant factors. It is the standard Indian answer to "a screening measure of childhood psychopathology".

Subtests / scales / indices8 factors (commonly cited): low intelligence with behaviour problems, conduct disorder, anxiety, depression, psychotic symptoms, special symptoms, physical illness with emotional problems, somatization (names vary slightly by source)

CPMS = Malhotra, PGIMER, 75 items, adapted from CBCL. Do not attribute it to NIMHANS.

9. Structured diagnostic interviews and the mental status examination

In the 1960s and 1970s studies such as the US-UK Diagnostic Project (1972) showed that psychiatrists in New York and London gave different diagnoses to the same patients, mostly because they asked different questions and used different definitions (information variance and criterion variance). Two fixes followed. Operational criteria (Feighner criteria 1972, Research Diagnostic Criteria 1978, then DSM-III 1980) fixed the definitions. Structured interviews fixed the questions, so that every patient is asked the same things in the same order, and the answers are scored against the criteria.

Interviews sit on a continuum. A fully structured interview (DIS, CIDI, DISC) has fixed wording and fixed coding, so it can be given by trained lay interviewers in large epidemiological surveys, but it cannot use clinical judgement. A semi-structured interview (SCID, SCAN, K-SADS, IPDE) gives required questions but lets the clinician probe, rephrase and judge whether a symptom is really present, so it needs clinically trained interviewers. A brief structured interview (MINI) trades depth for speed. The mental status examination, at the end of this section, is not a test at all: it is the clinician's systematic, here-and-now observation of the patient, and every clinical psychology viva expects you to know its headings cold.

SCID-5: Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-5

The SCID is a semi-structured interview, administered by a clinician, that walks through the DSM criteria module by module (mood episodes, psychotic symptoms, substance use, anxiety, OCD, trauma, and so on), with suggested questions on the left and the DSM criterion beside them. The interviewer rates each criterion as absent, subthreshold or present, using judgement. The SCID-5 comes in several versions for different purposes, which is a favourite exam question.

FieldDetail
Full nameStructured Clinical Interview for DSM-5 Disorders
Author(s), yearMichael B. First, Janet B. W. Williams, Rhonda S. Karg and Robert L. Spitzer (2015 research version; clinician version published 2016); SCID-5-PD First, Williams, Benjamin and Spitzer (2016); SCID-5-AMPD First, Skodol, Bender and Oldham (2018)
VersionsSCID-5-RV (Research Version, most comprehensive, with subtypes and specifiers); SCID-5-CV (Clinician Version, streamlined for clinical settings); SCID-5-CT (Clinical Trials Version, adapted for inclusion/exclusion); SCID-5-PD (personality disorders, with the self-report screening questionnaire SCID-5-SPQ); SCID-5-AMPD (Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders, three modules). History: original SCID (Spitzer and Williams, 1983 onwards, for DSM-III and DSM-III-R, 1987/1990); SCID-I for DSM-IV Axis I and SCID-II for DSM-IV Axis II personality disorders (First, Spitzer, Gibbon and Williams, 1996/1997); SCID-I/P (patient), SCID-I/NP (non-patient) editions
PublisherAmerican Psychiatric Association Publishing
Age rangeAdults 18 and above (usable with older adolescents with minor wording changes)
ItemsModule-based; not normally described by a single item count
Subtests / scales / indicesSCID-5-CV modules (commonly listed): A mood episodes, B psychotic symptoms, C differential diagnosis of psychotic disorders, D differential diagnosis of mood disorders, E substance use, F anxiety, G OCD and related, H sleep-wake (trimmed), I feeding and eating, J externalizing, K trauma and stressor-related (letters differ between RV and CV)
Scoring and normsEach criterion coded (for example ? inadequate information, 1 absent, 2 subthreshold, 3 threshold); diagnosis by algorithm and clinical judgement
MeasuresDSM-5 diagnoses (current and lifetime)
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian research in English and translation (uncertain standard adaptation)
Exam trap

SCID is semi-structured and needs a clinician. In the DSM-IV era SCID-I covered Axis I clinical disorders and SCID-II covered Axis II personality disorders; in DSM-5 there are no axes, so SCID-II became SCID-5-PD. RV = research, CV = clinician, CT = clinical trials.

MINI: Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview

David Sheehan (University of South Florida) and Yves Lecrubier (Paris) designed the MINI as a short structured interview covering the main DSM and ICD diagnoses, for situations where the SCID or CIDI would take too long (primary care, multicentre trials). Each module opens with screening questions; if they are negative, the module is skipped. It takes about 15 minutes.

FieldDetail
Full nameMini International Neuropsychiatric Interview
Author(s), yearDavid V. Sheehan, Yves Lecrubier and colleagues (1997/1998, Journal of Clinical Psychiatry 1998)
VersionsMINI 7.0 for DSM-5 (2014 onwards); earlier versions for DSM-IV and ICD-10 (5.0, 6.0); MINI-Plus (longer); MINI-KID (children and adolescents); MINI-Screen
PublisherMedical Outcome Systems / Harm Research Institute (licensing; uncertain current publisher)
Age rangeAdults (MINI-KID 6 to 17)
ItemsModule-based yes/no questions; DSM-IV version covered about 16 to 17 disorders (sources differ)
Subtests / scales / indicesModules for major depressive episode, suicidality, bipolar, panic, agoraphobia, social anxiety, OCD, PTSD, alcohol and substance use, psychotic disorders, anorexia, bulimia, GAD, antisocial personality disorder (varies by version)
Scoring and normsYes/no answers; diagnostic boxes
MeasuresCurrent (and some lifetime) DSM/ICD diagnoses
Indian adaptationHindi and other translations used in Indian research (uncertain)
Exam trap

MINI = Sheehan and Lecrubier, about 15 minutes, structured, compatible with both DSM and ICD. Do not confuse with the Mini-Mental State Examination (Folstein 1975), a cognitive screen.

CIDI: Composite International Diagnostic Interview

The CIDI was developed by the World Health Organization with the US Alcohol, Drug Abuse and Mental Health Administration (Lee Robins and colleagues, 1988) by combining the Diagnostic Interview Schedule (DIS) with items from the Present State Examination (PSE). It is fully structured, designed for lay interviewers, and gives both ICD and DSM diagnoses. Ronald Kessler and Bedirhan Üstün (2004) produced the WHO World Mental Health version (WMH-CIDI, CIDI 3.0), used in the World Mental Health Surveys, which included India.

FieldDetail
Full nameComposite International Diagnostic Interview
Author(s), yearWHO and US ADAMHA, Lee N. Robins et al. (1988); WMH-CIDI Ronald C. Kessler and T. Bedirhan Üstün (2004)
VersionsCIDI 3.0 (WMH-CIDI, computer-assisted CAPI); CIDI 2.1; CIDI 1.0; CIDI-SF (short form)
PublisherWHO
Age rangeAdults (adolescent version CIDI-A exists)
ItemsModule-based (uncertain single count)
Subtests / scales / indicesDiagnostic sections for mood, anxiety, substance, impulse control and other disorders, plus services and functioning sections
Scoring and normsComputer algorithms for ICD-10 and DSM-IV diagnoses
MeasuresPrevalence of mental disorders in epidemiological surveys
Indian adaptationUsed in Indian sites of WHO World Mental Health Surveys (uncertain details)
Exam trap

CIDI is fully structured, lay-administered, WHO, built from DIS + PSE. SCAN is the WHO clinician-administered counterpart.

SCAN and PSE: Schedules for Clinical Assessment in Neuropsychiatry and the Present State Examination

John Wing at the Institute of Psychiatry, London developed the Present State Examination from the late 1950s as a semi-structured clinical interview about the patient's mental state over the past month. The ninth edition (PSE-9; Wing, Cooper and Sartorius, 1974, "The Measurement and Classification of Psychiatric Symptoms") has 140 items and was used, with the CATEGO computer program that turns ratings into diagnostic classes, in the US-UK Diagnostic Project and the WHO International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia (IPSS, 1973), which included Agra, India. PSE-10 was absorbed into SCAN, a WHO instrument (about 1992 to 1994) that adds a glossary, an Item Group Checklist and a Clinical History Schedule.

FieldDetail
Full namePresent State Examination; Schedules for Clinical Assessment in Neuropsychiatry
Author(s), yearJohn K. Wing; PSE-9 Wing, John Cooper and Norman Sartorius (1974); SCAN WHO (Wing et al., 1990 to 1994)

Exam trap

PSE = John Wing; PSE-9 = 140 items, 1974; computer program = CATEGO. SCAN is semi-structured and clinician-administered (unlike CIDI).

DIS: Diagnostic Interview Schedule

Lee Robins and colleagues (Robins, Helzer, Croughan and Ratcliff, 1981, Archives of General Psychiatry) wrote the DIS for the US National Institute of Mental Health Epidemiologic Catchment Area (ECA) study, which needed thousands of interviews done by lay interviewers. Every question and probe is fully scripted (the "probe flow chart" checks whether a symptom was clinically significant and not due to medication, drugs or physical illness), and a computer generates DSM-III diagnoses.

PublisherWashington University, St Louis (uncertain)

Subtests / scales / indicesDiagnostic sections by disorder
Scoring and normsComputer algorithm diagnoses
MeasuresLifetime and current DSM diagnoses in community surveys
Indian adaptationUncertain
Exam trap

DIS = Robins 1981, ECA study, lay interviewers, fully structured. It is the parent of the CIDI.

K-SADS: Schedule for Affective Disorders and Schizophrenia for School-Age Children

The adult SADS (Jean Endicott and Robert Spitzer, 1978) was adapted for children by Joaquim Puig-Antich and William Chambers (1978) as the Kiddie-SADS. It is a semi-structured interview in which the clinician interviews the parent and the child separately, then combines the information into summary ratings using clinical judgement. The most widely used version is the K-SADS-PL (Present and Lifetime), by Joan Kaufman, Boris Birmaher, David Brent and colleagues (1997), with a screen interview followed by supplements for positive areas. A DSM-5 version (K-SADS-PL DSM-5) appeared in 2016.

Exam trap

K-SADS = semi-structured, parent and child separately, Puig-Antich and Chambers 1978; PL = Present and Lifetime (Kaufman 1997). DISC is the fully structured, lay-administered child interview.

DISC: Diagnostic Interview Schedule for Children

The DISC is the child counterpart of the DIS: a fully structured interview for NIMH child epidemiological research, usable by lay interviewers, with parallel parent (DISC-P) and youth (DISC-Y) versions. The first version was developed by Anthony Costello and colleagues around 1982 to 1984; the widely cited DISC-IV is by David Shaffer, Prudence Fisher, Christopher Lucas, Mina Dulcan and Mary Schwab-Stone (2000).

Exam trap

DISC = fully structured, lay, child version of DIS. Do not mix up with K-SADS (semi-structured, clinician).

IPDE: International Personality Disorder Examination

Armand Loranger developed the Personality Disorder Examination (PDE, 1988) and then, in a WHO and US ADAMHA joint programme, the IPDE (Loranger et al., 1994, Archives of General Psychiatry), a semi-structured clinical interview that assesses personality disorders according to both ICD-10 and DSM-IV. Questions are organised by life area (work, self, interpersonal relationships, affects, reality testing, impulse control) rather than by disorder, to make the interview feel natural. A trait counts only if it is enduring: commonly described as present for at least 5 years, with at least one criterion present before age 25. A self-administered screening questionnaire identifies people who need the full interview.

Scoring and normsEach criterion 0 (absent or normal), 1 (exaggerated or accentuated), 2 (criterion level or pathological)
MeasuresPersonality disorders (ICD-10 and DSM-IV)
Indian adaptationThe IPDE field trial included an Indian centre (Bangalore, reported; uncertain); used in Indian research
Exam trap

IPDE = Loranger, WHO, semi-structured, covers both ICD-10 and DSM-IV personality disorders. SCID-II / SCID-5-PD is DSM-only.
