The Mental Status Examination (MSE)

The MSE is the psychological equivalent of the physical examination. History tells you what has happened over months and years; the MSE records what the clinician observes and elicits now, during the interview. It is cross-sectional (one point in time), and it is written descriptively, in the patient's own words where possible, rather than with labels alone ("he said, 'my neighbours are poisoning my food'" is better than "persecutory delusions" by itself). Indian teaching (following Fish's Clinical Psychopathology and Indian textbooks such as Niraj Ahuja's) usually uses the order below. Learn the headings, then what goes under each.

Memory hook

"All Patients Really Should Make Their Psychiatrist Carefully Inquire": Appearance and behaviour, Psychomotor activity, Rapport, Speech, Mood and affect, Thought, Perception, Cognition, Insight (with judgement under cognition).

1. General appearance and behaviour

What a stranger would notice: apparent age versus stated age, build, grooming and hygiene, dress (appropriate to weather and setting, or bizarre, or provocative), facial expression, eye contact, posture, gait, any physical abnormalities, tics, tremors or mannerisms. Behaviour includes attitude towards the examiner (cooperative, guarded, hostile, seductive, evasive, apathetic) and any abnormal behaviour during the interview (hallucinatory behaviour such as muttering or looking at empty corners, social withdrawal, disinhibition). Examples: unkempt and dishevelled in severe depression, schizophrenia or dementia; flamboyant, colourful, over-decorated in mania.

2. Psychomotor activity

The amount and speed of movement. Increased: restlessness, pacing, hand-wringing (agitation, typical of agitated depression or anxiety), overactivity (mania), akathisia (drug-induced restlessness). Decreased: psychomotor retardation (slow movements, long pauses; depression), stupor (no movement or speech despite clear consciousness). Catatonic signs belong here: waxy flexibility (limbs stay where the examiner places them), posturing, negativism, echopraxia (copying movements), automatic obedience, mannerisms (purposeful-looking but odd movements) and stereotypies (repetitive, non-goal-directed movements).

3. Rapport

Whether a working emotional connection can be established: "rapport established with ease", "established with difficulty", or "could not be established". Rapport is often hard with paranoid, negativistic or severely withdrawn patients; a strikingly "glass wall" or "praecox feeling" (the examiner's sense of being unable to reach the patient, described in schizophrenia by H. C. Rümke, 1941) was classically noted. Eye contact (maintained, avoided, staring) is usually recorded here or under appearance.

4. Speech

Speech is described as an act, separately from the thought it carries. Record: rate (rapid, slow), volume (loud, soft), tone and prosody (monotonous or modulated), quantity (talkative, or poverty of speech with brief answers), reaction time (latency before answering, prolonged in depression), spontaneity (speaks spontaneously or only in reply), pressure (rapid, hard to interrupt: mania), relevance and coherence (answers to the point or not; understandable or not), and abnormalities such as stuttering, dysarthria (articulation problem, often neurological), aphasia, mutism, neologisms, echolalia (repeating the examiner's words) and clang associations (words linked by sound).

5. Mood and affect

This is the most tested distinction in the MSE. Mood is the patient's pervasive and sustained emotional state, reported by the patient (subjective): "How have you been feeling in your spirits?" "Low, doctor, very low." It is recorded in the patient's own words, often alongside the clinician's description (Indian case formats write "subjective" and "objective" mood). Affect is the observable, moment-to-moment expression of emotion, seen by the examiner in face, voice and gesture. The classic analogy: mood is the climate, affect is the weather.

Affect is described along several dimensions:

  • Quality: what emotion is shown (sad, anxious, irritable, euphoric, perplexed).
  • Range: how many different emotions are shown during the interview. Full (normal), restricted or constricted (reduced range), blunted (severe reduction in intensity), flat (virtually no expression).
  • Reactivity: whether affect shifts appropriately with the topic or with the examiner's humour (reduced in melancholic depression).
  • Appropriateness (congruence): whether affect matches the content of thought. Inappropriate or incongruent affect, for example laughing while describing a parent's death, is classically seen in schizophrenia. A second, separate question is whether mood is congruent with the delusions (mood-congruent versus mood-incongruent psychotic features).
  • Stability: stable versus labile (rapid, unprovoked shifts, as in mania, organic states, pseudobulbar affect).
  • Intensity and communicability (whether the examiner can empathise with it).
Definition

Other mood terms: euthymia (normal mood), dysphoria (unpleasant mood), euphoria (exaggerated well-being), elation (euphoria with increased motor activity), exaltation (intense elation with grandiosity), ecstasy (intense rapture), alexithymia (difficulty identifying and describing one's emotions, Peter Sifneos 1973), anhedonia (loss of pleasure).

6. Thought

Indian MSE formats divide thought into four parts:

  • Stream (tempo and continuity): how fast and how smoothly thoughts flow. Flight of ideas (rapid thoughts with abrupt shifts, but connections understandable: mania), pressure of thought, retardation or slowing (depression), thought block (sudden stopping of thought mid-sentence, the patient cannot explain why: schizophrenia), perseveration (persistent repetition of the same response; organic conditions), circumstantiality (delay in reaching the goal through excessive irrelevant detail, but the goal is reached) and tangentiality (the goal is never reached) are sometimes placed here and sometimes under form.
  • Form: the organisation and logical connection of thoughts, i.e. formal thought disorder. Loosening of associations (Eugen Bleuler, 1911: ideas shift between unrelated topics), derailment, knight's move thinking, word salad (incoherent mixture of words), neologisms (new words), overinclusion (Norman Cameron, 1938: inability to keep conceptual boundaries), concrete thinking, illogicality, and Schneider's descriptions (derailment, substitution, omission, fusion, drivelling).
  • Content: what the patient thinks about. Delusions (fixed, false, unshakeable beliefs out of keeping with sociocultural background: persecutory, reference, grandiose, guilt, nihilistic, jealousy, erotomanic, somatic, infidelity), overvalued ideas (Carl Wernicke, 1900: strongly held but understandable beliefs that dominate life, e.g. in anorexia), obsessions, preoccupations, phobias, hypochondriacal ideas, and always suicidal and homicidal ideation, which must be asked about directly.
  • Possession: whether the patient experiences thoughts as their own. In obsessions, thoughts are recognised as one's own though unwanted (ego-dystonic). In passivity of thought, a Schneiderian first-rank symptom, ownership is lost: thought insertion (thoughts put in by an outside agency), thought withdrawal (thoughts taken out), thought broadcasting (thoughts spread so others know them).

7. Perception

Abnormal perceptual experiences, with the modality always specified. Hallucinations are perceptions without an external stimulus, experienced as real and in external space: auditory (most common in schizophrenia; second-person commenting/commanding, or third-person voices discussing the patient, a first-rank symptom, and audible thoughts, Gedankenlautwerden), visual (common in organic and delirious states), olfactory and gustatory (temporal lobe lesions, also depression), tactile (formication in cocaine and alcohol withdrawal), somatic and kinaesthetic. Illusions are misperceptions of a real stimulus. Pseudohallucinations are perceived in inner subjective space and recognised as not real (definitions differ between Karl Jaspers' and later usage). Hypnagogic (falling asleep) and hypnopompic (waking) hallucinations can be normal. Also record depersonalisation (feeling oneself unreal or detached) and derealisation (the surroundings feel unreal).

8. Cognitive functions (higher mental functions)

Cognition is tested by direct bedside tasks. Formal tests such as the MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) or the Hindi Mental State Examination (HMSE, Ganguli et al., 1995) can supplement it.

  • Consciousness: alert, drowsy, clouded, stuporose or comatose (often recorded first).
  • Orientation: to time (day, date, month, year, time of day), place and person. Orientation to time is lost first in organic states and orientation to person last; disorientation strongly suggests an organic condition such as delirium or dementia.
  • Attention and concentration: attention is the ability to focus on a task; concentration is the ability to sustain that focus. Tests: serial 7s (subtract 7 from 100 repeatedly: 93, 86, 79, 72, 65; record errors and time taken), serial 3s from 20 if 7s are too hard, months of the year or days of the week backwards, and digit span (forward digit span of about 7 plus or minus 2 in normal adults; backward span is usually one or two fewer, with sources giving slightly different normal values). Digit span is strictly a test of attention and immediate registration rather than memory.
  • Memory: immediate (registration and recall within seconds, e.g. repeating digits or three words straight away), recent (events of the past hours or days: what did you eat this morning, how did you come here; plus recall of the three words after about 5 minutes, which tests recent memory/new learning), recent past (past few months: news events) and remote (years ago: date of marriage, schooling, verified with relatives). Remote memory is typically preserved longest in dementia (Ribot's law, Théodule Ribot, 1881: recent memories are lost before older ones).
  • General information and intelligence: fund of knowledge appropriate to education and culture (name of the Prime Minister, capital cities, recent major events), simple arithmetic, and a clinical estimate of whether intelligence is average, above or below. The questions must be adjusted for the patient's education and background.
  • Abstraction (abstract thinking): the ability to move from concrete instances to general concepts. Proverb interpretation ("What does 'a rolling stone gathers no moss' mean?") and similarities ("How are an apple and a banana alike?" Abstract answer: both are fruits; concrete answer: both are round, or "you can eat them"). Concrete thinking appears in schizophrenia, organic disorders and intellectual disability; proverb responses are strongly influenced by culture and education, so interpret with caution.
  • Judgement: the ability to assess a situation and act appropriately. Personal judgement (realistic plans for one's own future, e.g. what will you do after discharge), social judgement (behaviour in social situations, observed during the interview and from history) and test judgement (responses to hypothetical situations: "What would you do if you found a stamped, addressed envelope on the road?" or "if you saw a fire in a cinema hall?").

9. Insight

Insight is the patient's awareness and understanding of their own illness: whether they recognise that they are ill, that the illness is psychological, and that they need treatment. A widely taught six-point grading appears in Kaplan and Sadock's Synopsis of Psychiatry and is reproduced in many Indian textbooks and case formats. It is a textbook teaching convention rather than a validated scale, and wording varies slightly between editions and sources:

  1. Complete denial of illness.
  2. Slight awareness of being sick and needing help, but denying it at the same time.
  3. Awareness of being sick but blaming it on others, on external factors or on organic (physical) factors.
  4. Awareness that illness is caused by something unknown in oneself.
  5. Intellectual insight: admission that one is ill and that symptoms or failures in adjustment are due to one's own irrational feelings or disturbances, but without applying this knowledge to future experiences.
  6. True emotional insight: emotional awareness of one's own motives and feelings and those of important people in one's life, which can lead to basic changes in behaviour.

Structured measures of insight also exist, for example the Schedule for Assessment of Insight (Anthony David, 1990), which rates three components: recognition of illness, relabelling of unusual experiences as pathological, and treatment adherence; and the Scale to Assess Unawareness of Mental Disorder (SUMD; Xavier Amador et al., 1993).

Exam trap

Intellectual insight is grade 5, not the highest: the patient "knows" but does not change behaviour. Only true emotional insight (grade 6) produces change. Grade 3 (aware but blames external or organic causes) is a common choice for patients with somatisation or alcohol use. If a question cites the six grades, it is following Kaplan and Sadock; do not attribute the grading to Karl Jaspers or Fish.

9.2 MSE summary and traps

MSE headingWhat is recordedKey examples / tests
Appearance and behaviourGrooming, dress, attitude, eye contact, abnormal behaviourUnkempt in depression; colourful dress in mania; hallucinatory behaviour
Psychomotor activityAmount and speed of movementAgitation, retardation, catatonic signs (waxy flexibility, posturing)
RapportEase of establishing contactDifficult in paranoid, withdrawn patients
SpeechRate, volume, tone, quantity, reaction time, coherencePressure of speech (mania), poverty of speech, mutism
MoodSubjective, sustained emotional stateRecorded in patient's own words
AffectObserved, moment-to-moment expressionRange, reactivity, appropriateness, stability (labile), intensity
ThoughtStream, form, content, possessionFlight of ideas, thought block, loosening, delusions, thought insertion
PerceptionHallucinations, illusions, depersonalisationThird-person auditory hallucinations (first-rank)
CognitionConsciousness, orientation, attention, memory, information, abstraction, judgementSerial 7s, digit span, 3-word recall, proverbs, similarities, envelope test
InsightAwareness of illness and need for treatmentSix grades (Kaplan and Sadock convention)
Exam trap

Mood is subjective and sustained; affect is objective and momentary. "Patient says he feels fine but looks tearful" = euthymic reported mood with a sad (incongruent) affect. Blunted and flat affect describe range and intensity; inappropriate affect describes congruence; labile affect describes stability. Thought block is a disorder of stream, thought insertion a disorder of possession, a delusion a disorder of content, loosening of associations a disorder of form. Serial 7s test attention and concentration, not memory; proverbs and similarities test abstraction; the envelope question tests test judgement.

High yield

Section 8 and 9 quick recall: HAM-D is clinician-rated, BDI is self-report. PANSS 30, BPRS 18, Y-BOCS 10 core, YMRS 11, CARS 15 with cut-off 30, ISAA 40 with cut-off 70. GHQ-12 is the screening default. SCL-90-R has 9 dimensions and 3 global indices. GAF was removed in DSM-5 in favour of WHODAS 2.0. Fully structured lay interviews: DIS, CIDI, DISC. Semi-structured clinician interviews: SCID, SCAN/PSE, K-SADS, IPDE. MINI takes about 15 minutes.
